4. Run sqlmap with detection-only defaults:

   ```bash
   sqlmap \
     -u "$TARGET_URL" \
     ${METHOD:+--method="$METHOD"} \
     ${DATA:+--data="$DATA"} \
     ${COOKIE:+--cookie="$COOKIE"} \
     --batch \
     --level=3 \
     --risk=2 \
     --technique=BEUST \
     --smart \
     --threads=2 \
     --output-dir="./findings/sqli-$SLUG/sqlmap" \
     --flush-session \
     --answers="follow=N,quit=N"

    Post-process the sqlmap log:
        Extract the vulnerable parameter and DBMS.
        Extract the technique that produced the positive signal (boolean-based, time-based, error-based, UNION, stacked).
        Save the raw response pair (baseline vs. injected) to evidence/.
    Write finding.yml summarizing the injection.

Safety rails

    --dump, --os-shell, --os-pwn, --sql-shell, --file-read, and --file-write are refused unless the caller passes allow_destructive=true AND the engagement scope file contains techniques.data_extraction: allowed.
    --random-agent is enabled by default so runs are attributable to a documented UA string, not a random one; the UA is logged.
    Rate is capped at 2 threads by default.
    The skill will not run against a hostname that resolves to a private range unless scope.yml explicitly permits internal targets.

Output

    ./findings/sqli-<slug>/sqlmap/ — sqlmap session directory.
    ./findings/sqli-<slug>/evidence/ — baseline and injected response pair.
    ./findings/sqli-<slug>/finding.yml.

Example
sqli-detect target_url="https://lab-02.internal.lab/product?id=1" cookie="PHPSESSID=abcd"
References

    sqlmap wiki: https://github.com/sqlmapproject/sqlmap/wiki
    OWASP SQL Injection: https://owasp.org/www-community/attacks/SQL_Injection


### 5.2 Reading sqlmap Output for a Report

The skill's post-processor pulls three specific lines from `sqlmap`'s log:

- `Parameter: id (GET)` — the parameter name and method.
- `Type: boolean-based blind` — the technique class.
- `Title: AND boolean-based blind - WHERE or HAVING clause` — the specific test.

Those three lines, plus the DBMS banner, are enough to write a report finding. Everything else is supporting evidence — keep it for the appendix, do not put it in the executive summary.

---

## 6. Skill 4 — `xss-check`

`dalfox` is a modern XSS scanner that supports parameter analysis, DOM sink discovery, and a scriptable custom payload set. The skill wraps it with a controlled payload file so you can point to exactly what was fired.

### 6.1 SKILL.md

```markdown
---
name: xss-check
description: Scan a URL or list of URLs for reflected, stored, and DOM XSS using dalfox and a curated payload set.
inputs:
  - name: target
    description: URL or path to a file of URLs. Hostnames must be in scope.
    required: true
  - name: mode
    description: url or file. Defaults to url.
    required: false
  - name: payloads
    description: Path to a payload file. Defaults to ./skills/xss-check/payloads.txt.
    required: false
allowed-tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
---

# xss-check

## When to use

Use during web assessment when you have identified reflection points, form inputs, or URL parameters that echo user input back to the page.

## Preconditions

- `dalfox` installed.
- Target host present in `./engagement/scope.yml`.
- A payload file with fewer than 200 entries. Large payload lists inflate runtime and noise without improving detection.

## Behavior

1. Scope check the target. Refuse on mismatch.
2. Log to `./logs/xss-check-<date>.log`.
3. If `mode=url`:
   ```bash
   dalfox url "$TARGET" \
     --custom-payload "$PAYLOADS" \
     --skip-bav \
     --skip-mining-dict \
     --worker 5 \
     --delay 200 \
     --format json \
     -o "./findings/xss-$SLUG/dalfox.json"

    If mode=file:

    dalfox file "$TARGET" \
      --custom-payload "$PAYLOADS" \
      --worker 5 \
      --delay 200 \
      --format json \
      -o "./findings/xss-$SLUG/dalfox.json"

    Post-process the JSON output into summary.md with, per finding:
        URL and parameter
        Payload that triggered
        Reflection context (attribute, JS block, HTML body)
        Evidence: request and response snippet

Safety rails

    No --headless browser automation without explicit approval — headless XSS proofs execute JavaScript against a real DOM and can trigger callbacks.
    No --blind mode without a documented, in-scope out-of-band listener.
    The default payload set contains only benign markers (alert('xss-check'), console.log('xss-check'), unique nonce strings).

Default payload set
"><svg/onload=alert('xss-check-'+Math.random())>
"><img src=x onerror=alert('xss-check')>
"><script>console.log('xss-check')</script>
'-alert('xss-check')-'
javascript:alert('xss-check')
"><iframe srcdoc="<script>alert('xss-check')</script>">
"onmouseover=alert('xss-check')//

Each payload is prefixed with xss-check- so grepping web logs for that string reveals every probe the skill fired.
Output

    ./findings/xss-<slug>/dalfox.json
    ./findings/xss-<slug>/summary.md

Example
xss-check target="https://lab-03.internal.lab/search?q=hello" mode=url
References

    dalfox: https://github.com/hahwul/dalfox
    OWASP XSS: https://owasp.org/www-community/attacks/xss/


### 6.2 Why the Payload Marker

Every payload contains the literal string `xss-check`. That marker exists so the blue team can correlate the probes to your engagement: they grep the WAF logs for `xss-check`, see your source IP and time window, and cleanly separate assessment noise from real attack traffic. This is a small courtesy that pays off enormously during the wash-up meeting.

---

## 7. Skill 5 — `poc-scaffold`

Scaffolding is deliberate. When you need to reproduce a vulnerability under authorization, you write the PoC yourself, informed by the public advisory, on the target you actually own. This skill produces the empty template — a checklist, references, placeholder steps — and refuses to fill in exploit code without a validated in-scope target and a human confirming the scope.

### 7.1 SKILL.md

```markdown
---
name: poc-scaffold
description: Given a CVE or advisory reference and a target, create a PoC directory with placeholder steps, references, and a validation checklist. Never generates working exploit code without explicit user confirmation and an in-scope target.
inputs:
  - name: advisory
    description: A CVE ID, GHSA ID, or vendor advisory URL.
    required: true
  - name: target
    description: In-scope target host, IP, or URL.
    required: true
  - name: technique
    description: Short slug for the technique, e.g. deserialization, sstemplate, ssrf.
    required: false
allowed-tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
  - WebFetch
---

# poc-scaffold

## When to use

Use when you have a validated finding (via cve-validate or manual analysis) and you need to prepare a proof-of-concept directory for reproduction under authorization.

## Preconditions

- `cve-validate` (or a manual equivalent) has produced a `plausible` verdict.
- Target is in `./engagement/scope.yml`.
- Engagement RoE permits reproduction of the vulnerability class (some ROEs disallow, e.g., RCE demonstrations).

## Behavior

1. Scope check the target.
2. Log to `./logs/poc-scaffold-<date>.log`.
3. Create `./poc/<advisory-slug>/` with the following files:

   - `README.md` — narrative header, references, RoE quote.
   - `CHECKLIST.md` — the validation checklist (see below).
   - `steps.md` — numbered reproduction steps with placeholders.
   - `references.md` — collected advisory URLs.
   - `evidence/` — empty directory for captured responses, screenshots, pcaps.
   - `reset.md` — how to restore the target to its pre-test state.

4. Populate `references.md` with:
   - The advisory URL and CVE ID.
   - The NVD entry.
   - The vendor bulletin.
   - Any public analysis by well-known researchers (blog posts from vendors, CERT, MITRE ATT&CK mapping).

5. Populate `CHECKLIST.md` with the checklist below.

6. Populate `steps.md` with **placeholders only**. Each step is a header and a description of what the operator would do; the skill never inserts a working payload.

## Validation checklist template

```markdown
- [ ] The target host is in ./engagement/scope.yml (verified this session)
- [ ] The engagement RoE permits this vulnerability class
- [ ] A human has confirmed the test window
- [ ] A reset procedure is documented in reset.md
- [ ] The blue team point of contact has been notified (if RoE requires)
- [ ] The finding directory `./findings/<id>/` exists
- [ ] The scan or manual step that surfaced the CVE is documented
- [ ] The version fingerprint from cve-validate is filed in evidence/
- [ ] The PoC steps do not run destructive flags (dump, shell, write)
- [ ] Evidence capture is enabled (tcpdump, mitmproxy, screen recording)
Safety rails

    The skill refuses to write to steps.md any of: base64-encoded blobs, bash -c, curl | sh, eval, powershell -enc, Invoke-Expression, nc -e, python -c "import".
    If the user asks Claude to fill in a working payload, the skill responds with the class of payload and the vendor's own advisory example, not a downloaded exploit.
    If the target is not in scope, the skill refuses to create the directory at all.

Output

    ./poc/<slug>/README.md
    ./poc/<slug>/CHECKLIST.md
    ./poc/<slug>/steps.md
    ./poc/<slug>/references.md
    ./poc/<slug>/reset.md

Example
poc-scaffold advisory=CVE-2021-44228 target=https://lab-01.internal.lab technique=jndi-injection
References

    Internal: ./engagement/roe.md
    MITRE ATT&CK: https://attack.mitre.org/


### 7.2 What a Scaffolded `steps.md` Looks Like

```markdown
# Reproduction Steps — CVE-2021-44228 (Log4Shell)

## Precondition
- [ ] Target: https://lab-01.internal.lab
- [ ] Version fingerprint confirmed vulnerable range: see evidence/fingerprint.txt

## Step 1 — Baseline request
Capture a baseline request to the endpoint the CVE affects. Save
the response headers and body to evidence/step-01-baseline.txt.
Do not send any injection payload here.

## Step 2 — Craft the class-of-payload
Consult the vendor advisory for the injection sink. For this CVE,
the sink is a log-formatted user-controlled string. Document the
class only — do not write the payload string here. The operator
will construct the payload in an interactive session with a colleague
observing.

## Step 3 — Fire against an out-of-band listener you control
Point the payload at an authorized listener (e.g. interactsh instance
in the assessment environment). Do not point at any third-party
service. Capture the listener log.

## Step 4 — Reset
Follow reset.md.

This is deliberately terse. It is a working template, not an exploit.
8. Skill 6 — web-fuzz-directories

ffuf is the fastest general-purpose web fuzzer in wide use. Directory brute-force is one of its most common applications, and it is easy to get wrong: bad wordlists produce hours of 404s, missing status filters bury real hits, and unbounded rates trigger WAF blocks that end the engagement window.
8.1 SKILL.md
---
name: web-fuzz-directories
description: Directory and file discovery with ffuf, tuned wordlists, and status-code filters.
inputs:
  - name: base_url
    description: Base URL with FUZZ placeholder or trailing path, e.g. https://lab/FUZZ.
    required: true
  - name: wordlist
    description: Path to a wordlist. Defaults to /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/Web-Content/raft-medium-directories.txt.
    required: false
  - name: extensions
    description: Comma-separated file extensions to append.
    required: false
  - name: filter_size
    description: Comma-separated response sizes to filter out (soft 404s).
    required: false
allowed-tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
---

# web-fuzz-directories

## When to use

Use after nmap and manual browsing have identified a web root, and you want to discover unlinked paths — admin interfaces, backup files, dev endpoints, ancient uploaded artifacts.

## Preconditions

- `ffuf` installed.
- SecLists installed at `/usr/share/seclists` or wordlist path passed explicitly.
- Target host present in `./engagement/scope.yml`.

## Behavior

1. Scope check the URL host. Refuse on mismatch.
2. Log target and wordlist to `./logs/web-fuzz-<date>.log`.
3. Calibrate first: send 5 random long strings (`fj83fh39fj3.txt`, etc.) and record the response length distribution. If a distribution is stable, add its lengths to `filter_size`.
4. Run:

   ```bash
   ffuf \
     -u "$BASE_URL" \
     -w "$WORDLIST" \
     ${EXTENSIONS:+-e "$EXTENSIONS"} \
     -mc 200,204,301,302,307,401,403 \
     ${FILTER_SIZE:+-fs "$FILTER_SIZE"} \
     -t 40 \
     -p 0.1 \
     -o "./findings/fuzz-$SLUG/ffuf.json" \
     -of json \
     -recursion \
     -recursion-depth 2

    Post-process into summary.md:
        Group hits by status code.
        Highlight 401 and 403 entries (authenticated content behind auth walls is often the real prize).
        Highlight paths with common backup extensions (.bak, .old, .zip, .tar, .tar.gz, .sql).

Safety rails

    Threads capped at 40. Rate is smoothed with -p 0.1 (100ms jitter).
    No -x (proxy) without explicit approval — a rogue proxy exfiltrates every request.
    Recursion depth capped at 2 to prevent runaway trees.
    If the host returns > 1000 hits in the first 30 seconds, the skill halts and asks for a tighter wordlist.

Output

    ./findings/fuzz-<slug>/ffuf.json
    ./findings/fuzz-<slug>/summary.md
    ./findings/fuzz-<slug>/calibration.txt

Example
web-fuzz-directories base_url="https://lab-04.internal.lab/FUZZ" extensions=".php,.bak,.old"
References

    ffuf: https://github.com/ffuf/ffuf
    SecLists: https://github.com/danielmiessler/SecLists


### 8.2 Tuned Wordlist Selection

A short table for choosing a wordlist by phase:

| Phase | Wordlist | Rationale |
|-------|----------|-----------|
| First pass | `raft-small-directories.txt` | Fast, hits most CMS paths |
| Second pass | `raft-medium-directories.txt` | Broader, still under an hour |
| Deep pass | `raft-large-directories.txt` | Overnight only |
| Backup hunt | `common-backup.txt` | With `-e .bak,.old,.zip,.tar,.gz` |
| API paths | `api-endpoints.txt` from Kiterunner | For REST endpoints |
| Config | `configs.txt` | `.env`, `wp-config.php`, `.git/config` |

Do not run the large wordlist without calibration. Soft 404s that pass content-length filters will fill your results with noise.

---

## 9. Skill 7 — `session-token-analyze`

Session tokens are one of the highest-yield validation targets. A misconfigured JWT can hand you administrative access; a short-secret HMAC token can be brute-forced offline in seconds. This skill decodes tokens, runs a small dictionary against JWT HMAC secrets, and flags common misconfigurations without ever attempting to use a forged token against a live service.

### 9.1 SKILL.md

```markdown
---
name: session-token-analyze
description: Decode a session token or JWT, check for weak HMAC secrets against a small dictionary, flag common misconfigurations.
inputs:
  - name: token
    description: The token string.
    required: true
  - name: dictionary
    description: Path to a wordlist for HMAC secret cracking. Defaults to ./skills/session-token-analyze/weak-secrets.txt.
    required: false
allowed-tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
---

# session-token-analyze

## When to use

Use whenever you obtain a session cookie, an Authorization header token, or any bearer credential during an assessment. Runs offline — no requests to the target.

## Preconditions

- `jwt_tool` or `python3` with `pyjwt` installed.
- `hashcat` installed for HMAC secret cracking.

## Behavior

1. Detect the token format:
   - Three dot-separated base64url segments → JWT
   - Single opaque base64 string → generic session ID
   - `hex.hex` → HMAC-signed cookie
2. If JWT:
   - Decode header and payload with `base64 -d`.
   - Extract `alg`, `kid`, `iss`, `aud`, `exp`, `iat`, `nbf`, custom claims.
   - Flag misconfigurations:
     - `alg: none`
     - `alg: HS256` when the payload references an admin role (`sub`, `role`, `is_admin`)
     - `exp` missing or > 24h
     - `kid` present with path-traversal-friendly value
     - Public claim collisions (`role` vs `roles`)
   - Attempt HMAC secret cracking with `hashcat -m 16500`:
     ```bash
     hashcat -m 16500 "$TOKEN" "$DICTIONARY" \
       -o "./findings/token-$SLUG/cracked.txt"
     ```
   - If cracked, print the secret and flag as critical.
3. If generic session ID:
   - Base64-decode and check for readable content (user IDs, roles, tenant IDs).
   - Check entropy (Shannon entropy > 4.5 bits/char is expected for random IDs; less suggests structure).
4. Write `./findings/token-<slug>/analysis.md` summarizing findings.

## Safety rails

- Never send the token to a third-party JWT decoder website. All decoding is local.
- Never fire a forged token against the live target. Forgery, if warranted, is a separate manual step under explicit approval.
- The default `weak-secrets.txt` dictionary contains fewer than 1000 entries — common defaults (`secret`, `changeme`, `password`, `jwt-secret`, and known framework defaults). Larger dictionaries require user approval.

## Default weak-secrets sample

secret change-me changeme password jwt-secret default supersecret your-256-bit-secret mysecret jwt_secret signing_key

## Output

- `./findings/token-<slug>/analysis.md`
- `./findings/token-<slug>/cracked.txt` (if applicable)

## Example

session-token-analyze token="eyJhbGciOi..."

## References

- jwt.io spec (offline reference): https://jwt.io/introduction
- hashcat modes: https://hashcat.net/wiki/doku.php?id=example_hashes
9.2 Reading the Analysis File

The analysis output looks like:
# JWT Analysis — token-a1b2c3

## Header
- alg: HS256
- typ: JWT
- kid: (none)

## Payload
- sub: alice@example.com
- role: user
- iat: 2026-09-05T12:00:00Z
- exp: 2027-09-05T12:00:00Z (365 days — flagged)

## Flags
- [MEDIUM] exp is 365 days — session lifetime is excessive
- [CRITICAL] HMAC secret cracked: `changeme`

## Recommendation
The signing secret `changeme` was recovered from the weak-secrets
dictionary. Any HS256 token issued by this service can be forged
by anyone with the token structure. Rotate the secret to a
cryptographically strong value (>= 32 random bytes) and revoke
all outstanding tokens.
10. Skill 8 — finding-reproduce

Once a finding is captured, it needs to be re-runnable — by you tomorrow, by the retest window in 30 days, and by the client's engineer after they patch. finding-reproduce reads a finding.yml and replays the reproduction steps in a controlled way, checking that the vulnerability is still present.
10.1 SKILL.md
---
name: finding-reproduce
description: Given a finding YAML, replay its reproduction steps in a controlled way and check whether the vulnerability is still present.
inputs:
  - name: finding
    description: Path to a finding.yml file.
    required: true
  - name: mode
    description: verify (default) or diff. verify runs the steps and compares; diff shows step-by-step differences.
    required: false
allowed-tools:
  - Bash
  - Read
  - Write
---

# finding-reproduce

## When to use

Use at the retest phase, or during an engagement whenever you want to confirm a finding is still exploitable before writing it up.

## Preconditions

- `finding.yml` exists and validates against the schema below.
- Every target in `finding.yml` is in `./engagement/scope.yml`.
- The RoE permits reproduction at the current time.

## finding.yml schema

```yaml
id: SQLI-2026-0001
title: Boolean-based blind SQLi in product lookup
severity: high
target:
  url: https://lab-02.internal.lab/product
  method: GET
  parameter: id
class: sqli
technique: boolean-blind
discovered_at: 2026-09-05T10:00:00Z
discovered_by: joasantonio108@gmail.com
authorization:
  scope_ref: ./engagement/scope.yml
  roe_ref: ./engagement/roe.md
reproduction:
  - name: baseline
    request: |
      GET /product?id=1 HTTP/1.1
      Host: lab-02.internal.lab
    expect:
      status: 200
      body_contains: "Widget A"
  - name: true-condition
    request: |
      GET /product?id=1%20AND%201=1 HTTP/1.1
      Host: lab-02.internal.lab
    expect:
      status: 200
      body_contains: "Widget A"
  - name: false-condition
    request: |
      GET /product?id=1%20AND%201=2 HTTP/1.1
      Host: lab-02.internal.lab
    expect:
      status: 200
      body_not_contains: "Widget A"
evidence:
  raw: ./findings/SQLI-2026-0001/evidence/
references:
  - https://owasp.org/www-community/attacks/Blind_SQL_Injection
Behavior

    Load finding.yml, validate schema.
    Scope check target.url against ./engagement/scope.yml. Refuse on mismatch.
    For each reproduction step:
        Send the request with curl -sS -i (headers included).
        Capture status, headers, body to ./findings/<id>/replay/<step>.txt.
        Compare against expect:
            status — exact match
            body_contains — substring present
            body_not_contains — substring absent
            latency_ms_gte — response time floor (for time-based tests)
    Emit ./findings/<id>/replay/verdict.md:
        PRESENT — every step matched expectations
        MITIGATED — one or more steps no longer produce the vulnerable behavior
        INCONCLUSIVE — the target is unreachable or the response shape has drifted
    If mode=diff, additionally emit a side-by-side diff of each step's expected vs. actual response.

Safety rails

    The skill only reads YAML; it does not write to finding.yml.
    Requests use curl -sS --max-time 15 — no unbounded requests.
    The skill refuses to replay any step whose request block contains destructive keywords (see poc-scaffold Safety Rails).
    Every replayed target hit is logged to ./logs/finding-reproduce-<date>.log.

Output

    ./findings/<id>/replay/<step>.txt — captured raw responses
    ./findings/<id>/replay/verdict.md

Example
finding-reproduce finding=./findings/SQLI-2026-0001/finding.yml 
### 10.2 Why YAML

A structured finding format is the single biggest lift a small skill can give. Once findings are YAML, everything downstream — the report generator, the retest window, the CI job that runs before a release — reads from one source of truth. Text-only findings die in someone's Notes app.

---

## 11. Skill Scope Guard Convention

Every skill in this lesson reads `./engagement/scope.yml`. That is the guard rail that keeps a well-meaning skill from firing at a bystander asset.

### 11.1 The `scope.yml` Schema

```yaml
engagement:
  id: ACME-2026-Q3
  window:
    start: 2026-09-01T00:00:00Z
    end: 2026-09-30T23:59:59Z
targets:
  hosts:
    - lab-01.internal.lab
    - lab-02.internal.lab
    - lab-03.internal.lab
    - lab-04.internal.lab
  ip_ranges:
    - 10.10.10.0/24
  wildcards:
    - "*.staging.internal.lab"
  disallowed:
    - production.internal.lab
    - "*.customer-owned.example.com"
techniques:
  scanning: allowed
  active_exploitation: allowed
  data_extraction: disallowed
  destructive: disallowed
  social_engineering: disallowed
  physical: disallowed
contacts:
  primary: security-lead@example.com
  escalation: soc@example.com
  phone: +1-555-0100
11.2 The Scope-Check Snippet

Every skill embeds a lightweight scope check. In shell, the reference implementation is:
scope_check() {
  local host="$1"
  local scope="./engagement/scope.yml"

  if [[ ! -f "$scope" ]]; then
    echo "REFUSED: no ./engagement/scope.yml found" >&2
    exit 2
  fi

  # Exact host match
  if yq -r '.targets.hosts[]' "$scope" | grep -Fxq "$host"; then
    return 0
  fi

  # Wildcard match
  while read -r pattern; do
    [[ -z "$pattern" ]] && continue
    # Convert *.example.com to regex ^[^.]+\.example\.com$
    regex="^${pattern//./\\.}$"
    regex="${regex//\\.\*/\.*}"
    if [[ "$host" =~ $regex ]]; then
      return 0
    fi
  done < <(yq -r '.targets.wildcards[]' "$scope")

  # Disallowed match short-circuits
  while read -r bad; do
    [[ -z "$bad" ]] && continue
    if [[ "$host" == "$bad" ]]; then
      echo "REFUSED: $host is in disallowed list" >&2
      exit 3
    fi
  done < <(yq -r '.targets.disallowed[]' "$scope")

  echo "REFUSED: $host not in scope" >&2
  exit 4
}

Each skill sources this function and calls scope_check "$hostname" before any network activity. The exit codes matter: 2 means missing scope file, 3 means explicitly disallowed, 4 means not listed. A blue team reading the logs later can immediately tell the difference.
11.3 Logging Every Target Hit

Every skill appends one line per target hit to ./logs/<skill>-<date>.log:
2026-09-05T14:02:11Z nuclei-run start target=https://lab-01.internal.lab user=joasantonio108@gmail.com engagement=ACME-2026-Q3
2026-09-05T14:02:12Z nuclei-run request template=cves/2021/CVE-2021-44228.yaml url=https://lab-01.internal.lab/api/login
2026-09-05T14:07:44Z nuclei-run finish target=https://lab-01.internal.lab hits=3 duration=333s

The log is append-only and rotates daily. In practice this file is your alibi: it proves what you touched and when.
12. Output Convention

Every skill writes evidence to ./findings/<id>/. The layout is standardized:
findings/
  <id>/
    finding.yml           # canonical record
    evidence/             # raw captures
      request-01.txt
      response-01.txt
      screenshot-01.png
      pcap-01.pcap
    reproduce.sh          # single script that replays the finding
    report.md             # human-readable narrative
12.1 The reproduce.sh Contract

Every finding has a reproduce.sh that, run from the engagement root, reproduces the vulnerability. It must:

    Be idempotent
    Read scope from ./engagement/scope.yml
    Refuse on scope mismatch
    Print each step to stdout before executing it
    Save each response to evidence/

Example:
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail
source ./skills/lib/scope-guard.sh

TARGET="lab-02.internal.lab"
scope_check "$TARGET"

echo "Step 1 — baseline"
curl -sS -i "https://$TARGET/product?id=1" \
  -o evidence/step-01-baseline.txt

echo "Step 2 — true condition"
curl -sS -i "https://$TARGET/product?id=1%20AND%201=1" \
  -o evidence/step-02-true.txt

echo "Step 3 — false condition"
curl -sS -i "https://$TARGET/product?id=1%20AND%201=2" \
  -o evidence/step-03-false.txt

echo "Reproduction complete. Compare evidence/step-02 and step-03."
12.2 Naming Conventions

Findings are named <CLASS>-<YEAR>-<NNNN>. For example:

    SQLI-2026-0001
    XSS-2026-0002
    AUTH-2026-0003
    RCE-2026-0004

The class prefix makes report generation trivial: group by prefix, sort by severity, done.
13. Chain Example

The eight skills chain naturally. The canonical chain is:
nuclei-run → cve-validate → finding-reproduce → poc-scaffold
13.1 Step 1 — nuclei-run
> nuclei-run target_file=./targets.txt severity=high,critical
Loaded 4 targets from ./targets.txt.
Scope check: all targets in ./engagement/scope.yml.
Running nuclei with community-templates, severity high,critical.
Wrote ./findings/nuclei-20260905T140000/raw.jsonl.
Wrote ./findings/nuclei-20260905T140000/summary.md.

summary.md contains three high-severity hits. The most interesting is:
### CVE-2021-44228 — Apache Log4j RCE (Log4Shell)
- Severity: critical
- Matched: https://lab-01.internal.lab/api/login
- Template: cves/2021/CVE-2021-44228.yaml
- Next skill: cve-validate cve_id=CVE-2021-44228 target=https://lab-01.internal.lab
13.2 Step 2 — cve-validate
> cve-validate cve_id=CVE-2021-44228 target=https://lab-01.internal.lab
Scope check: lab-01.internal.lab in scope.
Fetched NVD entry for CVE-2021-44228.
Fingerprinting target: Server=Apache/2.4.51, X-Powered-By=(none),
  favicon=fdc1d8f2..., /manager/html=404.
Detected Log4j via error page banner in /api/login response.
Version fingerprint: log4j-core 2.14.1 → in vulnerable range (< 2.15.0).
Verdict: plausible.
Wrote ./findings/CVE-2021-44228/methodology.md.

The methodology file describes a non-destructive JNDI probe pointing to an authorized out-of-band listener you control.
13.3 Step 3 — finding-reproduce

You draft a finding YAML by hand or by adapting the template, then reproduce it:
> finding-reproduce finding=./findings/RCE-2026-0001/finding.yml
Loaded RCE-2026-0001, schema OK.
Scope check: lab-01.internal.lab in scope.
Step 1 — baseline: HTTP 200, body_contains "Login" — OK.
Step 2 — jndi-probe: HTTP 200, latency 210ms — OK.
Step 3 — oob-callback: listener received DNS query from lab-01 — OK.
Verdict: PRESENT.
Wrote ./findings/RCE-2026-0001/replay/verdict.md.
13.4 Step 4 — poc-scaffold
> poc-scaffold advisory=CVE-2021-44228 target=https://lab-01.internal.lab technique=jndi-injection
Scope check: lab-01.internal.lab in scope.
Created ./poc/CVE-2021-44228/.
Wrote README.md, CHECKLIST.md, steps.md, references.md, reset.md.
steps.md contains placeholders only — human operator to fill in
under supervision per RoE.

You now have a scaffolded PoC directory, a validated finding, and a reproduction script — all with per-target logs, all bounded by scope.
14. Guardrails Against Silent Damage

Skills that touch live systems can cause silent damage. A misfired scan against a legacy authentication service can lock every account. A directory brute-force against a mailer can trigger a hundred password-reset emails to real users. The skills in this lesson enforce five defaults to prevent that.
14.1 No Destructive Flags in Defaults

Every skill has a list of flags that are refused unless the caller explicitly opts in and the scope file explicitly permits the technique.
Skill	Refused flag	Enabling condition
sqli-detect	--dump, --os-shell, --sql-shell	techniques.data_extraction: allowed
xss-check	--blind, --headless	RoE line item + human confirmation
web-fuzz-directories	-x proxy, unbounded threads	Human confirmation
poc-scaffold	Any working exploit payload	Skill never emits, period
cve-validate	Any exploit request	Skill never emits, period
14.2 Rate Caps

Every skill has an implicit rate cap:

    nuclei-run — 50 rps
    sqli-detect — 2 threads
    xss-check — 5 workers, 200ms delay
    web-fuzz-directories — 40 threads, 100ms jitter

Raising a rate cap requires editing the skill file (auditable) and typically a note in the engagement log.
14.3 Explicit Approval for State Changes

Any skill or PoC step that could modify server state — writes to /upload/, admin form submits, password resets, account creation — must be gated behind an explicit "APPROVED_STATE_CHANGE=1" environment variable. The skill logs the change to a special file ./logs/state-changes-<date>.log with:

    Timestamp
    Target
    Endpoint
    Approver (from git config user.email or the USER env var)
    Reset procedure reference

14.4 Kill Switches

Every long-running skill respects two kill switches:

    A file at ./engagement/PAUSE — check every 30 seconds, halt if it exists.
    A file at ./engagement/STOP — check every 30 seconds, exit non-zero if it exists.

The engagement lead can drop those files without needing to know which Kali box is running.
14.5 Time-Window Enforcement

If scope.yml sets a window.start and window.end, every skill checks the current time on start and refuses if outside the window. This prevents accidental after-hours runs.
15. Full End-to-End Walk

The following is a lab-only walkthrough. The lab is the same Docker-based environment you built at the end of Module 5: four lab-0N.internal.lab hosts under 10.10.10.0/24, all running deliberately vulnerable services.
15.1 Environment Setup
mkdir -p engagement findings poc logs skills
cat > engagement/scope.yml <<'EOF'
engagement:
  id: LAB-2026-M06L04
  window:
    start: 2026-09-05T00:00:00Z
    end: 2026-09-30T23:59:59Z
targets:
  hosts:
    - lab-01.internal.lab
    - lab-02.internal.lab
    - lab-03.internal.lab
    - lab-04.internal.lab
  ip_ranges:
    - 10.10.10.0/24
  wildcards: []
  disallowed:
    - 10.10.10.1
techniques:
  scanning: allowed
  active_exploitation: allowed
  data_extraction: disallowed
  destructive: disallowed
contacts:
  primary: self@lab.local
EOF

cat > targets.txt <<'EOF'
https://lab-01.internal.lab
https://lab-02.internal.lab/product?id=1
https://lab-03.internal.lab/search?q=hello
https://lab-04.internal.lab
EOF
15.2 Register the Skills in Claude Code
> /skill register ./skills/nuclei-run/SKILL.md
> /skill register ./skills/cve-validate/SKILL.md
> /skill register ./skills/sqli-detect/SKILL.md
> /skill register ./skills/xss-check/SKILL.md
> /skill register ./skills/poc-scaffold/SKILL.md
> /skill register ./skills/web-fuzz-directories/SKILL.md
> /skill register ./skills/session-token-analyze/SKILL.md
> /skill register ./skills/finding-reproduce/SKILL.md
15.3 First-Pass Scan

Ask Claude Code to run the first-pass triage:
Run nuclei-run against ./targets.txt and summarize the top three findings.

Claude Code invokes nuclei-run, waits for it to finish, and returns a Markdown block that shows:
Top three findings (severity high/critical):

1. CVE-2021-44228 (Log4Shell) — lab-01, /api/login endpoint (critical)
2. Reflected XSS in search parameter — lab-03, /search?q= (high)
3. Directory listing enabled — lab-04, /backup/ (high)
15.4 Deepen the First Hit
Validate the Log4Shell finding on lab-01. Do not run an exploit;
just confirm plausibility with cve-validate.

Claude Code runs cve-validate cve_id=CVE-2021-44228 target=https://lab-01.internal.lab. The output methodology file is captured under ./findings/CVE-2021-44228/methodology.md. Claude Code then asks for confirmation before proceeding further.
15.5 Deepen the Second Hit
Run xss-check against the search endpoint on lab-03.

Claude Code runs xss-check target="https://lab-03.internal.lab/search?q=xss-check" mode=url. It reports:
XSS confirmed. Parameter: q. Reflection context: HTML body,
outside attribute. Successful payload:
  "><svg/onload=alert('xss-check-0.42')>
Evidence saved to ./findings/xss-a1b2c3/.
15.6 Deepen the Third Hit
Fuzz lab-04 with web-fuzz-directories using default wordlist and
common backup extensions.

Claude Code runs web-fuzz-directories. Output:
Calibration: baseline 404 size = 4123 bytes. Filtering that size.
Hits (200): /backup/, /old/, /admin/
Hits (401): /manage/, /api/v1/users/
Hits (200, backup ext): /backup/db.sql.old (72MB)

You do not download the 72MB file. You note it and move on — the finding is the presence of the backup, not its contents. Data extraction is disallowed in scope.
15.7 Session Token Triage

The /manage/ endpoint returned an authentication challenge that included a JWT cookie in an error response. Feed that token to session-token-analyze:
Analyze this JWT: eyJhbGciOi...

Claude Code runs session-token-analyze. It cracks the HS256 secret against the default weak-secrets dictionary in 0.4 seconds. The secret is jwt-secret. This is now a critical finding — every token issued by that service is forgeable.
15.8 Reproduce and Scaffold

Build the finding YAML for the JWT weakness, then reproduce:
id: AUTH-2026-0004
title: HS256 JWT signed with a weak, dictionary-recoverable secret
severity: critical
target:
  url: https://lab-04.internal.lab/manage/
class: auth
technique: jwt-weak-secret
authorization:
  scope_ref: ./engagement/scope.yml
  roe_ref: ./engagement/roe.md
reproduction:
  - name: fetch-error-token
    request: |
      GET /manage/ HTTP/1.1
      Host: lab-04.internal.lab
    expect:
      status: 401
      body_contains: "eyJhbGciOi"
evidence:
  raw: ./findings/AUTH-2026-0004/evidence/
references:
  - https://owasp.org/www-community/vulnerabilities/JWT_algorithm_confusion

Then:
> finding-reproduce finding=./findings/AUTH-2026-0004/finding.yml
Verdict: PRESENT.

And scaffold the PoC directory (no exploit code — just the harness):
> poc-scaffold advisory="OWASP JWT weak secret" target=https://lab-04.internal.lab technique=jwt-weak-secret
Wrote ./poc/jwt-weak-secret/README.md, CHECKLIST.md, steps.md, references.md, reset.md.

You now have four validated findings — one critical (JWT), one critical (Log4Shell, plausibility verdict), one high (XSS), one high (exposed backup listing) — with reproducible evidence directories, ready for the report skill you will build in Lesson 05.
16. Exercises
16.1 Guided Exercises

Exercise 16.1.1 — Author the scope-guard library.

Create ./skills/lib/scope-guard.sh with the scope_check function from section 11.2. Write two unit tests: one that passes for an in-scope host, one that fails for an out-of-scope host. Run them and confirm both behaviors.

Exercise 16.1.2 — Wire nuclei-run against your own lab.

Stand up a deliberately vulnerable target (OWASP Juice Shop, DVWA, or a local Docker image). Add the host to scope.yml. Register nuclei-run. Run it and read the summary Markdown. Confirm that raw.jsonl and summary.md both exist, and that the log file shows one target-hit line per template hit.

Exercise 16.1.3 — Reproduce a finding.

Write a finding.yml for one finding you produced in Exercise 16.1.2. Run finding-reproduce twice: once immediately, once after restarting the target container so it comes up clean. Confirm the first verdict is PRESENT and the second is either PRESENT (if the vuln is intrinsic) or INCONCLUSIVE (if the container's state matters).
16.2 Independent Exercises

Exercise 16.2.1 — Extend cve-validate with a local cache.

Add a step to cve-validate that, before hitting the NVD API, checks ./cache/nvd/<cve>.json and reuses the cached advisory if present. Add a --refresh flag to bypass the cache. Document the change in the SKILL.md.

Exercise 16.2.2 — Author a new skill.

Write a ssrf-probe skill following the conventions in this lesson: scope-guard, ./findings/ output, no destructive defaults, references public advisories. The skill should take a URL and a probe host, and check whether the target fetches an arbitrary URL (via Host header, redirect, or URL parameter). Bound the test to your own OAST listener.

Exercise 16.2.3 — Author a defensive-detection companion.

For each of the eight skills in this lesson, write one Sigma rule or one Suricata rule that would detect the skill running against a target. Save them to ./defensive/detections/. The point of the exercise is to think about what the blue team would see, and to make your assessment process transparent to defenders.
17. Summary and Next-Lesson Pointer

You now have eight validation-oriented skills that share one scope guard, one evidence layout, and one convention for what they will and will not do. The skills do not replace judgment — they encode it. Every one of them refuses to run against out-of-scope targets, none of them ship exploit code, all of them log every target hit, and every finding they produce is replayable.

The chain you walked in section 15 is the daily rhythm of a paid engagement:

    Scan wide with nuclei-run.
    Validate a hit with cve-validate.
    Reproduce it with finding-reproduce.
    Scaffold a PoC with poc-scaffold.
    Triage tokens with session-token-analyze.
    Fuzz for hidden content with web-fuzz-directories.
    Detect XSS with xss-check.
    Detect SQLi with sqli-detect.

Each skill's output feeds the next. Each skill's output is also the raw material for the report skill you will build in the next lesson.

Next lesson: Lesson 05 — Reporting and Retest Skills. You will build the skills that consume the ./findings/ directory this lesson produced and emit an executive summary, a technical appendix, a retest bundle for the client, and a schedule of retest windows. The lesson 
















Lesson 05 — Building Report Generation Skills

    Module 06: Building Pentest Skills with Claude and Codex · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

Ask any senior penetration tester how they spend their week and the honest answer is uncomfortable: the majority of billable hours are not spent typing exploits into a shell — they are spent typing prose into a report. Screenshots have to be cropped and captioned. Requests and responses have to be sanitized. CVSS vectors have to be argued about in Slack. Executive summaries have to be rewritten because the CISO wants three paragraphs, not five. Methodology sections have to be copied from the last engagement and then edited so the client cannot tell.

That reporting workload is the single largest lever for productivity in an offensive security practice. If you can compress a five-day writing sprint into a one-day review-and-ship sprint, you have effectively doubled the number of engagements a consultant can deliver in a year — without cutting a single corner on quality. The way to compress that sprint is to stop treating the report as a monolithic Word document assembled by hand and start treating it as a build artifact produced by a pipeline of small, well-scoped skills.

This lesson teaches you to build that pipeline as a set of eight Claude Code skills, each with a narrow contract, deterministic inputs, and predictable outputs. The pipeline reads from a YAML source of truth, enriches it with external threat intelligence, renders it through a Typst template, and produces a client-ready PDF. Every step is auditable. Every LLM-drafted paragraph is gated by human review. Every finding card looks identical to every other finding card, engagement after engagement, because the template — not the tester's mood at 11pm on delivery day — decides the format.

By the end of this lesson, you will have a skill catalog you can copy into any Red Team Leaders engagement and run end-to-end from findings/*/finding.yml to report.pdf in a single Claude Code session. You will also understand where LLM assistance ends and human judgment begins, which is the single most important boundary in any AI-assisted deliverable process.
1. The Findings Pipeline — YAML as Source of Truth

Before writing any skill, you have to commit to a data model. Every finding in the pipeline has exactly one canonical representation: a YAML file at ./findings/<slug>/finding.yml. Everything else — the Markdown card, the finding tile in the appendix, the row in the executive summary risk matrix, the entry in the CSV export the client requested — is derived from that YAML by transformation. No skill in the pipeline is allowed to invent a field that does not appear in the schema. No human is allowed to hand-edit the Markdown card without updating the YAML first.

The pipeline has four stages:
┌──────────────┐    ┌────────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────┐
│  finding.yml │ ─► │ skill enrich   │ ─► │ Markdown     │ ─► │ Typst    │
│  (raw input) │    │ CVSS, CVE,     │    │ finding card │    │ compile  │
│              │    │ OWASP, ATT&CK  │    │ + summary    │    │ → PDF    │
└──────────────┘    └────────────────┘    └──────────────┘    └──────────┘

Each stage is idempotent. Running finding-enrich twice on the same YAML produces the same output. Running finding-card twice produces byte-identical Markdown. This property is what makes the pipeline safe to re-run at any point — if the client asks for a change to the executive summary at 4pm on delivery day, you edit the YAML, re-run the last three stages, and the PDF is regenerated in seconds.

The canonical finding.yml schema is:
id: RTL-2026-0042
slug: apache-struts-rce-cve-2017-5638
title: Remote Code Execution in Apache Struts Content-Type Header
severity: critical
status: draft
target:
  hostname: erp.example.internal
  ip: 10.20.30.40
  port: 8080
  url: https://erp.example.internal/struts2-showcase/showcase.action
  asset_class: production-web-application
discovered:
  by: joao.antonio
  date: 2026-09-04
  tool: burp-suite-scanner
  request_id: burp-req-000817
description: |
  The application runs Apache Struts 2.3.20, vulnerable to CVE-2017-5638.
  The Jakarta Multipart parser evaluates OGNL expressions supplied in the
  Content-Type header, leading to unauthenticated remote command execution
  as the tomcat service account.
impact: |
  An unauthenticated attacker can execute arbitrary operating system
  commands with the privileges of the Tomcat service account. The Tomcat
  account has read access to the customer database credentials stored in
  /opt/tomcat/conf/db.properties, which grants direct access to 2.4M
  customer records including PII regulated under LGPD.
cvss:
  version: "4.0"
  vector: ""       # filled by cvss-score
  base_score: 0    # filled by cvss-score
  rationale: ""    # filled by cvss-score
references:
  cve: []          # filled by finding-enrich
  owasp_top_10: [] # filled by finding-enrich
  wstg: []         # filled by finding-enrich
  attack: []       # filled by finding-enrich
evidence:
  screenshots: []  # filled by evidence-collect
  requests: []     # filled by evidence-collect
  responses: []    # filled by evidence-collect
  tool_output: []  # filled by evidence-collect
remediation:
  short: ""        # human-authored
  detailed: ""     # human-authored
retest:
  status: not-yet-retested
  date: null
  outcome: null

Every field in the schema has a designated author. Some are human-authored (title, description, impact, remediation). Some are skill-authored (cvss, references, evidence). Some are machine-authored at import time (discovered, id). The pipeline enforces this by refusing to run enrichment skills on fields the human is supposed to write, and refusing to let humans overwrite fields the machines are supposed to write.
2. Skill 1 — finding-create

The first skill is the simplest one — it takes a title, a description, evidence pointers, and a target, then produces a normalized YAML on disk with the correct filename slug and a generated ID.
2.1 Contract

Inputs:

    title (string, required) — human-readable finding title
    description (string, required) — one to three paragraphs of technical description
    target_url (string, required) — the URL, host, or asset the finding is against
    evidence_paths (list of strings, optional) — paths to raw evidence files to be organized later
    severity_hint (string, optional) — critical | high | medium | low | info, used only as a starting point

Outputs:

    Creates ./findings/<slug>/finding.yml with the schema above
    Creates ./findings/<slug>/evidence/ as an empty directory
    Returns the slug to the caller

Guarantees:

    Slug is derived from the title via a deterministic slugifier — lowercase, alphanumeric plus hyphens, max 60 chars
    ID is RTL-<year>-<counter> where counter is the next unused integer under ./findings/
    The file will not be created if a finding with the same slug already exists — the skill refuses instead of overwriting

2.2 Full SKILL.md
---
name: finding-create
description: |
  Create a new penetration-testing finding as a YAML file under
  ./findings/<slug>/finding.yml. Use this whenever a new vulnerability
  has been confirmed and needs to enter the reporting pipeline.
inputs:
  title:
    type: string
    required: true
  description:
    type: string
    required: true
  target_url:
    type: string
    required: true
  evidence_paths:
    type: array
    required: false
  severity_hint:
    type: string
    required: false
    enum: [critical, high, medium, low, info]
allowed_tools: [Read, Write, Bash]
---

# finding-create

## When to invoke

The user says one of:
- "log a new finding"
- "create finding for <thing>"
- "add this vuln to the report"
- pastes a raw description and asks you to file it

## Procedure

1. Derive the slug from the title:
   - Lowercase.
   - Replace any non-alphanumeric character with a hyphen.
   - Collapse consecutive hyphens.
   - Strip leading/trailing hyphens.
   - Truncate to 60 characters.
   - If the resulting slug is empty, abort with an error.

2. Compute the next ID:
   - Run `ls ./findings/ | wc -l` to count existing findings.
   - Add one; format as `RTL-<current-year>-<counter zero-padded to 4>`.

3. Check for collisions:
   - If `./findings/<slug>/finding.yml` already exists, abort and tell the
     user to use `finding-update` instead.

4. Write the YAML using the canonical schema.
   - `severity` = severity_hint if provided, else `medium`.
   - `status` = `draft`.
   - `discovered.by` = the current git user.email if available, else `unknown`.
   - `discovered.date` = today (YYYY-MM-DD).
   - All skill-authored fields left blank as shown in the schema.

5. Create `./findings/<slug>/evidence/` as an empty directory.

6. If `evidence_paths` was provided, do NOT move the files yet — that is
   `evidence-collect`'s job. Just record the paths in a temporary
   `.pending-evidence` file inside the finding directory.

7. Return: "Created finding <ID> at ./findings/<slug>/finding.yml".

## Non-goals

- This skill never computes CVSS. That is `cvss-score`.
- This skill never fetches CVE metadata. That is `finding-enrich`.
- This skill never renders Markdown. That is `finding-card`.
2.3 Example invocation
User: log a new finding — Apache Struts RCE on erp.example.internal port 8080,
      unauthenticated content-type OGNL injection, evidence in ~/burp/struts.zip

Claude (invokes finding-create):
  slug     = "apache-struts-rce-on-erp-example-internal"
  id       = "RTL-2026-0042"
  → wrote ./findings/apache-struts-rce-on-erp-example-internal/finding.yml
  → created ./findings/apache-struts-rce-on-erp-example-internal/evidence/
  → recorded pending evidence: ~/burp/struts.zip

Note the skill never writes to the description, impact, or remediation text based on its own understanding of Struts. Those are human inputs. It records what it is given, verbatim.
3. Skill 2 — cvss-score

CVSS scoring is the single most contentious part of a pentest report. Two testers looking at the same finding can produce vectors that differ by two or three levels. The cvss-score skill does not resolve that disagreement — humans still disagree — but it enforces a consistent starting point and forces the tester to justify every metric.
3.1 What the skill does

    Reads the finding's description, impact, and target fields.
    Uses the LLM to draft a CVSS 4.0 vector string.
    Validates the vector against the CVSS 4.0 spec — refuses to write invalid combinations.
    Computes the base score from the vector using a deterministic formula (not the LLM).
    Writes a rationale paragraph explaining each metric choice.
    Marks the finding's cvss block as pending-review so a human must sign off.

3.2 Full SKILL.md
---
name: cvss-score
description: |
  Compute a CVSS 4.0 vector, base score, and rationale for a finding.
  Uses LLM assist to draft the vector from the description, then validates
  against the CVSS 4.0 spec and computes the base score deterministically.
inputs:
  slug:
    type: string
    required: true
allowed_tools: [Read, Edit, Bash]
---

# cvss-score

## When to invoke

- After `finding-create` has produced a YAML with description and impact.
- When the user asks "score this finding" or "add CVSS".
- Refuse if `description` or `impact` is empty — you cannot score air.

## Procedure

1. Read `./findings/<slug>/finding.yml`.

2. If `cvss.vector` is already populated and `cvss.status` is `approved`,
   refuse and tell the user to run `cvss-score --force` (a separate flow).

3. Draft the vector.
   Read the description, impact, and target fields. For each of the eleven
   base metrics of CVSS 4.0, choose a value and record a one-sentence
   justification. The eleven metrics are:

   AV (Attack Vector)     — N | A | L | P
   AC (Attack Complexity) — L | H
   AT (Attack Requirements) — N | P
   PR (Privileges Required) — N | L | H
   UI (User Interaction)  — N | P | A
   VC (Vuln Confidentiality Impact) — H | L | N
   VI (Vuln Integrity Impact)       — H | L | N
   VA (Vuln Availability Impact)    — H | L | N
   SC (Subsequent Confidentiality Impact) — H | L | N
   SI (Subsequent Integrity Impact)       — H | L | N
   SA (Subsequent Availability Impact)    — H | L | N

4. Assemble the vector string in canonical order:
   CVSS:4.0/AV:.../AC:.../AT:.../PR:.../UI:.../VC:.../VI:.../VA:.../SC:.../SI:.../SA:...

5. Validate.
   Run `cvss4-validate "<vector>"` — a small bundled Python tool that
   checks the vector against the CVSS 4.0 spec. If it returns non-zero,
   the LLM draft is broken; regenerate once, then abort with the
   validation error if it fails again.

6. Compute the base score.
   Run `cvss4-score "<vector>"` — again a bundled tool, not the LLM.
   The LLM is not allowed to compute the number; it is allowed only to
   draft the vector. This is the deterministic-vs-drafted boundary.

7. Write back to the YAML:
     cvss:
       version: "4.0"
       vector: "<validated vector>"
       base_score: <computed>
       status: pending-review
       rationale: |
         AV:N — the vulnerability is exposed on a network interface reachable
         from the corporate LAN without authentication.
         AC:L — no special conditions are required to exploit.
         ...

8. Return: "Scored finding <slug> at <base_score> (<severity>). Vector
   marked pending-review; human sign-off required before ship."

## Notes

- Never guess metrics you cannot support from the description. Ask the user.
- If VC/VI/VA are all N and SC/SI/SA are all N, the score is 0.0. That is
  almost always a mistake in the description; ask the user to expand it.
3.3 The deterministic boundary

This skill is the clearest example in the pipeline of the deterministic-vs-LLM boundary. The LLM drafts the vector because reading English and mapping it to metric values is exactly what LLMs are good at. The score is computed by a bundled Python tool because arithmetic is exactly what LLMs are bad at. If you let the LLM compute the score, you will occasionally get a 7.4 vector reported as 8.1, and that will end up in a client PDF. Do not do that.
4. Skill 3 — finding-enrich

Once a finding is scored, it needs to be tied to the broader threat landscape — CVE numbers, OWASP Top 10 categories, WSTG test IDs, and MITRE ATT&CK techniques. This context matters for two reasons: it lets the client's blue team map your finding to detections they already own, and it demonstrates to the client's auditor that the assessment covered recognized threat frameworks.
4.1 Full SKILL.md
---
name: finding-enrich
description: |
  Attach CVE metadata, OWASP Top 10 categories, WSTG test IDs, and MITRE
  ATT&CK technique IDs to a finding. Pulls from NVD and bundled reference
  tables; refuses to invent identifiers.
inputs:
  slug:
    type: string
    required: true
  cve_hint:
    type: string
    required: false
    description: "If the finding is a known CVE, name it (e.g. CVE-2017-5638)."
allowed_tools: [Read, Edit, Bash, WebFetch]
---

# finding-enrich

## When to invoke

- After `cvss-score` has produced a validated vector.
- The user asks to "enrich" a finding, "add references", or "add ATT&CK
  mapping".

## Procedure

1. Read `./findings/<slug>/finding.yml`.

2. Resolve CVE metadata.
   If `cve_hint` was passed, verify the format matches `CVE-YYYY-NNNN+`
   and fetch from NVD:
     WebFetch https://services.nvd.nist.gov/rest/json/cves/2.0?cveId=<CVE>
   Extract:
     - Published date
     - CWE ID(s)
     - Affected products (cpeMatch)
     - Reference URLs
   If the CVE is not in NVD, note that fact but do not invent a description.

3. Map to OWASP Top 10 (2021 edition).
   Read the bundled table `refs/owasp-top-10-2021.yml`. For each entry,
   check whether the finding's description or CWE matches its included
   CWE list. Attach every matching category as
     references.owasp_top_10:
       - id: A03:2021
         name: Injection
         reason: "CWE-77 command injection matches A03 category."

4. Map to WSTG.
   Read `refs/wstg-4.2.yml`. Match by CWE or by keyword. Attach:
     references.wstg:
       - id: WSTG-INPV-12
         name: "Testing for Command Injection"
         url: https://owasp.org/.../WSTG-INPV-12/

5. Map to MITRE ATT&CK.
   Read `refs/attack-enterprise-14.yml`. Match on technique keywords
   drawn from the finding's impact section (not the description).
   Attach up to three highest-relevance technique IDs:
     references.attack:
       - id: T1190
         name: "Exploit Public-Facing Application"
         tactic: "Initial Access"

6. Write back to the YAML. Never overwrite existing entries; append.

7. Return a one-paragraph summary of what was attached, so a human can
   spot-check the mappings.

## Refusals

- Do not attach a CVE unless it was passed as cve_hint AND resolved
  successfully in NVD. Hallucinated CVE numbers in a client report are
  a career-ending event.
- Do not attach a WSTG or ATT&CK ID that is not in the bundled table.
  The tables are the ground truth.
4.2 Why bundled tables

The skill relies on three bundled YAML files: refs/owasp-top-10-2021.yml, refs/wstg-4.2.yml, refs/attack-enterprise-14.yml. These are shipped with the skill catalog and versioned in git. The skill will not hit the live OWASP or MITRE sites for these mappings — it uses the frozen tables. Why? Because a client report needs to be reproducible six months from now, and the live sites change. The tables are updated on a scheduled cadence (once per quarter) and the update is a deliberate act, not an accident.

CVE lookups are the exception because NVD entries are stable once published and clients expect current data.
5. Skill 4 — evidence-collect

Every finding needs evidence, and evidence organization is the second-most-time-consuming activity in reporting after prose writing. The evidence-collect skill takes loose files — screenshots, Burp exports, curl output, Nmap results — and organizes them into a canonical structure with a manifest.
5.1 Directory layout it produces
./findings/<slug>/evidence/
├── manifest.yml
├── screenshots/
│   ├── 001-login-page.png
│   ├── 002-post-injection.png
│   └── 003-shell-output.png
├── requests/
│   ├── 001-poc-request.http
│   └── 002-followup-request.http
├── responses/
│   ├── 001-poc-response.http
│   └── 002-followup-response.http
└── tool-output/
    ├── nmap-scan.txt
    ├── burp-scanner.xml
    └── curl-verify.log
5.2 Full SKILL.md
---
name: evidence-collect
description: |
  Organize raw evidence files for a finding into the canonical directory
  layout under ./findings/<slug>/evidence/ and write a manifest.yml
  describing every file, its source, and its content-hash.
inputs:
  slug:
    type: string
    required: true
  paths:
    type: array
    required: false
    description: "Additional evidence files to import; defaults to reading .pending-evidence."
allowed_tools: [Read, Write, Bash]
---

# evidence-collect

## Procedure

1. Read `./findings/<slug>/.pending-evidence` if it exists; combine with
   the paths input.

2. For each source path:
   a. Classify by extension and content:
      - .png, .jpg, .jpeg, .gif → screenshots/
      - .http, .req, .raw (or headers-then-body text) → requests/ or responses/
      - .xml, .txt, .log, .json → tool-output/
   b. Copy (do not move) into the target directory with a numbered prefix
      in insertion order.
   c. Compute the SHA-256 hash of the file.
   d. Record in manifest.yml:
        - name: 001-login-page.png
          source: ~/burp/login.png
          type: screenshot
          sha256: e3b0c4...
          added: 2026-09-05T14:22:03-03:00
          note: ""    # human-editable

3. If a Burp Suite session .zip or .xml is included, unpack it and split
   into request/response pairs.

4. Never delete or modify a source file. The pentester's original
   evidence remains untouched.

5. When finished, print the manifest so the human can add notes.

## Non-goals

- Do not redact secrets or PII automatically. That is `evidence-redact`
  (not part of this lesson).
- Do not embed evidence into Markdown. The card skill references paths.
5.3 Why hashes

The SHA-256 hash in the manifest is not decoration. It is a chain-of-custody control. If the client ever asks "is this the same screenshot you took on 2026-09-04?" you can produce the hash and prove the file has not been altered. Some regulated engagements (financial services, healthcare, government) require this level of evidence integrity.
6. Skill 5 — finding-card

The finding card is the client-facing representation of a single finding. It is a Markdown document that renders inside the final PDF as a self-contained section. The card skill converts the YAML into Markdown using a fixed template — no creative reinterpretation, just data-into-template substitution.
6.1 The Red Team Leaders card template
## <ID> — <Title>

**Severity:** <Severity> (CVSS 4.0 <Score> — `<Vector>`)
**Status:** <Status>
**Target:** `<Hostname>` (<IP>) — <URL>
**Discovered:** <Date> by <Tester>

### Description

<Description paragraph(s)>

### Impact

<Impact paragraph(s)>

### Evidence

**Screenshot — <caption from manifest note>**

![<caption>](evidence/screenshots/001-...png)

**Request:**

```http
<contents of requests/001-...http>

Response:
<contents of responses/001-...http, truncated to 40 lines>
References

    CVE: <linked CVE list, or "None assigned">
    OWASP Top 10 (2021):
    OWASP WSTG:
    MITRE ATT&CK:

Remediation

Short-term: <remediation.short>

Long-term: <remediation.detailed>

### 6.2 Full SKILL.md

```markdown
---
name: finding-card
description: |
  Render a finding YAML into a Markdown card following the Red Team
  Leaders template. Deterministic substitution — no rewriting, no
  paraphrasing, no smoothing.
inputs:
  slug:
    type: string
    required: true
  out:
    type: string
    required: false
    default: "./build/cards/<slug>.md"
allowed_tools: [Read, Write, Bash]
---

# finding-card

## Procedure

1. Read `./findings/<slug>/finding.yml`.
2. Read `./findings/<slug>/evidence/manifest.yml`.
3. Refuse to render if any of the following are missing or empty:
   - title, description, impact
   - cvss.vector, cvss.base_score, cvss.status == approved
   - remediation.short
4. Load the bundled template at `templates/finding-card.md.tmpl`.
5. Substitute fields using the Jinja-style tags in the template.
6. For screenshots, iterate the manifest in numeric order. For each,
   emit a caption line using manifest.note, then the image reference.
7. For requests/responses, include the first three pairs verbatim.
   Truncate responses over 40 lines with `... (truncated, N more lines)`.
8. Write to the output path (default `./build/cards/<slug>.md`).
9. Print the first 20 lines so the user can spot-check.

## Non-goals

- Do not rewrite the description. Do not "improve" the impact wording.
  The YAML is source of truth. If the prose is wrong, edit the YAML.
- Do not add references not present in the YAML.
- Do not compute or re-derive the severity from the score. Use what the
  YAML says. If they conflict, that is a bug the human must resolve.
6.3 Why refuse on missing fields

The refusal in step 3 is deliberate. If the CVSS is still pending-review, the card would render a client-facing document with an unreviewed score. If remediation.short is empty, the client is going to receive a finding with no fix guidance. The skill refuses instead of silently emitting a broken card. The tester sees the refusal and fills the gap.
7. Skill 6 — exec-summary

The executive summary is the section every executive reads and every tester dreads. It has to compress an entire engagement into three paragraphs of language that a non-technical CISO or COO can act on. The exec-summary skill drafts it from the findings YAMLs — with heavy caveats about human review.
7.1 Full SKILL.md
---
name: exec-summary
description: |
  Draft the three-paragraph executive summary for a report by reading
  every finding YAML under ./findings/ and synthesizing context, top
  risks, and recommended actions.
inputs:
  engagement:
    type: string
    required: true
    description: "The engagement name or ID."
  scope_notes:
    type: string
    required: false
    description: "Any scope context that should shape the summary."
allowed_tools: [Read, Write]
---

# exec-summary

## Procedure

1. Enumerate `./findings/*/finding.yml`.
2. Load each, extract: title, severity, base_score, target.asset_class,
   remediation.short.
3. Group by severity. Count each bucket.
4. Draft three paragraphs, in this fixed order:

   Paragraph 1 — Context.
   State the engagement name, the assessment window, and the scope in
   one or two sentences. Mention the number of assets tested and the
   number of findings by severity. This paragraph contains only facts
   drawn from the YAMLs and the scope_notes input; no interpretation.

   Paragraph 2 — Top risks.
   Name the top three findings by base_score (or by severity if scores
   tie). For each, state in one sentence what an attacker could do.
   Do not name the CVE. Do not name the tool. Use business language.

   Paragraph 3 — Recommended actions.
   Group findings by remediation theme (patching, configuration hardening,
   access-control review, monitoring). Recommend the top three themes
   in priority order. Estimated effort is not the skill's job — do not
   include time estimates.

5. Write to `./build/exec-summary.md`.

6. Mark the file with a front-matter header:
     ---
     drafted_by: exec-summary skill
     drafted_at: <ISO timestamp>
     review_status: pending
     ---
   The `review_status` field flips to `approved` only after a human
   edits and re-saves.

## Refusals

- If there are zero findings, do not draft a summary that says "no
  issues were identified". Refuse and ask the human what to write.
- If any finding has cvss.status != approved, warn but proceed with
  a note in the header.
7.2 Why three paragraphs

Three paragraphs is a Red Team Leaders house standard. Every summary the firm delivers, in every engagement, in every industry, is three paragraphs in the same order. Clients learn to skim them the same way. Auditors learn to find the facts in the same place. The tester does not get to decide the structure — the template does.
8. Skill 7 — report-assemble

The report-assemble skill is the orchestrator. It reads the cards, the executive summary, the methodology section, and the appendices, then produces a single Markdown file ready to be rendered.
8.1 The report skeleton
# <Engagement Name> — Penetration Test Report
| Prepared by Red Team Leaders | Confidential

## 1. Executive Summary          ← exec-summary output
## 2. Scope and Rules of Engagement  ← from engagement.yml
## 3. Methodology                ← from templates/methodology.md
## 4. Findings                   ← every finding-card, ordered by severity
## 5. Recommendations Summary    ← auto-generated from finding.remediation
## 6. Appendix A — Tools and Versions  ← from tool-inventory.yml
## 7. Appendix B — Evidence Manifest    ← flat list from every manifest
## 8. Appendix C — Retest Results      ← from retest blocks in YAMLs
8.2 Full SKILL.md
---
name: report-assemble
description: |
  Assemble every finding card, the executive summary, and the standard
  appendices into a single build/report.md Markdown file, ready for
  Typst rendering.
inputs:
  engagement:
    type: string
    required: true
  out:
    type: string
    required: false
    default: "./build/report.md"
allowed_tools: [Read, Write, Bash]
---

# report-assemble

## Procedure

1. Read `./engagement.yml` to get the engagement name, dates, scope,
   client contact, and rules of engagement.
2. Read `./build/exec-summary.md`. If review_status != approved, warn
   loudly but proceed.
3. Enumerate `./build/cards/*.md`. Sort by severity (critical → info),
   then by ID within each severity bucket.
4. Read `./templates/methodology.md`. This file is edited once per year
   and copied verbatim.
5. Read `./tool-inventory.yml`. Render Appendix A as a table.
6. Walk `./findings/*/evidence/manifest.yml`. Render Appendix B as one
   row per evidence file — slug, filename, sha256, source.
7. Walk retest blocks. Render Appendix C. If every retest.status is
   `not-yet-retested`, omit the appendix entirely.
8. Emit the report in section order to the output path.
9. Print the table of contents.

## Non-goals

- Do not modify any card. Cards are already rendered.
- Do not modify the executive summary. If it needs edits, the human
  edits the source and this skill is re-run.
8.3 The one-file principle

The report is one Markdown file. Not a directory of files that get glued together at PDF time — one file. This is a hard rule because it means the human can, at any point, less build/report.md and see exactly what the client will see. There is no invisible layer of composition happening at render time. What you see in the Markdown is what ships.
9. Skill 8 — typst-render

Typst is a modern typesetting system that produces PDFs from a simple, LaTeX-like source language. Red Team Leaders uses Typst rather than Word or LaTeX because the templates are diffable in git, the compile is deterministic and fast, and the output is production-grade typography. The typst-render skill runs typst compile against the bundled Red Team Leaders template.
9.1 The bundled template

The template lives at templates/rtl-report.typ and looks (in outline) like:
#import "components/cover.typ": cover
#import "components/section.typ": section
#import "components/finding.typ": finding-block

#let engagement = yaml("../engagement.yml")

#cover(
  client: engagement.client,
  engagement: engagement.name,
  window: engagement.window,
  version: engagement.version,
)

#include "../build/report.md"

The #include is where the assembled Markdown gets pulled in. Typst renders Markdown natively via its read and raw functions plus a small Markdown-to-Typst adapter shipped with the template.
9.2 Full SKILL.md
---
name: typst-render
description: |
  Compile the assembled report Markdown into a client-ready PDF using
  the Red Team Leaders Typst template.
inputs:
  engagement:
    type: string
    required: true
  out:
    type: string
    required: false
    default: "./build/report.pdf"
allowed_tools: [Read, Bash]
---

# typst-render

## Procedure

1. Verify `typst` is on PATH. If not, print the install command and abort.
2. Verify `./build/report.md` exists. If not, tell the user to run
   `report-assemble` first.
3. Run:
     typst compile templates/rtl-report.typ <out>
4. If compile fails, print the last 40 lines of stderr and abort.
5. On success, print:
     PDF written to <out> (<N> pages, <M> KB).
6. Optionally, run `pdfinfo <out>` and print the metadata for a
   sanity check.

## Non-goals

- Do not modify the Typst template. The template is a build artifact
  under change control; changes go through a pull request.
- Do not open the PDF. That is a human action.
9.3 Why Typst

Typst is chosen because the compile is reproducible byte-for-byte given the same inputs, the template is a small readable file that a non-designer can maintain, and the output is professionally typeset. LaTeX would work but has a much steeper maintenance cost. Word would work but is not diffable and not reproducible. Pandoc-driven Markdown-to-PDF would work but produces documents that look like Pandoc output, which is a giveaway that the report was auto-generated.
10. Deterministic vs LLM-Drafted Content

Every skill in the pipeline has a boundary between what the LLM drafts and what a deterministic computation produces. Cross-check your skill catalog against this table before you ship:
Content	Author	Reason
Finding title, description, impact	Human	Client-facing prose; tester's professional judgment
Finding remediation	Human	Requires knowledge of the client's tech stack
Finding YAML structure	Skill (finding-create)	Mechanical schema application
Slug and ID	Skill (deterministic)	Uniqueness and format enforcement
CVSS vector	LLM (cvss-score)	Reading English and mapping to metrics
CVSS base score	Deterministic (cvss4-score)	Arithmetic — LLMs get arithmetic wrong
CVE metadata	External source (NVD)	Ground truth, not model belief
OWASP / WSTG / ATT&CK mappings	Deterministic table match	Frozen ground-truth tables
Evidence file layout	Skill (evidence-collect)	Mechanical file operations
Evidence hashes	Deterministic (SHA-256)	Chain of custody
Finding card Markdown	Deterministic (template)	No creative variation across engagements
Executive summary paragraphs	LLM (exec-summary)	Synthesis of many YAMLs into prose
Report skeleton assembly	Deterministic (report-assemble)	Fixed section order
PDF layout	Deterministic (Typst template)	Reproducible typography

The pattern is: LLMs draft prose and interpret English; deterministic code enforces structure, computes numbers, and produces reproducible artifacts. Whenever you are tempted to let the LLM decide a number, a filename, or a schema field, back up and write the deterministic piece instead. This is not because the LLM is stupid — it is because the client needs a report they can trust six months from now, and reproducibility is what makes trust possible.
11. Human Review Gates

Every LLM-drafted paragraph in the pipeline has a review gate. The gate is enforced by a review_status field on the artifact:
review_status: pending    # LLM drafted, human has not yet reviewed
review_status: approved   # human has reviewed and signed off
review_status: rejected   # human rejected; artifact must be redrafted

Skills that consume a gated artifact behave differently based on the status:

    finding-card refuses to render if cvss.status is not approved.

    report-assemble warns but proceeds if exec-summary.review_status is not approved.

    typst-render prints a large banner in the PDF footer of any preview build where any gate is not approved. The banner reads:

        DRAFT — CONTAINS UNREVIEWED LLM-DRAFTED CONTENT. NOT FOR CLIENT DELIVERY.

The banner can only be removed by re-running typst-render --final, which itself refuses unless every gate is approved.
11.1 The sign-off flow

The sign-off flow is a one-line shell command:
rtl approve exec-summary
rtl approve finding <slug> cvss

This flips the status field, records the approver (from git config user.email) and timestamp, and writes an audit entry to ./audit.log. The audit log is committed to the engagement repo alongside the report and is retained as evidence that a qualified human reviewed every LLM-drafted paragraph.
11.2 Why this matters

The gate is not a paranoia measure. It is a legal and professional-liability measure. If the LLM drafts an executive summary that says "no critical findings were identified" and the tester ships it without reading — because they trust the pipeline — and it turns out one critical finding was missed, the pentester is on the hook, not the LLM. The gate makes the review step impossible to skip.
12. Style Guide Enforcement

Red Team Leaders enforces a house style. The style has three rules:

    No hedging language. "Might be vulnerable," "could potentially allow," "appears to be exposed" — these phrases are banned. The tester either confirmed the vulnerability or did not. If confirmed, state it. If not confirmed, remove the finding.
    No first person. "I found" becomes "the assessment identified." "We tested" becomes "testing was performed." The report is written from the assessment's perspective, not the tester's.
    No marketing filler. "State-of-the-art," "best-in-class," "leveraging synergy" — banned outright.

The style-lint skill enforces this:
---
name: style-lint
description: |
  Lint the assembled report against the Red Team Leaders house style
  guide. Flags hedging language, first-person voice, and forbidden
  marketing phrases. Refuses to emit a final PDF while violations exist.
inputs:
  path:
    type: string
    required: false
    default: "./build/report.md"
allowed_tools: [Read, Bash]
---

# style-lint

## Procedure

1. Read the target file.

2. Scan for forbidden patterns using the bundled `style/forbidden.yml`:

     hedging:
       - "might be"
       - "may be vulnerable"
       - "could potentially"
       - "appears to"
       - "seems to"
       - "possibly"
     first_person:
       - "\\bI \\b"
       - "\\bwe \\b"
       - "\\bour \\b"
       - "\\bmy \\b"
     marketing:
       - "state-of-the-art"
       - "best-in-class"
       - "leveraging"
       - "synergy"
       - "cutting-edge"
       - "world-class"

3. For each hit, print the line number, the matched phrase, and a
   suggested rewrite.

4. Exit non-zero if any hit was found. `typst-render --final` gates on
   this exit code.

The lint is intentionally strict — it will occasionally flag a legitimate use of "we" (for example, in a quoted CVE description). The tester deals with false positives by rewriting or by adding a <!-- style-lint: allow-line --> marker.
13. Full Workflow — YAML to PDF in One Session

Here is what the end-to-end pipeline looks like from a tester's chair, using six of the skills in one Claude Code session. The tester has just finished a two-week engagement and has raw notes, screenshots, and Burp exports in ~/engagement-notes/.
13.1 The session
User: I finished the ACME Corp engagement. I have twelve findings in
      ~/engagement-notes/. Let's build the report.

Claude: Sure. Let's start by importing each finding. Paste the first
        one, or point me at a file listing the titles and descriptions.

User: (pastes finding 1 — a critical Apache Struts RCE)

Claude: (invokes finding-create)
  → Created RTL-2026-0042 at findings/apache-struts-rce-on-erp/finding.yml
  → Pending evidence recorded from ~/engagement-notes/struts-*.zip

User: (pastes finding 2, 3, ..., 12)

Claude: (invokes finding-create eleven more times)
  → Created RTL-2026-0043 through RTL-2026-0053.

User: Score them all.

Claude: (invokes cvss-score for each slug)
  → 12 findings scored. All marked pending-review.
  → Summary: 2 critical, 4 high, 5 medium, 1 low.

User: Enrich them.

Claude: (invokes finding-enrich for each slug)
  → CVE metadata attached where available (3 CVEs resolved).
  → OWASP Top 10 categories attached to all findings.
  → WSTG test IDs attached to 10 findings.
  → MITRE ATT&CK techniques attached to all findings.

User: Organize evidence.

Claude: (invokes evidence-collect for each slug)
  → Evidence for 12 findings organized. Manifests written.

User: I'll review the CVSS vectors now.

(User opens each finding.yml, reviews the vector and rationale, edits
where needed, and runs `rtl approve finding <slug> cvss` for each.)

User: Approvals done. Draft cards.

Claude: (invokes finding-card for each slug)
  → 12 cards written to build/cards/.

User: Draft the executive summary.

Claude: (invokes exec-summary)
  → build/exec-summary.md written. review_status: pending.

(User reads the summary, edits paragraph two to reflect the client's
industry context, and runs `rtl approve exec-summary`.)

User: Assemble the report.

Claude: (invokes report-assemble)
  → build/report.md written. 87 sections, 6 appendices.

User: Lint it.

Claude: (invokes style-lint)
  → 3 hedging violations found in finding cards. Fix and re-run.

(User edits three YAML descriptions to remove "may be" and "appears to",
re-runs finding-card for those slugs, re-runs report-assemble, re-runs
style-lint. Clean.)

User: Render final.

Claude: (invokes typst-render --final)
  → build/report.pdf written. 64 pages, 2.1 MB.
  → No draft banner emitted (all gates approved).

User: Ship it.

That entire session, end to end, is roughly one billable hour of the tester's time — most of it spent reviewing LLM drafts and approving gates, not writing prose. The prose-writing work that used to consume five days is compressed into that hour of review.
13.2 What did not happen

Notice what the pipeline did not do:

    It did not invent CVE numbers.
    It did not compute CVSS scores in the LLM's head.
    It did not decide on its own whether the executive summary was good enough to ship.
    It did not skip a human review gate.
    It did not deviate from the Red Team Leaders template.

Every one of those is a temptation. Every one of those is exactly what makes AI-assisted reports untrustworthy when the boundary is drawn wrong. The pipeline is worth building precisely because it forces the boundary to be drawn right.
14. Exercises
14.1 Guided Exercises
