Exercise 1 — Build finding-create and dry-run it.

Copy the SKILL.md above into ~/.claude/skills/finding-create/SKILL.md. In a scratch directory, run:
User: Log a new finding — reflected XSS in the search parameter of
      https://juice-shop.local/#/search, discovered via Burp scanner.

Verify that the resulting findings/reflected-xss-in-the-search-parameter/finding.yml contains:

    An ID of the form RTL-2026-0001.
    Empty cvss, references, and evidence blocks.
    A discovered.date matching today.
    A non-empty target.url equal to the URL you provided.

Now try the same command a second time. The skill should refuse rather than overwrite.

Exercise 2 — Score a finding and verify the deterministic boundary.

Using the finding from Exercise 1, ask Claude to invoke cvss-score. Read the resulting cvss.vector and cvss.base_score fields. Now edit cvss.vector by hand to change AV:N to AV:L, save the file, and re-invoke cvss-score with the --recompute-only flag (which you will need to add to the skill).

    Confirm that the base_score changes accordingly.
    Confirm that the rationale is regenerated to reflect the new AV.
    Confirm that a completely invalid vector (delete the AC:L chunk) causes the skill to refuse rather than emit a random score.

Exercise 3 — Full pipeline on a two-finding engagement.

Pick two vulnerabilities from a lab environment you already have — DVWA, Juice Shop, HackTheBox retired boxes, anything you have written permission to test. Take real screenshots, capture real requests. Then walk through the entire pipeline:

    finding-create for each finding.
    cvss-score for each.
    finding-enrich for each.
    evidence-collect for each.
    Approve gates.
    finding-card for each.
    exec-summary.
    Approve exec-summary gate.
    report-assemble.
    style-lint.
    typst-render --final.

Produce the PDF. Read it start to finish. Note every place where the output does not feel client-ready and identify whether the fix belongs in a skill, a template, or the human review step.
14.2 Independent Exercises

Exercise 4 — Design and build the evidence-redact skill.

Every real engagement produces evidence that contains data the tester should not ship to the client — password hashes captured mid-exploit, PII exposed in error messages, session tokens in request headers. Design a skill that scans evidence files for well-known patterns (session cookies, JWTs, hex-encoded hashes, email addresses, national ID numbers for your locale) and produces redacted copies alongside the originals, updating the manifest to reference the redacted versions. Decide what patterns are in scope. Decide how false positives are handled (a redaction that removes something the tester needed to keep). Decide how the human approves each redaction before the redacted copy is used in a card.

Exercise 5 — Design the retest workflow.

Real engagements often include a retest — the tester returns 30 to 90 days later, tries the same attacks against the fixed system, and reports what was actually fixed. Extend the schema, add a retest-run skill that walks the findings, prompts the human for each, and updates the retest block. Extend report-assemble to render Appendix C. Extend typst-render to add a "Retest Summary" section on the cover if any retest data is present.

Exercise 6 — Multi-language style linting.

Red Team Leaders delivers reports in English, Portuguese, and Spanish. Extend the style-lint skill to handle all three languages with per-language forbidden-pattern tables. Decide how the language is detected (a language field in engagement.yml, or content sniffing). Decide how mixed-language reports are handled (a client that wants the executive summary in Portuguese but findings in English).
15. Summary and Next Lesson

You now have the design for an eight-skill report generation pipeline that reads YAML, enriches with external intelligence, applies deterministic templates, drafts prose only where prose actually helps, gates every LLM output on human review, and produces a client-ready PDF via Typst. You have seen where deterministic code belongs, where LLM drafting belongs, and why the boundary is not negotiable if the report has to hold up in a client audit six months from delivery.

The pipeline you built in this lesson operates on findings that already exist. Lesson 06 shifts to the other end of the workflow: skills that help you find the findings in the first place. You will build recon-*, web-triage, and smb-triage skills that turn raw target information into structured leads for manual investigation. Those skills feed the reporting pipeline you built here — the two lessons compose into a full assessment workflow.

Continue to Lesson 06 — Building Reconnaissance Skills.

































Lesson 06 — Codex CLI for Security Automation

    Module 06: Building Pentest Skills with Claude and Codex · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

Claude Code is not the only autonomous coding agent that belongs in a red teamer's toolbox. OpenAI's Codex CLI — released in April 2025 and steadily hardened through 2026 — has become the second half of the modern pentesting AI stack. Where Claude Code excels at long-context exploration, structured planning, and MCP-driven tool chaining, Codex CLI is the lean, batch-friendly agent you reach for when you need to run the same reasoning across two hundred targets without spending half a day writing custom orchestration.

The two tools are complementary. Claude Code is the surgeon: careful, verbose, deliberate. Codex CLI is the field medic: fast, non-interactive, opinionated about its sandbox, cheap to invoke at scale. In a full engagement you will almost certainly use both — often against the same data, at different phases.

This lesson walks through installing Codex CLI on Kali 2026, understanding how it differs from Claude Code at the mechanical level, wiring it up for pentest work, and building a real batch-triage script that pipes nuclei findings through the agent for LLM-assisted validation. By the end of the lesson you will have a working codex-triage.sh on disk, understand exactly when to reach for Codex versus Claude, and know how to run one shared MCP server behind both agents.

Everything in this lesson uses the Codex CLI syntax current as of 2026-09. Codex has been moving quickly; if a command flag looks different from a blog post from 2025, trust the --help output on your box first.
1. What Codex CLI Is (and Is Not)

Codex CLI is OpenAI's open-source autonomous coding agent that runs entirely on your workstation. The binary is a Rust process (codex) that ships an agent loop, a sandbox, a small MCP client, and a set of built-in tools for reading and writing files and running shell commands. It talks to an OpenAI model — by default GPT-5.1-Codex — through your ChatGPT account or an API key.

Concretely:

    Codex CLI is the on-disk tool: codex, invoked from the terminal.
    Codex (the model) is what powers it: OpenAI's coding-tuned GPT family (gpt-5.1-codex, gpt-5.1-codex-mini, and so on).
    ChatGPT Codex is a separate cloud-hosted product; not what we care about here.

Codex CLI is a peer to Claude Code, not a replacement. Its design goals are:

    Run locally, in your terminal, with a real sandbox.
    Be scriptable — codex exec "<prompt>" is a first-class citizen.
    Speak MCP so external tools can be reused across agents.
    Stay opinionated: fewer knobs, saner defaults.

It is not a general-purpose chatbot, and it is not a fully sandboxed remote agent — it runs as your user, with whatever privileges you have. Treat it accordingly during red team work.
2. Installing Codex on Kali 2026

Codex CLI ships as an npm package. On Kali 2026 the recommended path is Node.js 20 LTS or 22 LTS from the nodejs package.
2.1 Prerequisites
# Verify Node.js is present and modern enough:
node --version
# v22.9.0

# If not present, install from the Debian repo:
sudo apt update
sudo apt install -y nodejs npm

# Codex needs Node 20+. If Kali's default is too old, use nvm:
curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nvm-sh/nvm/v0.40.1/install.sh | bash
source ~/.bashrc
nvm install --lts
nvm use --lts
2.2 Install Codex CLI
# Install globally:
sudo npm install -g @openai/codex

# Verify the install:
codex --version
# codex-cli 0.42.0

which codex
# /usr/local/bin/codex

If you prefer a per-user install (recommended so sudo is not part of your daily flow):
mkdir -p ~/.npm-global
npm config set prefix "$HOME/.npm-global"
export PATH="$HOME/.npm-global/bin:$PATH"
echo 'export PATH="$HOME/.npm-global/bin:$PATH"' >> ~/.bashrc

npm install -g @openai/codex
codex --version
2.3 Authenticate

Codex supports two authentication modes:

    ChatGPT account (browser-based login; uses your existing plan).
    API key (OPENAI_API_KEY environment variable; charged per-token).

For a pentest workstation the API key model is usually simpler because it doesn't tie the box to a personal ChatGPT session.
# Interactive login (opens a browser tab and pastes the token back):
codex login

# Or set an API key in your shell profile:
export OPENAI_API_KEY="sk-proj-..."
echo 'export OPENAI_API_KEY="sk-proj-..."' >> ~/.bashrc

# Sanity check:
codex exec "print hello world in python" --output-last-message /tmp/hello.txt
cat /tmp/hello.txt

    Pentester Insight: On engagement laptops, put your API key in a file readable only by your user (chmod 600 ~/.codex/auth.json or a systemd-creds vault). If a target's SOC ever pulls your workstation into evidence, an unencrypted key in your shell history is an embarrassing finding — and worse, it may still be valid.

2.4 Kali-Specific Notes

    Kali 2026's Rolling repo already has Node 22 LTS; you rarely need nvm.
    Codex CLI's sandbox uses Linux namespaces (unshare, landlock) — Kali kernels support these fine, but if you are running Codex inside a Docker container you must pass --cap-add=SYS_ADMIN or drop to --sandbox danger-full-access.
    The default cache lives at ~/.codex/cache; keep it off shared network mounts, it does a lot of small writes.

3. Comparing Codex CLI to Claude Code

The two agents look similar from thirty thousand feet — both are terminal-native coding agents driven by an autoregressive model over a tool loop. Up close, they differ in ways that matter for pentest work.
3.1 Side-by-Side
Dimension	Claude Code	Codex CLI
Model	Claude Opus/Sonnet/Haiku	GPT-5.1-Codex family
Language of the CLI	TypeScript (Node)	Rust binary
Agent loop	Managed by SDK, hidden from user	Explicit turn/step display
Sandbox	Filesystem allow-list, opt-in bash	Landlock/seccomp filesystem + network toggle
MCP client	First-class, transport-rich	First-class, stdio + HTTP
Tool set	Read, Write, Edit, Bash, Grep, Glob, Task, WebFetch	shell, apply_patch, read_file, write_file, mcp
Approval model	Permissions per tool + skill overrides	on-request / on-failure / never
Non-interactive mode	claude -p "..." (print)	codex exec "..." (exec)
Resume	Session-based, project-scoped	codex --resume <session-id>
Config format	JSON (~/.claude/settings.json)	TOML (~/.codex/config.toml)
Default streaming	Rich TUI with tool cards	Compact turn log
3.2 What This Means in Practice

    Long, exploratory tasks (map a huge codebase, plan a full engagement) — Claude Code, because the UX is richer and context management is more forgiving.
    Batch reasoning (triage 200 findings, run the same audit on 50 subdomains) — Codex CLI, because codex exec is fast and returns cleanly.
    Structured plans with lots of subtasks — Claude Code's Task tool + skills is unmatched.
    Tight loops with shell tools — Codex, because its default sandbox is easier to reason about and its exec mode has no interactive drift.
    Anywhere you want MCP — either, and you can point both at the same MCP server.

3.3 The Sandbox Difference

Claude Code's sandbox is a policy layer around a permission system: it will ask (or refuse) based on your allow/deny lists. Codex CLI's sandbox is a mechanism layer around Linux namespaces: files outside the workspace are literally not visible to the child processes it spawns, and the network is off by default unless the mode says otherwise.

Both approaches are valid. Claude Code's is more flexible; Codex CLI's is harder to bypass, which is why many red teamers reach for Codex when they want to run a suspect script against a payload.
4. Configuration — ~/.codex/config.toml

Codex CLI reads configuration from ~/.codex/config.toml. It is created on first run; the schema is stable but small.
4.1 A Real Working Config
# ~/.codex/config.toml

# Default model for all `codex` invocations.
model = "gpt-5.1-codex"

# Reasoning effort. One of: minimal, low, medium, high.
model_reasoning_effort = "medium"

# Default sandbox for the interactive REPL.
sandbox_mode = "workspace-write"

# Default approval policy for the interactive REPL.
approval_policy = "on-request"

# Where transcripts and rollout files live.
history_path = "~/.codex/history"

# Auto-compact context when it approaches the model window.
auto_compact = true

# Optional: pin a different model for `codex exec`.
[profiles.exec
]
model = "gpt-5.1-codex-mini"
model_reasoning_effort = "low"
sandbox_mode = "read-only"
approval_policy = "never"

# Optional: a heavy profile for deep review work.
[profiles.review
]
model = "gpt-5.1-codex"
model_reasoning_effort = "high"
sandbox_mode = "workspace-write"
approval_policy = "on-request"

# MCP servers Codex should attach on start-up.
[mcp_servers.nuclei
]
command = "/opt/mcp-servers/nuclei-mcp/target/release/nuclei-mcp"
args = []
env = { NUCLEI_TEMPLATES = "/opt/nuclei-templates" }

[mcp_servers.burp
]
command = "node"
args = ["/opt/mcp-servers/burp-mcp/dist/index.js"]

# Shell customisation.
[shell
]
# The wrapper Codex uses to spawn child commands inside the sandbox.
# Default is fine; override only if you have unusual PATH needs.
default_timeout_ms = 120000
4.2 Selecting a Profile

Profiles are selected with --profile:
codex --profile review
codex exec --profile exec "summarise diff between main and feature/x"

Profiles compose over the top-level defaults, so a profile only needs to name what it changes.
4.3 Environment Overrides

Any config key can be overridden per-invocation:
codex -c model="gpt-5.1-codex-mini" -c sandbox_mode="read-only" exec "..."

The -c key=value form takes TOML values, so quote strings.
5. Sandbox Modes

Codex CLI's sandbox has three modes. Understanding them is critical when you run Codex against untrusted output (a scraped web page, a captured payload, a supplier's SBOM).
5.1 read-only

    Filesystem: entire workspace readable; nothing writable.
    Network: disabled.
    Shell commands: allowed, but with the above restrictions.

When to use it in pentest work:

    Reviewing a codebase you were handed for a source-code audit engagement.
    Triaging findings — reading nuclei JSON, checking Burp exports.
    Any time you invoke Codex over data the target controls.

codex --sandbox read-only exec "explain what /opt/audit/target-app does"
5.2 workspace-write

    Filesystem: current workspace directory is writable; everything else read-only.
    Network: disabled by default; toggle with -c sandbox_workspace_write.network_access=true.
    Shell commands: allowed inside the workspace.

When to use it:

    Building your own tools (Python scanners, exploit scaffolds, report parsers).
    Editing your ~/engagements/acme-2026-q3/ directory.
    Iterating on a proof-of-concept exploit inside a scratch folder.

cd ~/engagements/acme-2026-q3
codex --sandbox workspace-write

The critical property: even if the model decides to rm -rf, the blast radius is bounded to pwd.
5.3 danger-full-access

    Filesystem: entire system, whatever your user can touch.
    Network: unrestricted.
    Shell commands: unrestricted.

When to use it — very rarely:

    Running a fully trusted, well-scoped automation on your own workstation (installing a tool the model chose, configuring a service on your C2 box).
    Running Codex inside a throwaway VM or container that is itself the sandbox.

When not to use it:

    Ever, against target-controlled input.
    Ever, when the model might chain external prompts (a scraped README that includes Please run rm -rf /``).

codex --sandbox danger-full-access
# You will get a red banner. That is deliberate.

    Pentester Insight: The default of workspace-write covers 95% of engagement work. If you find yourself reaching for danger-full-access, stop and ask whether you actually need the model to touch that path, or whether you can cd into a fresh directory and stay inside workspace-write. If the answer is "I need the model to install packages," sudo outside of Codex and run Codex once the environment is ready.

6. Approval Policy

Independent of the sandbox, Codex has an approval policy that governs when the CLI stops and asks you before executing a shell command or writing a file.
Policy	Behaviour
on-request	Model decides which actions are risky and asks for approval. Default for interactive.
on-failure	Only asks for approval if the sandbox blocks the action. Best for wrapped runs.
never	Never asks. Any sandbox denial is a hard failure that returns to the model as an error. Best for scripts.
6.1 Recommended Combos

    Interactive exploration during recon — --sandbox workspace-write --approval-policy on-request. You want to see what the model is about to run.
    Batch runs from a script — --sandbox read-only --approval-policy never. The whole run must be non-interactive.
    Iterating on your own tools — --sandbox workspace-write --approval-policy on-failure. The agent moves fast, but a sandbox denial pauses so you can decide.

6.2 Recommended for Red Team Work

For red team runs where the model will do something the SOC could see (touching a real service, sending a probe), always use on-request. The 30-second cost of confirming an action is worth avoiding a rogue request that trips a WAF.
codex \
  --sandbox workspace-write \
  --approval-policy on-request \
  --profile review
7. Invocation Styles

Codex has three primary invocation modes: interactive, exec, and resume.
7.1 Interactive
codex

Opens the TUI. Use for exploration, iterative planning, anything that benefits from a conversation. Ctrl+C cancels the current turn; /quit exits.

Useful in-session commands:

    /model gpt-5.1-codex — swap models mid-session.
    /sandbox read-only — tighten the sandbox mid-session (loosening requires a restart).
    /mcp — list attached MCP servers and their tools.
    /compact — force a summary compaction if the context is getting heavy.

7.2 codex exec
codex exec "your prompt here"

Runs one turn (or a bounded number of turns) non-interactively, streams tool activity to stderr, prints the final assistant message to stdout, and exits. This is the mode you script.

Flags that matter for automation:

    --output-last-message <path> — writes only the final assistant message to a file.
    --json — emits structured JSON events for every step.
    --max-turns <n> — hard cap on tool-use turns.
    --profile <name> — pick a config profile.
    -C <dir> — cd into a workspace before running.
    --include-plan — force the model to emit a plan block.

Example:
codex exec \
  --profile exec \
  --json \
  --max-turns 6 \
  --output-last-message /tmp/last.txt \
  "read ./target.txt, summarise the top three findings as bullet points"
7.3 codex --resume

Every Codex session is saved as a rollout file under ~/.codex/history/. You can resume by session id:
codex resume --last
codex resume 018f9c7c-2c3d-7f0a-8e2b-71e2be7b32aa

For batch work you rarely resume — you just start fresh. But when you were in a long interactive session and got disconnected, codex resume --last gets you back exactly where you were.
8. Reading and Writing Files in Codex — Its Tool Set

Codex CLI ships with a small, opinionated tool set. Every tool respects the current sandbox and approval policy.
8.1 Built-in Tools
Tool	Purpose	Notes
shell	Run a shell command	Time-bounded; captured stdout/stderr returned to the model
read_file	Read a file by path	Respects read-only/workspace boundaries
write_file	Overwrite or create a file	Refused outside workspace unless danger-full-access
apply_patch	Apply a unified diff	Preferred over write_file for edits; safer
list_dir	List directory	Cheap; used for orientation
search	ripgrep-like search across the workspace	Faster than launching rg via shell
mcp	Call an MCP-provided tool	Namespaced per MCP server
8.2 How Codex Decides Which Tool to Use

The model is trained to prefer apply_patch for edits over full-file writes; to use search before falling back to shell rg; and to use read_file rather than shell cat. These are strong preferences but not hard rules — you can nudge them with prompts:
Always use apply_patch for edits. Do not use shell to modify files.

Include that line in a project's CODEX.md (Codex's equivalent of CLAUDE.md) and it will be pre-loaded into every session started in that directory.
8.3 The shell Tool in Detail

shell is the tool you spend the most time thinking about because it is the one the sandbox constrains most heavily.

    Commands run under bash -lc.
    Working directory is the workspace root by default; the model can cd.
    The command is executed inside a Linux user namespace with landlock rules matching the sandbox mode.
    Network syscalls are dropped in read-only and workspace-write (unless overridden).
    Output is capped at ~200 KB per invocation to protect the context.

If a command exceeds the output cap, Codex truncates and tells the model. If a command exceeds the default 120-second timeout, Codex kills it. Both limits are configurable per-invocation with -c shell.default_timeout_ms=....
8.4 CODEX.md

Just as Claude Code reads CLAUDE.md, Codex reads CODEX.md (project-level) and ~/.codex/CODEX.md (user-level). A good pentest project CODEX.md looks like:
# Codex Instructions for Engagement acme-2026-q3

## Scope
- In-scope: *.acme.corp, *.acme-labs.com
- Out-of-scope: EVERYTHING else. Do not touch third-party domains.

## Conventions
- Use `apply_patch` for edits.
- Save findings under `./findings/<host>/<yyyy-mm-dd>.md`.
- All shell commands must be idempotent; do not send probes that alter state.

## Tools
- nuclei templates live at /opt/nuclei-templates
- burp state file: ./burp/state.burp
- do NOT run active tools without the `--target-in-scope` guard script
9. Building an Automation Loop

Now the fun part. Here is a bash wrapper that runs codex exec over each of 200 targets and collects JSON output.
9.1 Requirements

    Input: targets.txt, one hostname per line.
    Output: results.jsonl, one JSON object per target with fields {target, verdict, evidence, model, ts}.
    Constraints: never touch a target outside the file; hard 60-second timeout per invocation; parallelism configurable.

9.2 The Wrapper: codex-batch.sh
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# codex-batch.sh — run codex exec against a list of targets in parallel.
#
# Usage: ./codex-batch.sh targets.txt results.jsonl [PARALLELISM]

set -euo pipefail

TARGETS="${1:?missing targets file}"
OUT="${2:?missing output file}"
PAR="${3:-4}"

: > "$OUT"

# The prompt we send for every target. Keep it stable so the model's
# JSON schema does not drift between invocations.
read -r -d '' PROMPT <<'PROMPT_EOF' || true
You are a triage assistant. Given a hostname, run non-invasive
recon (DNS, whois, HTTP HEAD to /) and decide whether it looks
in-scope for an engagement against acme.corp.

Respond with EXACTLY one JSON object on stdout, nothing else:
{"target": "<hostname>", "verdict": "in-scope"|"out-of-scope"|"unknown",
 "evidence": "<one-sentence rationale>"}
PROMPT_EOF

# Function invoked per-target in parallel.
run_one() {
    local host="$1"
    local tmp
    tmp="$(mktemp)"

    # Bound the whole run to 60s.
    if timeout 60 codex exec \
            --profile exec \
            --json \
            --sandbox workspace-write \
            --approval-policy never \
            --max-turns 8 \
            --output-last-message "$tmp" \
            "$(printf '%s\n\nTarget: %s' "$PROMPT" "$host")" \
            >/dev/null 2>&1; then

        local last
        last="$(cat "$tmp")"

        # Enforce the JSON schema. If the model wandered off, mark unknown.
        if jq -e '.target and .verdict and .evidence' \
                <<<"$last" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
            jq -c \
                --arg model "gpt-5.1-codex-mini" \
                --arg ts "$(date -Is)" \
                '. + {model: $model, ts: $ts}' \
                <<<"$last" >> "$OUT"
        else
            jq -cn \
                --arg t "$host" \
                --arg ev "codex returned non-JSON: $last" \
                --arg ts "$(date -Is)" \
                '{target:$t,verdict:"unknown",evidence:$ev,ts:$ts}' >> "$OUT"
        fi
    else
        jq -cn \
            --arg t "$host" \
            --arg ts "$(date -Is)" \
            '{target:$t,verdict:"unknown",evidence:"timeout or codex error",ts:$ts}' >> "$OUT"
    fi

    rm -f "$tmp"
}

export -f run_one
export PROMPT

# Parallel fan-out via xargs.
< "$TARGETS" xargs -I{} -P "$PAR" bash -c 'run_one "$@"' _ {}

echo "done. wrote $(wc -l < "$OUT") lines to $OUT"
9.3 Running It
chmod +x codex-batch.sh

# 200 targets, 8 in parallel:
./codex-batch.sh targets.txt results.jsonl 8

# Inspect:
jq -s 'group_by(.verdict) | map({verdict: .[0].verdict, n: length})' results.jsonl
# [
#   {"verdict":"in-scope","n":142},
#   {"verdict":"out-of-scope","n":51},
#   {"verdict":"unknown","n":7}
# ]
9.4 Notes on Robustness

    Always wrap codex exec in timeout. The model can loop, and a stalled run wastes both money and calendar time.
    Always validate the JSON with jq -e. Codex is much more disciplined than 2023-era models, but it still occasionally sneaks a chatty prefix. The wrapper enforces schema.
    --approval-policy never is safe only because --sandbox workspace-write bounds writes to pwd. Do not remove one without the other.
    xargs -P is good enough up to about 16 workers per box. Beyond that you will hit the OpenAI rate limits and want a proper queue.

10. MCP Servers in Codex

The single biggest advantage of running both Claude Code and Codex CLI is that they can share MCP servers. You write a nuclei MCP once and both agents can call it.
10.1 Configuration

MCP servers go under [mcp_servers.<name>] in ~/.codex/config.toml:
[mcp_servers.nuclei
]
command = "/opt/mcp-servers/nuclei-mcp/target/release/nuclei-mcp"
args = ["--templates", "/opt/nuclei-templates"]
env = { RUST_LOG = "info" }

[mcp_servers.pentest-toolkit
]
command = "node"
args = ["/opt/mcp-servers/pentest-toolkit/dist/index.js"]

# HTTP transport is also supported for remote servers:
[mcp_servers.corp-scanner
]
url = "https://mcp.internal.redteamleaders.example/scanner"
headers = { Authorization = "Bearer $MCP_CORP_TOKEN" }
10.2 Verifying MCP Attachment
codex
> /mcp
# nuclei          9 tools
# pentest-toolkit 14 tools
# corp-scanner    3 tools

> /mcp nuclei
# nuclei:run(target: string, tags?: string[])
# nuclei:validate_template(path: string)
# ...
10.3 Using the Same MCP from Claude Code

In ~/.claude/settings.json:
{
  "mcp": {
    "servers": {
      "nuclei": {
        "command": "/opt/mcp-servers/nuclei-mcp/target/release/nuclei-mcp",
        "args": ["--templates", "/opt/nuclei-templates"]
      }
    }
  }
}

Same binary, same tools, same behaviour. The advantage of centralising your pentest MCPs (nuclei, ffuf, burp, katana, custom recon scripts) is enormous: you build the tool once and it lifts your effective tool set in both agents.

    Pentester Insight: During long engagements the MCP server itself becomes a canonical audit log. Every tool call is logged with arguments; you can go back at report-writing time and reconstruct exactly which probes fired against which targets. This is worth more than gold when the client asks whether you triggered an alarm at 03:47 on day three.

11. Comparing Prompt Styles

Claude and Codex are both good at following instructions but they respond to different prompt shapes.
11.1 What Claude Prefers

Claude models — especially Sonnet and Opus — respond exceptionally well to:

    Explicit XML tags for structured input (<scope>...</scope>, <findings>...</findings>).
    Numbered plans they can check off.
    Multi-example few-shot with negative examples.

Example:
<task>
Triage the finding below. Decide whether it is a real vulnerability
or a false positive.
</task>

<finding>
CVE-2024-12345 on https://acme.corp/api/v2/users
</finding>

<criteria>
1. Is the endpoint actually reachable?
2. Does the vulnerable version really run there?
3. Is authentication required?
</criteria>

<output_schema>
{"real": bool, "confidence": "low"|"medium"|"high", "notes": "..."}
</output_schema>
11.2 What Codex Prefers

Codex GPT-5.1 models respond better to:

    Terse, imperative instructions. "Do X. Then Y. Return Z."
    JSON schemas expressed as JSON, not as prose.
    Fewer roleplay framings. "You are a triage assistant" is fine; a paragraph of persona is noise.
    Explicit tool preferences ("use apply_patch, not shell").

Example:
Triage this finding.

Input:
{"cve":"CVE-2024-12345","url":"https://acme.corp/api/v2/users"}

Steps:
1. HEAD the URL.
2. Read the server header.
3. Compare against known vulnerable versions.

Output (exact schema, nothing else):
{"real": true|false, "confidence": "low|medium|high", "notes": "..."}
11.3 A Rule of Thumb

    Copy a Claude prompt into Codex and it will work, but you are burning tokens.
    Copy a Codex prompt into Claude and it may work, but Claude will keep asking clarifying questions.

Maintain two prompt libraries. It is not a lot of work — most prompts differ only in framing.
12. Using Codex for Tool-Building Sprints

Where Codex shines during an engagement is tool-building sprints: the moments where you need a scanner, parser, or filter for the client's environment and you need it in the next hour, not the next day.
12.1 The Pattern

    cd ~/engagements/acme/tools/
    codex --sandbox workspace-write --approval-policy on-request
    Ask for a Python module skeleton with the exact CLI you want.
    Iterate on it, running the tool against a sample after every change.
    Once it works, ask for tests, then ask for a README.
    Commit.

12.2 A Real Example

Prompt:
Build me a Python 3.11 CLI called `acme-cookie-audit` that:

- Takes a URL and a cookie name.
- Fetches the URL with httpx, follows redirects.
- Extracts the named cookie from the Set-Cookie header.
- Decodes it if it looks base64 or hex; parses it as JWT if it looks like one.
- Prints a structured report to stdout as JSON.

Use `click`. Add unit tests with pytest and one integration test that hits httpbin.org.
Do not use requests. Do not add async unless needed.

Codex will produce a working acme_cookie_audit/ package in about 30 seconds, run the tests, and iterate until they pass. When it stops you have a real, tested CLI to drop into the engagement toolbox.
12.3 When Not to Use Codex for This

    Anything that must be memory-safe and audited by hand (custom shellcode loaders, exploit primitives). Codex writes the scaffolding, you hand-verify the guts.
    Anything with hard performance requirements. The model tends to reach for the readable choice, not the fastest one.

13. Cost Management

Codex CLI is not free. Every turn is a model call, and long agent loops can chew through tokens quickly. Some habits that keep the bill sane during an engagement:
13.1 Pick the Right Model

    gpt-5.1-codex — reasoning workhorse; use for review, planning, complex tool-building.
    gpt-5.1-codex-mini — fast and cheap; use for batch triage, classification, non-invasive recon.
    gpt-5.1-codex-nano (where available in your region) — cheapest; use for shape-only tasks (JSON reformatting, one-shot yes/no).

Set the default in ~/.codex/config.toml and override per-profile.
13.2 Bound Turns

--max-turns 6 costs a fraction of --max-turns 30. For batch jobs, cap turns to the smallest value the task tolerates.
13.3 Use Prompt Caching

Codex CLI supports OpenAI's prompt caching for the static parts of a system prompt. Put your long, stable preamble at the top of every prompt and the model will bill it at the cached rate on repeated calls within the cache window.

For the batch wrapper in section 9, this means the shared PROMPT variable is (mostly) cached across the 200 invocations. You still pay for the target-specific tail, but the bulk of the prompt is essentially free after the first call.
13.4 Watch the Meter
codex usage --today
codex usage --this-month --profile exec

The usage subcommand reports rolled-up token counts pulled from your local rollout logs. Handy for engagement accounting.
13.5 A Budget Discipline

Give each engagement a token budget upfront. If you spend $50 of API credit on triage, that is money well spent. If you spend $500, something has gone wrong — probably an unbounded loop or a chatty prompt. Set an alert in your OpenAI dashboard and treat crossing it as a stop-the-line event.
14. Full Working Example — codex-triage.sh

This is the payoff. A wrapper that takes a nuclei JSONL output, batches each finding through Codex CLI for LLM triage, and writes a decorated JSONL back out.
14.1 What It Does

    Reads nuclei.jsonl (one finding per line, the default nuclei -jsonl schema).
    For each finding, asks Codex to decide: real, likely-fp, or needs-manual.
    Writes nuclei-triaged.jsonl with the original object plus a triage field.
    Skips findings the model has already seen (dedup by nuclei's template-id + matched-at).

14.2 The Script
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# codex-triage.sh — LLM-triage a nuclei JSONL file.
#
# Usage: ./codex-triage.sh nuclei.jsonl nuclei-triaged.jsonl

set -euo pipefail

IN="${1:?input jsonl}"
OUT="${2:?output jsonl}"
PAR="${PAR:-6}"
MODEL="${MODEL:-gpt-5.1-codex-mini}"

: > "$OUT"

# Cache directory for dedup. Cheap on-disk memoisation keyed by
# a stable hash of the finding.
CACHE="${TRIAGE_CACHE:-$HOME/.cache/codex-triage}"
mkdir -p "$CACHE"

read -r -d '' SYSTEM_PROMPT <<'SYS_EOF' || true
You are a nuclei finding triage assistant.

You will receive one nuclei finding as JSON. Decide whether it is:
- "real": a real vulnerability that a human should confirm and report
- "likely-fp": almost certainly a false positive
- "needs-manual": ambiguous; requires manual verification

Rules:
- Do NOT probe the target. Reason only from the JSON.
- Consider template severity, matcher type, extracted values, and known-noisy templates.
- Templates ending in "-detect" or "-fingerprint" without an obvious CVE are usually informational.
- If curl-response is present and shows a 200 with matching content, treat as evidence.

Respond with EXACTLY one JSON object, nothing else:
{
  "triage": "real"|"likely-fp"|"needs-manual",
  "confidence": "low"|"medium"|"high",
  "rationale": "<one or two sentences>"
}
SYS_EOF

triage_one() {
    local line="$1"
    local key hash cached

    # Stable key for the finding.
    key="$(jq -rc '[.["template-id"], .["matched-at"]] | @tsv' <<<"$line")"
    hash="$(printf '%s' "$key" | sha256sum | cut -c1-32)"
    cached="$CACHE/$hash.json"

    local triage
    if [[ -f "$cached" ]]; then
        triage="$(cat "$cached")"
    else
        local tmp
        tmp="$(mktemp)"

        if timeout 45 codex exec \
                -c model="\"$MODEL\"" \
                --json \
                --sandbox read-only \
                --approval-policy never \
                --max-turns 3 \
                --output-last-message "$tmp" \
                "$(printf '%s\n\nFinding:\n%s' "$SYSTEM_PROMPT" "$line")" \
                >/dev/null 2>&1; then
            triage="$(cat "$tmp")"
        else
            triage='{"triage":"needs-manual","confidence":"low","rationale":"codex error or timeout"}'
        fi

        rm -f "$tmp"

        # Enforce schema; if it fails, downgrade.
        if ! jq -e '.triage and .confidence' <<<"$triage" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
            triage='{"triage":"needs-manual","confidence":"low","rationale":"non-JSON response"}'
        fi

        echo "$triage" > "$cached"
    fi

    # Merge the triage object under the original finding.
    jq -c \
        --argjson t "$triage" \
        --arg model "$MODEL" \
        --arg ts "$(date -Is)" \
        '. + {triage: ($t + {model:$model, ts:$ts})}' \
        <<<"$line"
}

export -f triage_one
export SYSTEM_PROMPT MODEL CACHE

# Fan-out. Output is appended atomically because jq -c prints one line.
< "$IN" xargs -d '\n' -I{} -P "$PAR" bash -c 'triage_one "$@"' _ {} >> "$OUT"

# Summary.
echo "wrote $(wc -l < "$OUT") triaged findings."
jq -c 'group_by(.triage.triage) | map({verdict:.[0].triage.triage, n:length})' \
    <(jq -s '.' "$OUT")
14.3 Running It Against a Real Nuclei Output
# Run nuclei against a scope file:
nuclei -l scope.txt -jsonl -o nuclei.jsonl

# Triage everything:
PAR=8 MODEL=gpt-5.1-codex-mini ./codex-triage.sh nuclei.jsonl triaged.jsonl

# Pull the "real" findings out for manual work:
jq -c 'select(.triage.triage == "real")' triaged.jsonl > real.jsonl

# Pull the medium/high-confidence "likely-fp"s to double-check:
jq -c 'select(.triage.triage=="likely-fp" and .triage.confidence!="high")' \
    triaged.jsonl > review-fp.jsonl

On a typical 800-finding nuclei run, this reduces the manual triage load to maybe 60 findings — the rest are either high-confidence false positives you can dismiss or informational fingerprints the model correctly ignored.
14.4 What This Buys You

    Time. 800 findings at 30 seconds a piece of human attention is nearly seven hours. This script does it in about six minutes.
    Consistency. The same criteria applied to every finding. Human triage drifts as the day wears on.
    Reproducibility. The cache means running the script twice gives the same answers. Auditors love this.

14.5 Known Failure Modes

    Templates the model has not seen (custom in-house nuclei templates) get triaged as "needs-manual" more often. That is correct behaviour but skews the caseload.
    Very long extracted-value fields can push a finding over the max prompt size; the wrapper does not currently truncate. Add a jq step to cap extracted-results if this bites.
    Do not run this against findings from an untrusted source without inspecting them first. A malicious "finding" could contain a prompt-injection payload aimed at your Codex agent.

15. Combining Claude Code and Codex in the Same Engagement

The best red teams use both. Here is a division of labour that works.
15.1 Claude Code Owns

    Kickoff and planning. Reading the SOW, mapping scope, generating the engagement CLAUDE.md, drafting the initial threat model.
    Codebase reviews. Source-code audits benefit from Claude's long context and careful writing.
    Exploit development. The step-by-step reasoning and skill-driven workflow are unbeatable when you are iterating on a payload.
    Report writing. Claude's prose is a better fit for client deliverables.

15.2 Codex Owns

    Batch triage. Nuclei, dalfox, ffuf, custom scanner outputs.
    Tool-building sprints. Sub-hour Python or Rust CLIs that will live only for this engagement.
    CI-style checks. Pre-commit hooks on the engagement repo, sanity checks before you push a finding.
    Anywhere the loop is for x in xs; do llm(x); done.

15.3 A Day in the Life
09:00 — Claude Code, plan the day, review yesterday's findings.
10:00 — nuclei run kicks off in the background.
10:30 — Codex triages results as they come in (`codex-triage.sh --watch`).
12:00 — Claude Code, deep-dive on the two "real" findings Codex flagged.
14:00 — Codex builds a per-endpoint fuzzer for one of them.
15:00 — Claude Code, write up the confirmed finding.
16:30 — Codex batch-audits the remaining scope for the same class of bug.
17:00 — Claude Code, roll up the day into the client-facing status update.

Neither agent is a strict replacement for the other. Treat them as two colleagues with different specialities.
15.4 Sharing State

The simplest shared state is the filesystem. Both agents can read the same engagement directory, both write findings into the same findings/ folder, both consult the same CLAUDE.md/CODEX.md. Beyond that:

    Shared MCP servers (nuclei-mcp, ffuf-mcp, custom recon-mcp) — described in section 10.
    Shared prompt libraries in a prompts/ folder with .claude.md and .codex.md variants.
    Shared vault for secrets (systemd-creds, Bitwarden CLI, or a simple gpg-encrypted .env).

Do not try to have the two agents talk to each other directly. Every attempt to build "agent-to-agent" chat at this level of maturity ends in confused loops. Keep the humans in the middle.
16. Exercises
16.1 Guided Exercises

Exercise 1 — Install and Baseline

    Install Codex CLI on your Kali VM using either the sudo-global or per-user approach.
    Authenticate with an API key (create a new key just for this course; you will delete it later).
    Create ~/.codex/config.toml with a default profile using gpt-5.1-codex-mini and sandbox_mode = "workspace-write".
    Run codex exec "write a hello world in rust; then compile it with rustc; then run it" and confirm the binary produces the expected output.

Exercise 2 — Sandbox Comparison

    Start Codex with --sandbox read-only.
    Ask it to echo hello > /tmp/hi. Observe the refusal.
    Restart with --sandbox workspace-write inside /tmp/codex-exercise/.
    Repeat. Observe success — but only inside the workspace.
    From inside that session, ask it to write /etc/hosts. Observe the refusal.
    Write a one-paragraph note in ~/notes/codex-sandbox.md describing which sandbox mode you would use for each of: reviewing a client codebase, running a POC, editing your engagement notes.

Exercise 3 — The Batch Wrapper

    Copy the codex-batch.sh script from section 9 into ~/tools/codex-batch.sh.
    Populate targets.txt with 20 lines from your lab environment (never real internet hostnames without authorisation).
    Run the batch with PAR=4.
    Inspect results.jsonl and confirm every line validates against the schema {target,verdict,evidence,model,ts}.
    Modify the prompt to also return a port_hint field (e.g., a guess at the primary service port). Re-run and confirm the schema change flows through.

16.2 Independent Exercises

Exercise 4 — Your Own Triage Prompt

Adapt codex-triage.sh to triage output from a different tool of your choice: dalfox, ffuf, or your own scanner. Design a schema, write a prompt, run it against a sample. Measure the false-positive reduction versus manual triage. Write a short internal note (~200 words) describing what worked and what did not.

Exercise 5 — Shared MCP

Build or install one MCP server (a simple one — an "http-head" server that exposes a single tool). Wire it up in both ~/.codex/config.toml and ~/.claude/settings.json. Confirm that both agents can call the tool and that both see the same behaviour. Write a README.md describing the tool contract.

Exercise 6 — Model Trade-off Study

Take the same non-trivial task (for example, "write a Python parser for the following custom log format") and run it three times with different Codex profiles: one on gpt-5.1-codex at high reasoning, one on gpt-5.1-codex at low, one on gpt-5.1-codex-mini. Record wall time, token count (from codex usage), and whether the produced code passes your tests. Present the results as a small table with a one-paragraph recommendation for your engagement policy.
17. Summary and Next Lesson

Codex CLI is the second half of the modern pentester's AI toolkit. It complements Claude Code by being fast, non-interactive, batch-friendly, and cheap enough to run over hundreds of items. Its Linux-namespace sandbox is easier to trust when the input is target-controlled, and its exec mode makes it a natural fit for the shell-first workflows red teamers already live in.

The key takeaways:

    Install with npm i -g @openai/codex; verify with codex --version.
    Configure through ~/.codex/config.toml; profiles let you keep several ready-to-go setups.
    Three sandbox modes (read-only, workspace-write, danger-full-access) and three approval policies (on-request, on-failure, never). Match them to the task.
    codex exec is the workhorse for scripting; wrap it in timeout and jq -e and it becomes a reliable batch primitive.
    Share MCP servers between Claude Code and Codex. Build the tool once, use it in both agents.
    Different prompt styles for different models; maintain two libraries.
    Use Claude Code for exploration, Codex for batch. Do not mix them within a single subtask.

In the next lesson — Lesson 07: Multi-Agent Orchestration for Engagements — we move from running each agent solo to coordinating them through a shared queue, using a light-weight orchestrator that hands off between Claude Code and Codex CLI based on the phase of the engagement. You will build a small controller that watches for new nuclei findings, dispatches triage to Codex, and escalates confirmed vulnerabilities to Claude Code for deep-dive analysis and exploit development.

Bring the codex-triage.sh you built in this lesson. You will wire it into the orchestrator.
















Lesson 07 — MCP Servers for Pentest Integration

    Module 06: Building Pentest Skills with Claude and Codex · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is the universal socket that plugs any tool into any AI agent. Where an offensive engineer once had to write bespoke plugins for every LLM front-end — a Burp extension for one, a Cursor command for another, a Slack bot for a third — MCP collapses that mess into one wire format. Write the server once, and Claude Code, Codex, Zed, Continue, and any other MCP-aware host can drive it.

For a pentester this is transformative. The tools that matter — Nmap, Burp Suite, Metasploit, Nuclei, sqlmap, BloodHound, evidence stores, notes databases — all speak their own dialects: sockets, REST APIs, RPC, CLI. MCP lets you wrap each one in a small server that exposes a curated, schema-validated slice of its capability to an AI agent, gated by whatever scope guard your engagement requires. The agent no longer shells out blindly; it invokes typed, audited tools whose behaviour you control.

This lesson teaches you to build production-quality MCP servers for pentest work. Every server in this lesson enforces a scope guard as its first line — no scan runs against a host that does not appear in ./engagement/scope.yml. By the end you will have five working servers (pentest-nmap, burp-api, metasploit, nuclei, notes), a testing harness, a publishing checklist, and a full end-to-end lab session where Claude Code drives them all against an authorised range.
1. MCP Protocol Recap

MCP is a JSON-RPC 2.0 protocol layered over one of three transports. You must know the wire format because when a tool call misbehaves — a truncated response, a malformed schema, a hanging notification — you will read the raw frames.
1.1 Transports
Transport	Use case	Framing
stdio	Local subprocess, one server per Claude Code session	Newline-delimited JSON on stdin/stdout
SSE (deprecated)	Legacy remote server	HTTP GET for events, POST for requests
Streamable HTTP	Modern remote server, team-shared	Single POST endpoint, chunked responses

For every pentest server in this lesson we start with stdio because it is the simplest to develop, then wrap the team-shared ones in HTTP with API-key auth.
1.2 The three capabilities

An MCP server advertises any of three primitive kinds:

    tools — executable functions the agent can call (nmap_scan, burp_start_scan)
    resources — read-only content the agent can subscribe to or read on demand (engagement://notes/current)
    prompts — parameterised templates the user can invoke by name (/pentest-recon-checklist)

For offensive tooling you will spend 90% of your time on tools, 10% on resources (evidence, notes, scope), and rarely touch prompts.
1.3 Handshake
client → server : initialize    { protocolVersion, capabilities, clientInfo }
server → client : result        { protocolVersion, capabilities, serverInfo }
client → server : notifications/initialized
client → server : tools/list
server → client : result        { tools: [ ... ] }
client → server : tools/call    { name, arguments }
server → client : result        { content: [...], isError: false }

Every server implementation you write must handle initialize, tools/list, and tools/call at minimum. The SDKs hide the boilerplate but the wire shape never changes.
2. Choosing an SDK

Two first-party SDKs cover the vast majority of pentest work:
SDK	Package	Best for
TypeScript	@modelcontextprotocol/sdk	Wrapping Node-native tools, HTTP servers, browser instrumentation
Python	mcp (aka mcp-python-sdk)	Wrapping subprocess CLIs (nmap, nuclei, metasploit), SQLite notes, native security libs

Because most pentest tools are CLIs or Python-native (impacket, pwntools, scapy), Python is the default in this lesson. You will see one TypeScript example in the Burp section because Burp's Extender/BApp ecosystem is Java/Kotlin and the reference client we ship is TypeScript.
2.1 Installing the Python SDK
# On Kali:
python3 -m venv ~/.venv/mcp-pentest
source ~/.venv/mcp-pentest/bin/activate
pip install mcp pyyaml httpx python-nmap
2.2 Installing the TypeScript SDK
mkdir burp-mcp && cd burp-mcp
npm init -y
npm install @modelcontextprotocol/sdk zod undici
npm install -D typescript @types/node tsx
3. Building pentest-nmap — A Minimal Python MCP Server

Our first server exposes one tool: nmap_scan. It validates that the target is in scope, invokes nmap with sane defaults, parses the XML, and returns a compact JSON summary. The scope guard is the first thing that runs — before nmap, before argument parsing, before anything else.

Save this as ~/mcp-servers/pentest-nmap/server.py:
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""pentest-nmap MCP server — scope-gated nmap wrapper."""
from __future__ import annotations

import asyncio
import ipaddress
import json
import shutil
import subprocess
import sys
from pathlib import Path
from typing import Any

import yaml
from mcp.server import Server
from mcp.server.stdio import stdio_server
from mcp.types import TextContent, Tool

SCOPE_FILE = Path("./engagement/scope.yml")
NMAP_BIN = shutil.which("nmap") or "/usr/bin/nmap"
DEFAULT_TIMEOUT = 900  # 15 minutes


def load_scope() -> dict[str, list[str]]:
    if not SCOPE_FILE.exists():
        raise RuntimeError(
            f"scope file {SCOPE_FILE} not found — refuse to run without written scope"
        )
    with SCOPE_FILE.open("r", encoding="utf-8") as f:
        data = yaml.safe_load(f) or {}
    return {
        "hosts": [str(h) for h in data.get("hosts", [])],
        "networks": [str(n) for n in data.get("networks", [])],
        "domains": [str(d) for d in data.get("domains", [])],
    }


def in_scope(target: str, scope: dict[str, list[str]]) -> bool:
    """Return True only if target is a host, CIDR, or subdomain listed in scope."""
    if target in scope["hosts"]:
        return True
    try:
        ip = ipaddress.ip_address(target)
        for net in scope["networks"]:
            if ip in ipaddress.ip_network(net, strict=False):
                return True
    except ValueError:
        pass
    lowered = target.lower()
    for domain in scope["domains"]:
        d = domain.lower().lstrip(".")
        if lowered == d or lowered.endswith("." + d):
            return True
    return False


server = Server("pentest-nmap")


@server.list_tools()
async def list_tools() -> list[Tool]:
    return [
        Tool(
            name="nmap_scan",
            description=(
                "Run an nmap scan against an in-scope target. "
                "Refuses targets not present in ./engagement/scope.yml. "
                "Returns parsed host, port, and service data as JSON."
            ),
            inputSchema={
                "type": "object",
                "properties": {
                    "target": {
                        "type": "string",
                        "description": "Single IP, hostname, or CIDR block",
                        "pattern": r"^[A-Za-z0-9.\-:/]{1,64}$",
                    },
                    "ports": {
                        "type": "string",
                        "description": "nmap port spec, e.g. '1-1024' or '22,80,443'",
                        "default": "1-1024",
                    },
                    "profile": {
                        "type": "string",
                        "enum": ["quick", "service", "vuln"],
                        "default": "service",
                    },
                },
                "required": ["target"],
                "additionalProperties": False,
            },
        )
    ]


def profile_args(profile: str) -> list[str]:
    return {
        "quick": ["-T4", "-Pn", "--top-ports", "100"],
        "service": ["-T4", "-Pn", "-sV", "--version-intensity", "5"],
        "vuln": ["-T4", "-Pn", "-sV", "--script", "vuln"],
    }[profile]


async def run_nmap(target: str, ports: str, profile: str) -> dict[str, Any]:
    args = [NMAP_BIN, "-oX", "-", "-p", ports, *profile_args(profile), target]
    proc = await asyncio.create_subprocess_exec(
        *args,
        stdout=asyncio.subprocess.PIPE,
        stderr=asyncio.subprocess.PIPE,
    )
    try:
        stdout, stderr = await asyncio.wait_for(proc.communicate(), timeout=DEFAULT_TIMEOUT)
    except asyncio.TimeoutError:
        proc.kill()
        raise RuntimeError(f"nmap exceeded {DEFAULT_TIMEOUT}s timeout")
    if proc.returncode != 0:
        raise RuntimeError(f"nmap failed rc={proc.returncode}: {stderr.decode()[:400]}")
    return parse_nmap_xml(stdout.decode())


def parse_nmap_xml(xml_text: str) -> dict[str, Any]:
    import xml.etree.ElementTree as ET
    root = ET.fromstring(xml_text)
    hosts = []
    for host in root.findall("host"):
        addr_el = host.find("address")
        addr = addr_el.get("addr") if addr_el is not None else None
        state_el = host.find("status")
        state = state_el.get("state") if state_el is not None else "unknown"
        ports = []
        for port in host.findall("./ports/port"):
            svc = port.find("service")
            ports.append({
                "port": int(port.get("portid")),
                "proto": port.get("protocol"),
                "state": port.find("state").get("state"),
                "service": svc.get("name") if svc is not None else None,
                "product": svc.get("product") if svc is not None else None,
                "version": svc.get("version") if svc is not None else None,
            })
        hosts.append({"address": addr, "state": state, "ports": ports})
    return {"hosts": hosts, "count": len(hosts)}


@server.call_tool()
async def call_tool(name: str, arguments: dict[str, Any]) -> list[TextContent]:
    if name != "nmap_scan":
        raise ValueError(f"unknown tool {name}")
    target = arguments["target"]
    scope = load_scope()
    if not in_scope(target, scope):
        return [TextContent(
            type="text",
            text=json.dumps({"error": "target out of scope", "target": target}),
        )]
    result = await run_nmap(
        target=target,
        ports=arguments.get("ports", "1-1024"),
        profile=arguments.get("profile", "service"),
    )
    return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps(result, indent=2))]


async def main() -> None:
    async with stdio_server() as (read, write):
        await server.run(read, write, server.create_initialization_options())


if __name__ == "__main__":
    asyncio.run(main())

That is roughly 120 lines including comments. Every subsequent server will follow the same shape: import, scope loader, tool schema declaration, execution, JSON response.
3.1 Scope file format
# ./engagement/scope.yml
engagement: acme-webapp-2026-q3
authorised_by: Jane Doe, CISO
window_utc: "2026-09-01T00:00:00Z/2026-09-30T23:59:59Z"

hosts:
  - 203.0.113.7
  - 203.0.113.8
networks:
  - 10.10.10.0/24
domains:
  - acme.example.com
  - lab.acme.example.com

excluded:
  - hr.acme.example.com   # explicitly out of scope

The exclusion list is enforced in the sample above by convention; a stricter implementation returns false from in_scope when the target matches any entry in excluded. Add that check in your production build.
4. Registering the Server with Claude Code and Codex
4.1 Claude Code
# Register once per project. Path must be absolute.
claude mcp add pentest-nmap python3 /home/kali/mcp-servers/pentest-nmap/server.py

# Confirm it registered:
claude mcp list
# pentest-nmap  stdio  python3 /home/kali/mcp-servers/pentest-nmap/server.py

# Remove:
claude mcp remove pentest-nmap

The server is spawned when the Claude Code session starts. It shares the working directory of the CLI, which is why the scope file resolves as ./engagement/scope.yml — that is where you must launch claude from.
4.2 Codex CLI

Codex uses a TOML config file at ~/.codex/config.toml:
[mcp_servers.pentest-nmap
]
command = "python3"
args = ["/home/kali/mcp-servers/pentest-nmap/server.py"]
env = { PENTEST_ENGAGEMENT = "acme-webapp-2026-q3" }

[mcp_servers.burp-api
]
command = "node"
args = ["/home/kali/mcp-servers/burp-mcp/dist/server.js"]
env = { BURP_API_KEY = "REPLACE_ME", BURP_API_URL = "http://127.0.0.1:1337" }

Restart Codex after editing the config. If the process fails to spawn, Codex logs it in ~/.codex/logs/mcp-servers.log.

    Pentester Insight: Never hardcode API keys into config.toml if the file is version-controlled. Use a .env file loaded by the server itself or a secret manager (pass, 1password-cli), and inject values via env = { X = "$(pass burp/api-key)" } at process start.

5. Building burp-api — Talking to Burp Suite Professional REST

Burp Professional (2023.10+) ships a built-in REST API that exposes the scanner, the site map, and the issues store. Enable it under User options → Misc → REST API, set an API key, and note the base URL (default http://127.0.0.1:1337).

Save as ~/mcp-servers/burp-mcp/src/server.ts:
#!/usr/bin/env node
import { Server } from "@modelcontextprotocol/sdk/server/index.js";
import { StdioServerTransport } from "@modelcontextprotocol/sdk/server/stdio.js";
import {
  CallToolRequestSchema,
  ListToolsRequestSchema,
} from "@modelcontextprotocol/sdk/types.js";
import { readFile } from "node:fs/promises";
import { parse } from "yaml";
import { z } from "zod";

const SCOPE_PATH = "./engagement/scope.yml";
const BURP_URL = process.env.BURP_API_URL ?? "http://127.0.0.1:1337";
const BURP_KEY = process.env.BURP_API_KEY ?? "";

if (!BURP_KEY) {
  process.stderr.write("BURP_API_KEY not set — refusing to start\n");
  process.exit(2);
}

type Scope = { hosts: string[]; networks: string[]; domains: string[] };

async function loadScope(): Promise<Scope> {
  const raw = await readFile(SCOPE_PATH, "utf8");
  const doc = parse(raw) as Partial<Scope>;
  return {
    hosts: doc.hosts ?? [],
    networks: doc.networks ?? [],
    domains: doc.domains ?? [],
  };
}

function inScope(target: string, scope: Scope): boolean {
  let host: string;
  try {
    host = new URL(target).hostname;
  } catch {
    host = target;
  }
  if (scope.hosts.includes(host)) return true;
  const lowered = host.toLowerCase();
  return scope.domains.some(d => {
    const dd = d.toLowerCase().replace(/^\./, "");
    return lowered === dd || lowered.endsWith("." + dd);
  });
}

async function burp<T>(path: string, init?: RequestInit): Promise<T> {
  const res = await fetch(`${BURP_URL}/${BURP_KEY}/v0.1${path}`, {
    ...init,
    headers: {
      "content-type": "application/json",
      ...(init?.headers ?? {}),
    },
  });
  if (!res.ok) {
    throw new Error(`burp ${path} → ${res.status}: ${await res.text()}`);
  }
  return (await res.json()) as T;
}

const StartScanArgs = z.object({
  url: z.string().url(),
  scan_configurations: z.array(z.string()).optional(),
  scope_includes: z.array(z.string()).optional(),
});

const GetIssuesArgs = z.object({
  task_id: z.string(),
  severity_min: z.enum(["info", "low", "medium", "high"]).default("low"),
});

const server = new Server(
  { name: "burp-api", version: "0.1.0" },
  { capabilities: { tools: {} } },
);

server.setRequestHandler(ListToolsRequestSchema, async () => ({
  tools: [
    {
      name: "burp_start_scan",
      description:
        "Start a Burp Suite scan against an in-scope URL. Returns the task_id.",
      inputSchema: {
        type: "object",
        properties: {
          url: { type: "string", format: "uri" },
          scan_configurations: {
            type: "array",
            items: { type: "string" },
            description: "Named Burp scan configs (e.g. 'Crawl and audit - light')",
          },
          scope_includes: {
            type: "array",
            items: { type: "string" },
            description: "Additional URL prefixes to include in scan scope",
          },
        },
        required: ["url"],
        additionalProperties: false,
      },
    },
    {
      name: "burp_scan_status",
      description: "Get scan progress and phase for a Burp task.",
      inputSchema: {
        type: "object",
        properties: { task_id: { type: "string" } },
        required: ["task_id"],
      },
    },
    {
      name: "burp_get_issues",
      description:
        "Return issues found by a Burp scan, filtered by minimum severity.",
      inputSchema: {
        type: "object",
        properties: {
          task_id: { type: "string" },
          severity_min: {
            type: "string",
            enum: ["info", "low", "medium", "high"],
          },
        },
        required: ["task_id"],
      },
    },
  ],
}));

server.setRequestHandler(CallToolRequestSchema, async request => {
  const scope = await loadScope();

  if (request.params.name === "burp_start_scan") {
    const args = StartScanArgs.parse(request.params.arguments);
    if (!inScope(args.url, scope)) {
      return {
        content: [{ type: "text", text: JSON.stringify({ error: "url out of scope" }) }],
      };
    }
    const body = {
      urls: [args.url],
      scan_configurations: args.scan_configurations?.map(name => ({
        name, type: "NamedConfiguration",
      })) ?? [],
      scope: {
        include: [
          { rule: args.url, type: "SimpleScopeDef" },
          ...(args.scope_includes ?? []).map(r => ({
            rule: r, type: "SimpleScopeDef",
          })),
        ],
      },
    };
    const res = await burp<{ task_id: string }>("/scan", {
      method: "POST",
      body: JSON.stringify(body),
    });
    return { content: [{ type: "text", text: JSON.stringify(res) }] };
  }

  if (request.params.name === "burp_scan_status") {
    const { task_id } = request.params.arguments as { task_id: string };
    const res = await burp<any>(`/scan/${task_id}`);
    const summary = {
      task_id,
      status: res.scan_status,
      progress: res.scan_metrics?.crawl_and_audit_progress,
      issues_found: res.issue_events?.length ?? 0,
    };
    return { content: [{ type: "text", text: JSON.stringify(summary) }] };
  }

  if (request.params.name === "burp_get_issues") {
    const args = GetIssuesArgs.parse(request.params.arguments);
    const rank = { info: 0, low: 1, medium: 2, high: 3 } as const;
    const min = rank[args.severity_min];
    const res = await burp<any>(`/scan/${args.task_id}`);
    const issues = (res.issue_events ?? [])
      .map((e: any) => e.issue)
      .filter((i: any) => rank[i.severity as keyof typeof rank] >= min)
      .map((i: any) => ({
        name: i.name,
        severity: i.severity,
        confidence: i.confidence,
        origin: i.origin,
        path: i.path,
        evidence: (i.evidence ?? []).slice(0, 2),
      }));
    return { content: [{ type: "text", text: JSON.stringify({ issues, count: issues.length }) }] };
  }

  throw new Error(`unknown tool ${request.params.name}`);
});

const transport = new StdioServerTransport();
await server.connect(transport);

Compile with npx tsc (target es2022, module node16, outDir dist). Register with Codex or Claude Code exactly as before, providing BURP_API_URL and BURP_API_KEY in the env.
5.1 Why three tools, not one

A single burp_scan mega-tool that returns issues at the end would block the agent for hours. Splitting into start, status, and get_issues lets the agent poll — do a quick recon step, check scan progress, run another quick step, come back later. It also lets you cancel gracefully if scope changes.
6. Building metasploit — msfrpcd with Strict Scope Enforcement

Metasploit exposes an RPC service (msfrpcd) that speaks MessagePack over TLS. From an MCP server we can drive the console, launch modules, and query sessions. The scope guard here is stricter: no module may be launched unless every RHOSTS value is in scope, and post-exploitation modules refuse to run without an existing session ID that the operator provisioned.
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""metasploit MCP server — RPC bridge with hard scope enforcement."""
from __future__ import annotations

import asyncio
import ipaddress
import json
import os
from pathlib import Path
from typing import Any

import yaml
from mcp.server import Server
from mcp.server.stdio import stdio_server
from mcp.types import TextContent, Tool
from pymetasploit3.msfrpc import MsfRpcClient

MSF_HOST = os.environ.get("MSF_RPC_HOST", "127.0.0.1")
MSF_PORT = int(os.environ.get("MSF_RPC_PORT", "55553"))
MSF_USER = os.environ.get("MSF_RPC_USER", "msf")
MSF_PASS = os.environ["MSF_RPC_PASS"]  # required
SCOPE_FILE = Path("./engagement/scope.yml")

ALLOWED_MODULES: set[str] = {
    "auxiliary/scanner/smb/smb_version",
    "auxiliary/scanner/ssh/ssh_version",
    "auxiliary/scanner/http/title",
    "exploit/multi/handler",
    "exploit/windows/smb/ms17_010_eternalblue",
}


def load_scope() -> dict[str, list[str]]:
    data = yaml.safe_load(SCOPE_FILE.read_text()) or {}
    return {
        "hosts": [str(h) for h in data.get("hosts", [])],
        "networks": [str(n) for n in data.get("networks", [])],
    }


def in_scope_ip(ip_str: str, scope: dict[str, list[str]]) -> bool:
    if ip_str in scope["hosts"]:
        return True
    try:
        ip = ipaddress.ip_address(ip_str)
        return any(ip in ipaddress.ip_network(n, strict=False) for n in scope["networks"])
    except ValueError:
        return False


def expand_rhosts(rhosts: str, scope: dict[str, list[str]]) -> list[str]:
    """Expand an RHOSTS spec ('10.0.0.0/28' or '1.2.3.4 5.6.7.8') and verify each entry."""
    out: list[str] = []
    for token in rhosts.replace(",", " ").split():
        try:
            net = ipaddress.ip_network(token, strict=False)
            for host in net.hosts() if net.num_addresses > 1 else [net.network_address]:
                out.append(str(host))
        except ValueError:
            out.append(token)
    for entry in out:
        if not in_scope_ip(entry, scope):
            raise PermissionError(f"RHOSTS entry {entry} not in scope")
    return out


client: MsfRpcClient | None = None


def msf() -> MsfRpcClient:
    global client
    if client is None:
        client = MsfRpcClient(MSF_PASS, server=MSF_HOST, port=MSF_PORT, ssl=True, username=MSF_USER)
    return client


server = Server("metasploit")


@server.list_tools()
async def list_tools() -> list[Tool]:
    return [
        Tool(
            name="msf_run_module",
            description="Run an allowlisted Metasploit module against in-scope RHOSTS.",
            inputSchema={
                "type": "object",
                "properties": {
                    "module": {"type": "string", "enum": sorted(ALLOWED_MODULES)},
                    "options": {
                        "type": "object",
                        "properties": {
                            "RHOSTS": {"type": "string"},
                            "RPORT": {"type": "integer"},
                            "LHOST": {"type": "string"},
                            "LPORT": {"type": "integer"},
                            "PAYLOAD": {"type": "string"},
                        },
                        "required": ["RHOSTS"],
                        "additionalProperties": True,
                    },
                    "wait_seconds": {"type": "integer", "default": 60, "maximum": 900},
                },
                "required": ["module", "options"],
                "additionalProperties": False,
            },
        ),
        Tool(
            name="msf_list_sessions",
            description="List active Meterpreter/shell sessions.",
            inputSchema={"type": "object", "properties": {}, "additionalProperties": False},
        ),
        Tool(
            name="msf_session_run_cmd",
            description="Run a single shell command in an existing session.",
            inputSchema={
                "type": "object",
                "properties": {
                    "session_id": {"type": "integer"},
                    "cmd": {"type": "string", "maxLength": 200},
                },
                "required": ["session_id", "cmd"],
                "additionalProperties": False,
            },
        ),
    ]


async def _run_module(module: str, options: dict[str, Any], wait: int) -> dict[str, Any]:
    kind, path = module.split("/", 1)
    mod = msf().modules.use(kind, path)
    for k, v in options.items():
        mod[k] = v
    job = mod.execute()
    started = job.get("job_id")
    for _ in range(wait):
        await asyncio.sleep(1)
        if str(started) not in msf().jobs.list:
            break
    return {"module": module, "job_id": started, "status": "complete" if str(started) not in msf().jobs.list else "running"}


@server.call_tool()
async def call_tool(name: str, arguments: dict[str, Any]) -> list[TextContent]:
    scope = load_scope()

    if name == "msf_run_module":
        module = arguments["module"]
        if module not in ALLOWED_MODULES:
            return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"error": "module not allowlisted"}))]
        options = dict(arguments["options"])
        try:
            expanded = expand_rhosts(options["RHOSTS"], scope)
        except PermissionError as e:
            return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"error": str(e)}))]
        options["RHOSTS"] = " ".join(expanded)
        result = await _run_module(module, options, arguments.get("wait_seconds", 60))
        return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps(result))]

    if name == "msf_list_sessions":
        sessions = msf().sessions.list
        return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps(sessions))]

    if name == "msf_session_run_cmd":
        sid = arguments["session_id"]
        cmd = arguments["cmd"]
        sess = msf().sessions.session(sid)
        sess.write(cmd + "\n")
        await asyncio.sleep(2)
        out = sess.read()
        return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"session_id": sid, "output": out[-4000:]}))]

    raise ValueError(f"unknown tool {name}")


async def main() -> None:
    async with stdio_server() as (r, w):
        await server.run(r, w, server.create_initialization_options())


if __name__ == "__main__":
    asyncio.run(main())

Key defensive choices in the Metasploit server:

    Allowlisted modules only. ALLOWED_MODULES is your explicit trust list; nothing outside it can be called even by mistake.
    RHOSTS expansion first, then scope check. A CIDR like 10.0.0.0/16 is expanded (or its network object is checked against scope networks) before any packet is sent.
    Session command length capped. The agent cannot pipe a 100 KB payload into a Meterpreter session.
    No arbitrary console access. There is no msf_console_run tool that would accept freeform commands, because that would break the allowlist model.

7. Building nuclei — Templated Vulnerability Scanning

Nuclei is a natural MCP citizen: its input is a URL plus a template selector, its output is JSONL, and it terminates predictably. This wrapper streams progress notifications back to the client, which we cover in section 10.
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""nuclei MCP server — templated scans against in-scope URLs."""
from __future__ import annotations

import asyncio
import json
import shutil
from pathlib import Path
from typing import Any
from urllib.parse import urlparse

import yaml
from mcp.server import Server
from mcp.server.stdio import stdio_server
from mcp.types import TextContent, Tool

SCOPE_FILE = Path("./engagement/scope.yml")
NUCLEI_BIN = shutil.which("nuclei") or "/usr/bin/nuclei"

ALLOWED_TEMPLATE_TAGS = {
    "cve", "misconfig", "exposure", "tech", "default-login",
    "ssrf", "sqli", "xss", "rce", "lfi",
}


def load_scope() -> dict[str, list[str]]:
    data = yaml.safe_load(SCOPE_FILE.read_text()) or {}
    return {"domains": [str(d) for d in data.get("domains", [])],
            "hosts": [str(h) for h in data.get("hosts", [])]}


def in_scope_url(url: str, scope: dict[str, list[str]]) -> bool:
    host = urlparse(url).hostname or ""
    if host in scope["hosts"]:
        return True
    for d in scope["domains"]:
        dd = d.lower().lstrip(".")
        if host.lower() == dd or host.lower().endswith("." + dd):
            return True
    return False


server = Server("nuclei")


@server.list_tools()
async def list_tools() -> list[Tool]:
    return [
        Tool(
            name="nuclei_scan",
            description="Run nuclei templates against an in-scope URL. Only listed tags allowed.",
            inputSchema={
                "type": "object",
                "properties": {
                    "url": {"type": "string", "format": "uri"},
                    "tags": {
                        "type": "array",
                        "items": {"type": "string", "enum": sorted(ALLOWED_TEMPLATE_TAGS)},
                        "minItems": 1,
                    },
                    "severity": {
                        "type": "string",
                        "enum": ["info", "low", "medium", "high", "critical"],
                        "default": "medium",
                    },
                    "rate_limit": {"type": "integer", "default": 25, "maximum": 150},
                },
                "required": ["url", "tags"],
                "additionalProperties": False,
            },
        )
    ]


async def run_nuclei(url: str, tags: list[str], severity: str, rate: int) -> list[dict[str, Any]]:
    args = [
        NUCLEI_BIN, "-target", url,
        "-tags", ",".join(tags),
        "-severity", ",".join(_sev_ladder(severity)),
        "-rate-limit", str(rate),
        "-json-export", "-",
        "-silent",
    ]
    proc = await asyncio.create_subprocess_exec(
        *args, stdout=asyncio.subprocess.PIPE, stderr=asyncio.subprocess.PIPE,
    )
    stdout, _ = await asyncio.wait_for(proc.communicate(), timeout=1800)
    findings = []
    for line in stdout.decode().splitlines():
        line = line.strip()
        if not line:
            continue
        try:
            f = json.loads(line)
        except json.JSONDecodeError:
            continue
        findings.append({
            "template_id": f.get("template-id"),
            "name": f.get("info", {}).get("name"),
            "severity": f.get("info", {}).get("severity"),
            "matched_at": f.get("matched-at"),
            "matcher": f.get("matcher-name"),
        })
    return findings


def _sev_ladder(min_sev: str) -> list[str]:
    ladder = ["info", "low", "medium", "high", "critical"]
    return ladder[ladder.index(min_sev):]


@server.call_tool()
async def call_tool(name: str, arguments: dict[str, Any]) -> list[TextContent]:
    if name != "nuclei_scan":
        raise ValueError(f"unknown tool {name}")
    scope = load_scope()
    url = arguments["url"]
    if not in_scope_url(url, scope):
        return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"error": "url out of scope"}))]
    findings = await run_nuclei(
        url=url,
        tags=arguments["tags"],
        severity=arguments.get("severity", "medium"),
        rate=arguments.get("rate_limit", 25),
    )
    return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"findings": findings, "count": len(findings)}))]


async def main() -> None:
    async with stdio_server() as (r, w):
        await server.run(r, w, server.create_initialization_options())


if __name__ == "__main__":
    asyncio.run(main())
8. Building notes — Engagement Notes as MCP Resources

Tools describe actions the agent takes. Resources describe read-only content it can consult. Engagement notes — hypotheses, screenshots, IOC hashes — belong in resources so the agent can pull them into context without side effects.

We store notes in SQLite because it lives inside the engagement folder, survives across sessions, and can be diffed and version-controlled with sqldiff.
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""notes MCP server — engagement notes as MCP resources."""
from __future__ import annotations

import asyncio
import json
import sqlite3
from pathlib import Path
from typing import Any

from mcp.server import Server
from mcp.server.stdio import stdio_server
from mcp.types import Resource, TextContent, Tool

DB_PATH = Path("./engagement/notes.sqlite")


def db() -> sqlite3.Connection:
    conn = sqlite3.connect(DB_PATH)
    conn.execute("""
        CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS notes (
            id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
            slug TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
            title TEXT NOT NULL,
            body TEXT NOT NULL,
            tags TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT '',
            created_at TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT (datetime('now')),
            updated_at TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT (datetime('now'))
        )
    """)
    return conn


server = Server("notes")


@server.list_resources()
async def list_resources() -> list[Resource]:
    with db() as conn:
        rows = conn.execute("SELECT slug, title, tags FROM notes ORDER BY updated_at DESC").fetchall()
    return [
        Resource(
            uri=f"engagement://notes/{slug}",
            name=title,
            description=f"tags: {tags}" if tags else None,
            mimeType="text/markdown",
        )
        for slug, title, tags in rows
    ]


@server.read_resource()
async def read_resource(uri: str) -> str:
    if not uri.startswith("engagement://notes/"):
        raise ValueError("unknown resource scheme")
    slug = uri.removeprefix("engagement://notes/")
    with db() as conn:
        row = conn.execute("SELECT title, body, tags FROM notes WHERE slug = ?", (slug,)).fetchone()
    if not row:
        raise ValueError(f"note {slug} not found")
    title, body, tags = row
    return f"# {title}\n\ntags: {tags}\n\n{body}\n"


@server.list_tools()
async def list_tools() -> list[Tool]:
    return [
        Tool(
            name="note_upsert",
            description="Create or update an engagement note.",
            inputSchema={
                "type": "object",
                "properties": {
                    "slug": {"type": "string", "pattern": r"^[a-z0-9\-]{3,60}$"},
                    "title": {"type": "string", "maxLength": 120},
                    "body": {"type": "string", "maxLength": 20000},
                    "tags": {"type": "array", "items": {"type": "string"}},
                },
                "required": ["slug", "title", "body"],
            },
        ),
        Tool(
            name="note_search",
            description="Full-text search across engagement notes.",
            inputSchema={
                "type": "object",
                "properties": {"q": {"type": "string", "maxLength": 200}},
                "required": ["q"],
            },
        ),
    ]


@server.call_tool()
async def call_tool(name: str, arguments: dict[str, Any]) -> list[TextContent]:
