    if name == "note_upsert":
        with db() as conn:
            conn.execute(
                """INSERT INTO notes(slug, title, body, tags)
                   VALUES(?, ?, ?, ?)
                   ON CONFLICT(slug) DO UPDATE SET
                     title=excluded.title,
                     body=excluded.body,
                     tags=excluded.tags,
                     updated_at=datetime('now')""",
                (arguments["slug"], arguments["title"], arguments["body"],
                 ",".join(arguments.get("tags", []))),
            )
        return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"ok": True, "slug": arguments["slug"]}))]
    if name == "note_search":
        q = f"%{arguments['q']}%"
        with db() as conn:
            rows = conn.execute(
                "SELECT slug, title FROM notes WHERE title LIKE ? OR body LIKE ? LIMIT 25",
                (q, q),
            ).fetchall()
        return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"hits": [{"slug": s, "title": t} for s, t in rows]}))]
    raise ValueError(f"unknown tool {name}")


async def main() -> None:
    DB_PATH.parent.mkdir(exist_ok=True)
    async with stdio_server() as (r, w):
        await server.run(r, w, server.create_initialization_options())


if __name__ == "__main__":
    asyncio.run(main())

Because notes are resources, Claude Code renders them in the sidebar and lets you pin one to context with a click. Codex loads them into the model's context on demand via resources/read.
9. Long-Running Tools — Progress Notifications and Timeouts

A 15-minute nmap scan blocks the agent's tool response. MCP solves this with server-emitted progress notifications tied to a progressToken the client sends with the call. Add this to the nmap server to stream progress:
from mcp.server.session import ServerSession

@server.call_tool()
async def call_tool(name: str, arguments: dict[str, Any]) -> list[TextContent]:
    ctx: ServerSession = server.request_context.session
    progress_token = server.request_context.meta.progressToken if server.request_context.meta else None

    async def report(progress: float, total: float, msg: str):
        if progress_token is None:
            return
        await ctx.send_progress_notification(
            progress_token=progress_token,
            progress=progress,
            total=total,
            message=msg,
        )

    await report(0.0, 100.0, "nmap starting")
    # ... spawn nmap, tail its stderr, and periodically report progress
    await report(100.0, 100.0, "nmap complete")

Nmap does not emit machine-readable progress by default, but --stats-every 15s prints a heartbeat line to stderr you can parse for the percentage. For nuclei, the -stats flag emits progress JSON on stdout that maps cleanly to send_progress_notification.
9.1 Timeout choices
Tool	Recommended timeout	Reason
nmap -T4 -sV on /24	900 s	Version scan of 254 hosts averages 6-10 min
burp full crawl+audit	poll, no wall-clock cap	Duration is site-dependent; cancel on scope change
nuclei with cve tag	1800 s	Thousands of templates on a single URL
msf smb_version on /24	300 s	Short and predictable

Never rely on an infinite timeout. If a tool exceeds its bound, kill the child process, return {"error": "timeout"}, and let the agent decide whether to retry with narrower args.
10. Structured JSON Schemas — Enforcing Target Types

Tool schemas are your last defence before scope guard. Use JSON Schema constraints, not just descriptions, so the SDK rejects malformed inputs before your handler runs.
{
  "type": "object",
  "properties": {
    "target": {
      "oneOf": [
        { "type": "string", "format": "ipv4" },
        { "type": "string", "format": "ipv6" },
        { "type": "string", "format": "hostname" },
        { "type": "string", "pattern": "^([0-9]{1,3}\\.){3}[0-9]{1,3}/[0-9]{1,2}$" }
      ],
      "description": "IPv4, IPv6, hostname, or CIDR block"
    }
  },
  "required": ["target"],
  "additionalProperties": false
}

Combined with the runtime scope check this gives you two layers: type validation catches typos and shell metacharacter injection attempts (; rm -rf /), scope enforcement catches semantically valid but out-of-scope requests.

    Pentester Insight: additionalProperties: false is not optional. Without it an agent (or a poisoned prompt from a client-side attacker) can smuggle extra fields into arguments — RHOSTS_extra, raw_cmd, debug — hoping your handler dereferences them. Fail loudly on unknown fields.

11. Scope Enforcement — The Whole Point

Every server in this lesson starts with the same three questions:

    Does a scope.yml exist? If not, refuse to start.
    Is the target in scope? If not, return an error content block; do not raise.
    Is the tool allowed to run this action? Consult module/tag allowlists.

Log every refusal to a local audit log so post-engagement review shows what the agent tried and what was blocked:
import logging
audit = logging.getLogger("mcp.audit")
audit.addHandler(logging.FileHandler("./engagement/audit.log"))
audit.setLevel(logging.INFO)

if not in_scope(target, scope):
    audit.warning("REFUSED %s target=%s reason=out-of-scope", name, target)
    return [TextContent(type="text", text=json.dumps({"error": "target out of scope"}))]
audit.info("ALLOWED %s target=%s", name, target)

At the end of the engagement, the audit log is part of the deliverable evidence — proof that the AI-driven scanning stayed inside authorised boundaries.
12. Authentication — MCP over HTTP for Team-Shared Servers

Stdio servers are per-user. When two operators need to share a Burp Professional instance, you move the server behind HTTP and put an API key in front of it.

The MCP Python SDK ships an ASGI adapter. Wrap the Burp server in FastAPI and require a bearer token:
from fastapi import FastAPI, HTTPException, Header
from mcp.server.streamable_http_manager import StreamableHTTPSessionManager

api_key = os.environ["MCP_API_KEY"]

app = FastAPI()
manager = StreamableHTTPSessionManager(app=server)

@app.middleware("http")
async def auth(request, call_next):
    if request.headers.get("authorization") != f"Bearer {api_key}":
        raise HTTPException(status_code=401, detail="unauthorized")
    return await call_next(request)

app.mount("/mcp", manager.asgi_app)

Run behind a reverse proxy that terminates TLS. On the client side, register with:
claude mcp add burp-team --transport http \
  --url https://mcp.internal.acme.example/mcp \
  --header "Authorization: Bearer $BURP_TEAM_KEY"

For Codex, use the corresponding [mcp_servers.burp-team] block with transport = "http".
12.1 Never expose scope-bypass endpoints

Even behind auth, do not expose an "override scope for admin" endpoint. If someone needs to change scope, they edit scope.yml and restart the server. Configuration changes belong in code review, not in a hot API call.
13. Testing MCP Servers with the Inspector and curl

@modelcontextprotocol/inspector is a Node CLI that gives you an interactive UI for any local MCP server.
npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector \
  python3 /home/kali/mcp-servers/pentest-nmap/server.py

The inspector opens a browser tab where you can list tools, invoke them with form-generated JSON, and watch the raw JSON-RPC frames. Use it before you register a server with Claude Code — an unhandled exception during list_tools will silently crash the Claude Code session; the inspector shows you exactly where.
13.1 Manual curl smoke test for HTTP servers
curl -s -X POST https://mcp.internal.acme.example/mcp \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $BURP_TEAM_KEY" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{
    "jsonrpc": "2.0",
    "id": 1,
    "method": "tools/list"
  }' | jq .

If tools/list returns your tools with correct schemas, you know the transport, auth, and registration all work. Move on to tools/call with a known-good in-scope target.
13.2 Unit-testing scope logic

Scope enforcement is a pure function of (target, scope). Test it exhaustively:
# tests/test_scope.py
from server import in_scope

SCOPE = {
    "hosts": ["203.0.113.7"],
    "networks": ["10.10.10.0/24"],
    "domains": ["acme.example.com"],
}

def test_exact_host():
    assert in_scope("203.0.113.7", SCOPE)

def test_cidr_member():
    assert in_scope("10.10.10.55", SCOPE)

def test_subdomain():
    assert in_scope("api.acme.example.com", SCOPE)

def test_out_of_scope():
    assert not in_scope("8.8.8.8", SCOPE)

def test_domain_suffix_confusion():
    assert not in_scope("evil-acme.example.com", SCOPE)
    assert not in_scope("acme.example.com.evil.tld", SCOPE)

The last test is critical — a naive endswith check would incorrectly allow evil-acme.example.com because it ends with acme.example.com. The reference implementation guards against that by prepending a . before comparison.
14. Publishing MCP Servers as npm and PyPI Packages

Once a server proves itself in a single engagement, package it so the rest of the team can install it in one command.
14.1 Python (PyPI)

Add a pyproject.toml:
[project
]
name = "pentest-nmap-mcp"
version = "0.1.0"
description = "Scope-gated Nmap MCP server for pentest engagements"
requires-python = ">=3.11"
dependencies = ["mcp>=1.0", "pyyaml"]

[project.scripts
]
pentest-nmap-mcp = "pentest_nmap_mcp.server:cli"

[build-system
]
requires = ["hatchling"]
build-backend = "hatchling.build"

Package layout:
pentest-nmap-mcp/
├── pyproject.toml
└── src/
    └── pentest_nmap_mcp/
        ├── __init__.py
        └── server.py

Build and publish:
python -m build
python -m twine upload dist/*

Users install and register with:
pipx install pentest-nmap-mcp
claude mcp add pentest-nmap pentest-nmap-mcp
14.2 TypeScript (npm)
{
  "name": "@acme-rt/burp-mcp",
  "version": "0.1.0",
  "type": "module",
  "bin": { "burp-mcp": "dist/server.js" },
  "files": ["dist"],
  "dependencies": {
    "@modelcontextprotocol/sdk": "^1.0.0",
    "undici": "^6.0.0",
    "yaml": "^2.5.0",
    "zod": "^3.23.0"
  }
}

Publish with npm publish --access public. Users then run:
claude mcp add burp-api npx @acme-rt/burp-mcp

Ship a README.md with the exact scope file schema, required env vars, and threat model. If your users have to reverse-engineer your assumptions, they will misconfigure the guard.
15. End-to-End Lab — Enumerate, Scan, and Log a Finding

The setup:

    Kali VM with Nmap, Nuclei, Burp Professional (API enabled), and Metasploit
    Authorised lab range 10.10.10.0/24 with a vulnerable web app at 10.10.10.42
    ./engagement/scope.yml populated with hosts: [10.10.10.42] and networks: [10.10.10.0/24]
    Four MCP servers registered: pentest-nmap, burp-api, nuclei, notes

Launch Claude Code from the engagement folder:
cd ~/engagements/acme-webapp-2026-q3
claude

A representative session transcript:
Operator: Enumerate services on 10.10.10.42, then run nuclei tech and cve
          tags against the highest-value HTTP service, and record any
          finding of medium or higher severity in engagement notes.

Claude:   [tool_use: pentest-nmap.nmap_scan]
          { "target": "10.10.10.42", "ports": "1-10000", "profile": "service" }
          [tool_result]
          {
            "hosts": [{ "address": "10.10.10.42", "state": "up",
              "ports": [
                { "port": 22, "state": "open", "service": "ssh",
                  "product": "OpenSSH", "version": "9.2p1" },
                { "port": 80, "state": "open", "service": "http",
                  "product": "nginx", "version": "1.24.0" },
                { "port": 8080, "state": "open", "service": "http",
                  "product": "Apache Tomcat", "version": "9.0.71" }
              ]
            }]
          }

Claude:   Tomcat 9.0.71 on 8080 has known CVEs. Running nuclei against it.
          [tool_use: nuclei.nuclei_scan]
          { "url": "http://10.10.10.42:8080/",
            "tags": ["cve", "tech"], "severity": "medium" }
          [tool_result]
          {
            "findings": [
              { "template_id": "CVE-2023-46589",
                "name": "Apache Tomcat HTTP Request Smuggling",
                "severity": "high",
                "matched_at": "http://10.10.10.42:8080/",
                "matcher": "status" }
            ], "count": 1
          }

Claude:   [tool_use: notes.note_upsert]
          { "slug": "10-10-10-42-tomcat-cve-2023-46589",
            "title": "10.10.10.42:8080 vulnerable to CVE-2023-46589",
            "body": "Nuclei matched CVE-2023-46589 (HTTP request smuggling) on Tomcat 9.0.71 running at http://10.10.10.42:8080/.\n\nEvidence: nuclei template CVE-2023-46589 matched on status.\n\nNext steps:\n- Reproduce smuggling with a hand-crafted request via Burp Repeater.\n- Confirm CL.TE vs TE.CL variant.\n- Assess reachability from external attack surface.",
            "tags": ["tomcat", "cve-2023-46589", "high"] }
          [tool_result] { "ok": true, "slug": "10-10-10-42-tomcat-cve-2023-46589" }

Claude:   Kicked off Burp scan for deeper crawl and audit against Tomcat.
          [tool_use: burp-api.burp_start_scan]
          { "url": "http://10.10.10.42:8080/",
            "scan_configurations": ["Crawl and audit - light"] }
          [tool_result] { "task_id": "17" }

          Recorded the finding. Burp task 17 is running; poll
          burp_scan_status when you want progress.

Notice what did not happen:

    No shell command executed outside a tool schema.
    No target was scanned that was not in scope.yml.
    Every action was auditable in ./engagement/audit.log.
    The finding was recorded in the notes DB as a resource future sessions can read.

16. Exercises
Guided (walk-through in the lab)

    Add the exclusions check. Extend in_scope in pentest-nmap to consult an excluded: list and refuse targets that match. Add three unit tests: excluded host wins over included network, excluded domain wins over parent domain in scope, exclusion of subnet does not affect siblings.

    Wire progress notifications into nuclei. Parse nuclei's -stats JSON stream and emit send_progress_notification calls every 10 templates. Verify with @modelcontextprotocol/inspector that progress bars render.

    Convert notes to expose FTS5. Migrate the SQLite schema to use SQLite's FTS5 virtual table, add a note_search_fts tool that accepts a MATCH expression, and benchmark it against the LIKE search on 5,000 synthetic notes.

Independent (submit a diff)

    Build a sqlmap MCP server. Wrap sqlmap with a schema that requires a URL parameter and an injection point, allowlists techniques to BEUSTQ, caps --level at 3 and --risk at 2, and returns the summary JSON. Enforce scope on the target host.

    Build a bloodhound-ce MCP server. Talk to BloodHound Community Edition's REST API to expose two tools: bh_run_cypher (with a Cypher allowlist matching only read patterns) and bh_shortest_path. Reject Cypher containing CREATE, DELETE, MERGE, DROP.

    Add HTTP transport with mTLS to the Burp server. Move the Burp MCP server behind FastAPI + Uvicorn with mutual TLS. Issue client certificates per operator, log the client CN of every tool call to the audit log.

17. Summary and Next Lesson

MCP takes each of your favourite pentest tools and turns it into a first-class citizen of the AI agent. You wrap the tool in a small server, declare a typed schema, gate every action behind a scope guard, and register the server with Claude Code or Codex.

The pattern is always the same:

    Load ./engagement/scope.yml at startup and on every call.
    Declare tools with strict JSON schemas and additionalProperties: false.
    Refuse out-of-scope targets before any tool executes.
    Cap wall-clock timeouts; stream progress for long-runners.
    Log every allowed and refused invocation to a per-engagement audit trail.
    Package and share so the whole team benefits.

In the next lesson — Module 06, Lesson 08: Building Custom Slash Commands and Skill Packs — you will compose these MCP servers into higher-level workflows: a /recon slash command that chains pentest-nmap, nuclei, and notes with a pre-authored prompt, and a reusable skill pack that ships to any Claude Code or Codex operator on your team with one command.


























Lesson 08 — Workflow Automation with AI Agents

    Module 06: Building Pentest Skills with Claude and Codex · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

A single agent, no matter how capable, has hard ceilings. It carries only one context window, executes tools serially, forgets what it deemed irrelevant, and — when a task grows beyond a few dozen tool calls — it either burns through its budget on branches that produce nothing or drifts away from the objective. In penetration testing this ceiling shows up quickly: recon on a modest scope means enumerating dozens of subdomains, fingerprinting hundreds of services, spidering thousands of URLs, and diffing the result against last week's snapshot. Cramming that into one agent turn is possible only for a toy target.

Workflow automation is the answer. Instead of one agent doing everything, we compose specialized agents into a pipeline: each agent has a narrow job, a fixed prompt, a tight tool allow-list, and a predictable output shape. A workflow script — the outer control layer — decides who runs when, what state passes between them, which steps run in parallel, and where the pipeline joins results. The workflow is deterministic where determinism matters (fan-out, timeouts, retries, ordering) and non-deterministic where creativity matters (the agent's reasoning inside a phase).

This lesson teaches you how to orchestrate multi-agent pentest workflows using Claude Code's Workflow tool. You will build a recon fan-out that spawns four parallel enumerators, a review workflow with an adversarial verifier, a triage router that dispatches findings to web/cloud/crypto specialists, and a full 200-line web-recon-triage script that scans a domain end-to-end. Along the way we cover cost budgets, checkpointing, observability, multi-provider routing, and the anti-patterns that turn a workflow into a runaway.

By the end you will be able to look at a pentest engagement, decide whether a workflow is warranted, choose the correct topology, write the script, and enforce ethical scope inside every phase.
1. Introduction — Why a Single Agent Is Not Enough
1.1 The Ceiling of One Agent

A Claude Code agent has three practical limits during a pentest:

    Context window. Even a 200K token window fills fast when subdomain lists, nmap XML, and JavaScript bundles land in it. Once the window compacts, prior evidence blurs.
    Serial tool execution. An agent that needs to run subfinder, httpx, nuclei, and katana runs them one after another. Four thirty-second scans become two minutes of wall time before any reasoning happens.
    Task drift. A prompt asking for "full recon on example.com" invites the agent to invent scope. It may spider the marketing site, guess admin panels, or start requesting subdomains you never authorized.

A workflow lifts each ceiling by moving control out of the agent's head and into a script that you author, review, and version.
1.2 The Shape of a Workflow

A workflow is a JavaScript file executed by the Workflow tool. It defines phases, spawns agents, joins their outputs, applies deterministic logic (filters, sorts, JSON validation), and only returns to the parent Claude session when the pipeline completes or fails.
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ workflow: web-recon-triage.js                              │
│                                                            │
│   phase("scope")     → validate target, load rules-of-engagement
│   parallel([                                                │
│     agent("subs",   "enumerate subdomains"),               │
│     agent("ports",  "scan open ports"),                    │
│     agent("tech",   "fingerprint stacks"),                 │
│     agent("js",     "extract endpoints from JS")           │
│   ])                                                        │
│   phase("aggregate") → merge, dedupe, structure            │
│   agent("triage",   "rank findings by exploitability")     │
│   phase("gate")     → human approval before active testing │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The parent Claude session sees only the workflow's final result: a ranked list of findings and a decision. All the intermediate agent chatter stays inside the workflow log.
1.3 What Workflows Buy You
Property	Single Agent	Workflow
Parallelism	None (serial tools)	N-way fan-out
Context reuse	One shared window	Isolated per agent
Determinism	Prompt-dependent	Scripted control flow
Cost visibility	End-of-run total	Per-phase, per-agent
Resume on failure	Restart from zero	Checkpointed
Scope enforcement	Prompt hope	Script gate

The trade is complexity. A workflow is code — with all the debugging that implies. Use it when the shape of the task rewards the effort.
2. Workflow Topologies

Four topologies cover almost every pentest use case. Learn to recognize which one a task wants before you write a line of script.
2.1 Sequential Pipeline

Each phase depends on the last. Output of A feeds B, which feeds C.
[recon] → [asset filter] → [vuln scan] → [triage] → [report]

Use when each stage needs the previous stage's result and the total wall time is acceptable. Classic example: nmap output feeds vulnerability scanning which feeds exploit selection.
2.2 Parallel Fan-Out

One input, many independent workers, one joined output.
                    ┌─ [subdomains] ─┐
[target domain] ──→ ├─ [ports] ──────┤ ──→ [aggregator]
                    ├─ [tech-fp] ────┤
                    └─ [js-endpoints]┘

Use when subtasks share no state and can run simultaneously. Recon is the canonical fit: none of the enumerators need to know what the others found.
2.3 Hierarchical Supervisor

A supervisor agent decomposes work, delegates to specialists, then re-integrates.
              [supervisor]
             /    |    |    \
      [web]  [api]  [mobile] [cloud]
             \    |    |    /
              [supervisor]

Use when the decomposition itself is a reasoning task — you do not know upfront which specialists to spawn. Triage is the fit: the supervisor reads a finding and picks the correct expert.
2.4 Map-Reduce

Split a large input into chunks, run the same agent on each chunk, then reduce the results.
   [10,000 URLs] ─split─→ [chunk 1] ─┐
                          [chunk 2] ─├─ [reduce → dedup + rank]
                          [chunk 3] ─┤
                          [chunk N] ─┘

Use when the input is homogeneous and large. Classifying 10,000 URLs by likely attack surface is a map-reduce; enumerating four different data sources is a fan-out.
2.5 Topology Cheat Sheet
Task	Topology
Recon a single domain	Parallel fan-out
End-to-end pentest campaign	Sequential pipeline
Route a mixed bag of findings	Hierarchical supervisor
Classify a giant URL list	Map-reduce
Review a PR for bugs, style, tests	Parallel fan-out then aggregator
Multi-target scan across a scope list	Map-reduce across targets
3. When to Prefer a Workflow Over a Single Agent

Not every pentest task benefits from a workflow. The overhead of authoring, testing, and reviewing a script has to be earned.
3.1 The Three-Question Filter

Ask, in order:

    Is the scope bounded and enumerable? If you cannot list the phases up front, you are not ready for a workflow. Write a plan first.
    Would deterministic control flow reduce failure modes? If a single agent tends to skip steps, exhaust its budget on one branch, or forget scope, a scripted phase list fixes each.
    Is there real parallelism budget? If the tasks are inherently serial or bounded by a single rate-limited API, fan-out gains nothing.

Two yes answers is the threshold. Three is a strong signal.
3.2 When to Stay With a Single Agent

    Exploration. You do not know what you are looking for. Let an agent poke.
    Debugging a specific error. The context is small and the loop is tight.
    One-off recon. A single subfinder | httpx | nuclei chain is a bash pipeline, not a workflow.
    Anything under two minutes wall time. Workflow overhead is not worth it.

3.3 When to Reach for a Workflow

    Repeatable campaigns. Same shape every week — subdomain diff, vuln scan, triage, report.
    Multi-source aggregation. Four APIs, four agents, one joined view.
    Adversarial review. Producer agent plus verifier agent, both scripted.
    Long-running scans. You want checkpoints and resume.
    Multi-target scope. Same steps applied across a target list.
    Human-in-the-loop gates. You want to pause for approval at defined points.

4. Claude Code Workflow Tool Overview

The Workflow tool executes a JavaScript file that describes phases, agents, and joins. You author the script; the tool runs it.
4.1 The Script API

Four helpers cover most workflows:

    pipeline(...phases) — declare an ordered sequence.
    parallel(...agents) — declare a fan-out block that runs its arguments concurrently.
    agent(name, prompt, options) — spawn a subagent with a name, a prompt, and options (tools, model, budget, output_schema).
    phase(name, fn) — a scripted step that runs your JavaScript, not an agent. Use for filtering, joining, validating.

4.2 A Minimal Script
// hello-workflow.js
export default pipeline(
  phase("prepare", (ctx) => {
    ctx.target = ctx.input.domain;
    return ctx;
  }),
  agent("subs",
    (ctx) => `Enumerate subdomains for ${ctx.target}. Return JSON.`,
    { tools: ["Bash"], budget: { max_tokens: 20_000 } }
  ),
  phase("summary", (ctx) => {
    return { subdomains: ctx.subs.output };
  })
);

The tool loads this file, calls the exported pipeline with the parent's input, and returns the last phase's return value.
4.3 Context Passing

Every phase and agent gets a ctx object. Whatever an agent returns is attached at ctx.<name>. A phase that mutates ctx mutates it for downstream steps. A phase that returns a new object replaces the context.
4.4 Agent Options You Will Use
Option	Purpose
tools	Allow-list of tools this agent may call
model	Override the model (opus, sonnet, haiku, ollama:llama3)
budget.max_tokens	Hard token ceiling for this agent
budget.max_iterations	Cap on tool-use turns
output_schema	JSON schema the agent must match
timeout_ms	Wall-clock timeout
retry	{ max: 2, backoff: "exp" }
scope	Inherited scope object (see section 15)
4.5 Invoking a Workflow From Claude Code

From a Claude Code session:
Workflow(
  file_path: "/opt/pentest/workflows/web-recon-triage.js",
  input: { domain: "example.com", scope_file: "/opt/pentest/scope.json" }
)

The parent session pauses until the workflow returns. The return value is a JSON object.
5. Writing a First Workflow — recon-fan-out

Here is a complete, real workflow that spawns four recon agents in parallel and joins their outputs.
5.1 The Script
// recon-fan-out.js
// Fans four recon agents at a single domain, joins their JSON outputs.

const RECON_TOOLS = ["Bash", "Read", "Write", "Grep"];

const subdomainAgent = (ctx) => agent(
  "subdomains",
  `You are a subdomain enumeration agent. Target: ${ctx.target}.
   Use subfinder and amass passive mode only.
   Do not send active DNS queries beyond the target's authoritative servers.
   Return JSON: { "subdomains": [ { "name": "...", "source": "..." } ] }`,
  {
    tools: RECON_TOOLS,
    budget: { max_tokens: 30_000, max_iterations: 12 },
    timeout_ms: 180_000,
    output_schema: {
      type: "object",
      required: ["subdomains"],
      properties: {
        subdomains: {
          type: "array",
          items: {
            type: "object",
            required: ["name", "source"],
            properties: {
              name:   { type: "string" },
              source: { type: "string" }
            }
          }
        }
      }
    }
  }
);

const portAgent = (ctx) => agent(
  "ports",
  `You are a port scanning agent. Target: ${ctx.target}.
   Run: nmap -T3 -sS --top-ports 100 --open -oX -.
   Parse the XML and return JSON: { "hosts": [ { "ip": "...", "ports": [ { "port": N, "proto": "tcp", "service": "..." } ] } ] }`,
  {
    tools: RECON_TOOLS,
    budget: { max_tokens: 40_000, max_iterations: 15 },
    timeout_ms: 300_000,
    output_schema: {
      type: "object",
      required: ["hosts"],
      properties: {
        hosts: { type: "array" }
      }
    }
  }
);

const techAgent = (ctx) => agent(
  "tech",
  `You are a technology fingerprinting agent. Target: ${ctx.target}.
   Use httpx -tech-detect against the root domain and the top 5 subdomains from ctx.subdomains.
   Return JSON: { "stacks": [ { "url": "...", "technologies": [ "..." ] } ] }`,
  {
    tools: RECON_TOOLS,
    budget: { max_tokens: 30_000 },
    timeout_ms: 180_000
  }
);

const jsAgent = (ctx) => agent(
  "js",
  `You are a JavaScript endpoint extractor. Target: ${ctx.target}.
   Use katana -js-crawl and linkfinder on the top 5 subdomains.
   Return JSON: { "endpoints": [ { "url": "...", "source_file": "..." } ] }`,
  {
    tools: RECON_TOOLS,
    budget: { max_tokens: 40_000 },
    timeout_ms: 240_000
  }
);

export default pipeline(
  phase("validate", (ctx) => {
    if (!ctx.input.target || !/^[a-z0-9.-]+$/i.test(ctx.input.target)) {
      throw new Error(`invalid target: ${ctx.input.target}`);
    }
    ctx.target = ctx.input.target;
    return ctx;
  }),

  // Step 1: subdomains first (tech and js depend on it)
  subdomainAgent,

  // Step 2: everything else can fan out
  parallel(portAgent, techAgent, jsAgent),

  phase("join", (ctx) => ({
    target: ctx.target,
    subdomains: ctx.subdomains.subdomains,
    hosts:      ctx.ports.hosts,
    stacks:     ctx.tech.stacks,
    endpoints:  ctx.js.endpoints,
    generated_at: new Date().toISOString()
  }))
);
5.2 What This Buys

    Subdomain enumeration runs first because the tech and JS agents need its output.
    Ports, tech, and JS run truly in parallel — three tool-heavy tasks compressed from serial (about 12 minutes) to concurrent (about 5 minutes).
    Every agent has a token ceiling, an iteration cap, and a wall-clock timeout. No runaway.
    output_schema on the subdomain agent means downstream code can trust the shape.
    Scope is enforced in the prompt (passive sources, top-5 subdomains) and again by the tool allow-list.

5.3 Running It
Workflow(
  file_path: "/opt/pentest/workflows/recon-fan-out.js",
  input: { target: "lab.example.com" }
)

The parent session gets back a single JSON object with all four data sources merged.
6. Building a pentest-review Workflow

This workflow reviews a batch of code changes for security issues, cleanup, and test coverage — then adversarially verifies each finding to reduce false positives.
6.1 The Shape

For each dimension (bugs, cleanup, tests), a producer agent generates findings. A verifier agent then takes each finding and tries to disprove it. Only findings the verifier cannot disprove make it into the final report.
              [diff]
                │
     ┌──────────┼──────────┐
     ↓          ↓          ↓
  [bugs]    [cleanup]    [tests]
     │          │          │
     └────┬─────┴─────┬────┘
          ↓           ↓
    per-finding: [verifier] adversarial
                     │
                     ↓
              [merged report]
6.2 The Script
// pentest-review.js

const producer = (name, focus) => (ctx) => agent(
  name,
  `You are a ${focus} reviewer. Read the diff at ${ctx.diff_path}.
   Return JSON: { "findings": [ { "id": "...", "file": "...", "line": N,
                                   "severity": "low|med|high", "claim": "..." } ] }`,
  {
    tools: ["Read", "Grep", "Bash"],
    budget: { max_tokens: 40_000 },
    output_schema: {
      type: "object",
      required: ["findings"],
      properties: { findings: { type: "array" } }
    }
  }
);

const verifier = (finding) => agent(
  `verify-${finding.id}`,
  `You are an adversarial verifier. A reviewer claims:
   FILE: ${finding.file}
   LINE: ${finding.line}
   CLAIM: ${finding.claim}
   Try to disprove the claim. Read the file, trace the flow, propose a counterexample.
   Return JSON: { "id": "${finding.id}", "verdict": "confirmed|refuted|uncertain",
                  "reasoning": "..." }`,
  {
    tools: ["Read", "Grep"],
    budget: { max_tokens: 20_000, max_iterations: 8 },
    output_schema: {
      type: "object",
      required: ["id", "verdict", "reasoning"]
    }
  }
);

export default pipeline(
  phase("prep", (ctx) => {
    ctx.diff_path = ctx.input.diff_path;
    if (!ctx.diff_path) throw new Error("diff_path required");
    return ctx;
  }),

  parallel(
    producer("bugs",    "security-bug"),
    producer("cleanup", "code-cleanup"),
    producer("tests",   "test-coverage")
  ),

  phase("gather", (ctx) => {
    ctx.all_findings = [
      ...ctx.bugs.findings,
      ...ctx.cleanup.findings,
      ...ctx.tests.findings
    ];
    return ctx;
  }),

  // Fan out one verifier per finding
  phase("verify", async (ctx) => {
    const jobs = ctx.all_findings.map(f => verifier(f));
    const results = await Promise.all(jobs.map(j => j.run(ctx)));
    ctx.verdicts = results;
    return ctx;
  }),

  phase("report", (ctx) => {
    const confirmed = ctx.all_findings
      .map(f => ({ ...f, verdict: ctx.verdicts.find(v => v.id === f.id) }))
      .filter(f => f.verdict && f.verdict.verdict === "confirmed");
    return { confirmed, all: ctx.all_findings, verdicts: ctx.verdicts };
  })
);
6.3 Why the Adversarial Split Helps

Producers over-report. Every LLM reviewer flags things that a five-second look would dismiss. A verifier prompted to disprove — with only Read and Grep, so it must ground its argument in code — filters that noise. Empirically this cuts false positive rates by roughly half on non-trivial diffs.
7. Multi-Agent Triage — Routing Findings to Specialists

Recon and code review produce mixed bags. Triage routes each finding to the right specialist.
7.1 The Router Pattern
// triage.js

const webSpec = (finding) => agent(
  `web-${finding.id}`,
  `You are a web application security expert. Assess: ${JSON.stringify(finding)}.
   Return JSON with exploitability, impact, and reproduction steps.`,
  { tools: ["Bash", "Read", "Write"], budget: { max_tokens: 30_000 } }
);

const cloudSpec = (finding) => agent(
  `cloud-${finding.id}`,
  `You are a cloud security expert (AWS/GCP/Azure). Assess: ${JSON.stringify(finding)}.
   Return JSON with cloud-specific exploitability and blast radius.`,
  { tools: ["Bash", "Read"], budget: { max_tokens: 30_000 } }
);

const cryptoSpec = (finding) => agent(
  `crypto-${finding.id}`,
  `You are a cryptography expert. Assess: ${JSON.stringify(finding)}.
   Return JSON with algorithm weakness class and real-world impact.`,
  { tools: ["Read"], budget: { max_tokens: 20_000 } }
);

const CATEGORY_MAP = {
  web:    webSpec,
  cloud:  cloudSpec,
  crypto: cryptoSpec
};

const classifier = (finding) => agent(
  `classify-${finding.id}`,
  `Categorize this finding into one of: web, cloud, crypto, other.
   FINDING: ${JSON.stringify(finding)}.
   Return JSON: { "category": "web|cloud|crypto|other" }`,
  { tools: [], model: "haiku", budget: { max_tokens: 2_000 } }
);

export default pipeline(
  phase("classify", async (ctx) => {
    const classifications = await Promise.all(
      ctx.input.findings.map(f => classifier(f).run(ctx))
    );
    ctx.classified = ctx.input.findings.map((f, i) => ({
      ...f, category: classifications[i].category
    }));
    return ctx;
  }),

  phase("dispatch", async (ctx) => {
    const dispatched = ctx.classified
      .filter(f => f.category !== "other")
      .map(f => CATEGORY_MAP[f.category](f).run(ctx));
    ctx.assessments = await Promise.all(dispatched);
    return ctx;
  }),

  phase("rank", (ctx) => {
    ctx.assessments.sort((a, b) =>
      severityScore(b.severity) - severityScore(a.severity)
    );
    return { assessments: ctx.assessments };
  })
);

function severityScore(s) {
  return { critical: 4, high: 3, med: 2, low: 1 }[s] || 0;
}
7.2 Why a Cheap Classifier First

The classifier uses Haiku (or Ollama) because the task is small and its output shape is one field. Sending every finding straight to Opus would be four to five times more expensive. Route cheaply, work expensively only on the routes that matter.
8. Structured Outputs From Workflow Agents

Loose text between phases guarantees trouble. Enforce JSON schemas.
8.1 Attaching a Schema

Every agent accepts output_schema. The Workflow tool validates the agent's final message against the schema before attaching it to ctx. On failure it retries with the error injected into the next prompt.
agent(
  "cve-lookup",
  (ctx) => `Look up CVEs for ${ctx.tech.stacks[0].technologies.join(", ")}.
            Return strict JSON.`,
  {
    output_schema: {
      type: "object",
      required: ["cves"],
      properties: {
        cves: {
          type: "array",
          items: {
            type: "object",
            required: ["id", "cvss", "affected"],
            properties: {
              id:       { type: "string", pattern: "^CVE-\\d{4}-\\d+$" },
              cvss:     { type: "number", minimum: 0, maximum: 10 },
              affected: { type: "string" }
            }
          }
        }
      }
    },
    retry: { max: 2, backoff: "exp" }
  }
)
8.2 Aggregating Structured Output

Once each agent's output is a validated object, aggregation is plain JavaScript:
phase("aggregate", (ctx) => {
  const flat = ctx.results.flatMap(r => r.cves);
  const dedup = new Map(flat.map(c => [c.id, c]));
  return { cves: [...dedup.values()].sort((a, b) => b.cvss - a.cvss) };
});
8.3 Schema-First Prompt Design

A useful discipline: write the schema before writing the prompt. Then the prompt becomes: "produce data matching this schema." The agent's job narrows and its output shape stops surprising you.
9. Cost and Token Budget

Workflows can burn tokens fast. Budget them explicitly.
9.1 The Three Budget Levers

    max_tokens — total tokens the agent may consume across all turns.
    max_iterations — maximum tool-use turns.
    model — cheaper models for cheaper phases.

9.2 A Budget Table

For a typical recon workflow, the following budgets are conservative:
Agent	Model	max_tokens	max_iterations
classifier	haiku	2,000	1
subdomains	sonnet	30,000	12
ports	sonnet	40,000	15
tech	sonnet	30,000	10
js	sonnet	40,000	15
triage	opus	20,000	5
verifier	sonnet	20,000	8

Total: roughly 180K tokens per run. Track it — set an alert if any single phase burns more than 150% of its budget in a week.
9.3 Global Ceilings

Add a workflow-level ceiling to catch runaways:
export default pipeline(
  // ...
).withBudget({ max_total_tokens: 500_000, max_wall_ms: 20 * 60_000 });

If the workflow crosses either ceiling, it aborts, checkpoints, and returns a partial result with a budget_exceeded flag.
10. Persistence and Resume

Long workflows fail. Networks blip, an agent times out, the harness crashes. Checkpoint so restart is cheap.
10.1 Automatic Checkpoints

The Workflow tool checkpoints ctx after every phase to a state file:
/opt/pentest/state/<workflow_run_id>.json

On restart, it loads the last completed phase's ctx and resumes.
10.2 Explicit Checkpoints Inside a Phase

For long phases, checkpoint inside:
phase("scan-all-hosts", async (ctx) => {
  ctx.host_results = ctx.host_results || {};
  for (const host of ctx.hosts) {
    if (ctx.host_results[host]) continue;   // already done
    ctx.host_results[host] = await scanHost(host);
    await ctx.checkpoint();                 // persist after each host
  }
  return ctx;
});
10.3 Resuming a Run
Workflow(
  file_path: "/opt/pentest/workflows/web-recon-triage.js",
  resume: "run-2026-09-05-1439"
)

The tool re-loads state and picks up at the last unfinished phase.
10.4 Idempotency

Design phases to be idempotent. If a phase runs twice — first crash, then resume — its net effect should be the same. Write to temp files, then rename. Do not append to a target file inside a phase.
11. Observability — Logging Every Agent's Input and Output

You cannot debug what you cannot see. The Workflow tool writes a per-run log by default.
11.1 The Run Log
/opt/pentest/logs/<workflow_run_id>/
├── manifest.json           # phase list, timings, tokens
├── phase-01-validate/
│   ├── input.json
│   └── output.json
├── phase-02-subdomains/
│   ├── prompt.txt
│   ├── messages.jsonl      # every model turn
│   ├── tool-calls.jsonl    # every Bash/Read/etc call
│   └── output.json
└── phase-03-parallel/
    ├── ports/...
    ├── tech/...
    └── js/...
11.2 Reading the Log

The manifest.json tells you where time went:
{
  "run_id": "run-2026-09-05-1439",
  "workflow": "recon-fan-out.js",
  "duration_ms": 312_450,
  "phases": [
    { "name": "validate",   "duration_ms": 12,     "tokens": 0 },
    { "name": "subdomains", "duration_ms": 84_120, "tokens": 22_431 },
    { "name": "parallel",   "duration_ms": 214_800, "tokens": 91_204 },
    { "name": "join",       "duration_ms": 8,       "tokens": 0 }
  ],
  "total_tokens": 113_635
}
11.3 Replay for Debugging

When an agent misbehaves, replay it with the exact prompt and previous context:
Workflow(
  file_path: "/opt/pentest/workflows/recon-fan-out.js",
  replay: { run_id: "run-2026-09-05-1439", phase: "ports" }
)

Change the prompt, re-run just that phase, compare outputs.
11.4 Streaming Logs to a SIEM

For team runs, tail the log directory into your SIEM. Every tool call is a security event; every prompt is a piece of evidence for the engagement's record.
12. Multi-Provider Workflows

Not every task needs Claude Opus. Route cheap classification to Ollama, keep deep reasoning on Claude.
12.1 The Model Field

agent(name, prompt, { model: "ollama:llama3.1:8b" }) sends the agent's calls to a local Ollama process. Anything Ollama can serve is fair game.
12.2 A Cost-Aware Router
const bulkClassifier = (item) => agent(
  `classify-${item.id}`,
  `Which category best fits this URL? Choose one:
   [static, api, admin, auth, upload, other].
   URL: ${item.url}
   Return JSON: { "category": "..." }`,
  {
    model: "ollama:llama3.1:8b",
    budget: { max_tokens: 500 },
    tools: []
  }
);

const deepAnalyst = (item) => agent(
  `analyze-${item.id}`,
  `Deep-analyze this admin endpoint for authentication bypass.
   URL: ${item.url}. Consider timing, header injection, and state confusion.`,
  {
    model: "opus",
    budget: { max_tokens: 40_000 },
    tools: ["Bash", "Read"]
  }
);
12.3 When Provider Routing Pays

    Ten thousand URLs to classify — Ollama wins by two orders of magnitude on cost.
    One hundred admin endpoints to reason about deeply — Claude Opus is worth it.
    Everything in between — Sonnet, or Haiku for shape-only tasks.

12.4 Guarding Local Models

Local models hallucinate JSON. Always pair them with output_schema and retry. Better: force them into a numbered choice — "reply with a single digit 1-5" — and parse the digit yourself. LLMs are worse at free-form JSON than they are at picking one of five options.
13. Full Working Example — web-recon-triage

This is a complete workflow that scans a domain, fans out four recon agents, aggregates, triages, and gates on human review before any active testing. Roughly 200 lines.
// web-recon-triage.js
// End-to-end web recon and triage with a human gate before active testing.

const RECON_TOOLS = ["Bash", "Read", "Write", "Grep"];

// -------- Scope validation --------
const scopeCheck = phase("scope", (ctx) => {
  const scope = JSON.parse(require("fs").readFileSync(ctx.input.scope_file));
  const t = ctx.input.target.toLowerCase();
  const allowed = scope.domains.some(d =>
    t === d.toLowerCase() || t.endsWith("." + d.toLowerCase())
  );
  if (!allowed) {
    throw new Error(`REFUSING: target ${t} is not in scope ${JSON.stringify(scope.domains)}`);
  }
  ctx.target = t;
  ctx.scope = scope;
  return ctx;
});

// -------- Recon agents --------
const subs = (ctx) => agent(
  "subdomains",
  `Enumerate subdomains for ${ctx.target} using passive sources only.
   Tools: subfinder -silent, amass enum -passive.
   Do not perform active DNS brute forcing.
   Return JSON: { "subdomains": [ { "name": "...", "source": "..." } ] }`,
  {
    tools: RECON_TOOLS,
    model: "sonnet",
    budget: { max_tokens: 30_000, max_iterations: 12 },
    timeout_ms: 180_000,
    output_schema: {
      type: "object", required: ["subdomains"],
      properties: { subdomains: { type: "array" } }
    }
  }
);

const ports = (ctx) => agent(
  "ports",
  `Scan the top 5 subdomains (${ctx.subdomains.subdomains.slice(0,5).map(s=>s.name).join(", ")})
   with: nmap -T3 -sS --top-ports 100 --open -oX -.
   Return JSON: { "hosts": [ { "host": "...", "ports": [ { "port": N, "service": "..." } ] } ] }`,
  {
    tools: RECON_TOOLS,
    model: "sonnet",
    budget: { max_tokens: 40_000, max_iterations: 15 },
    timeout_ms: 300_000
  }
);

const tech = (ctx) => agent(
  "tech",
  `Fingerprint the top 5 subdomains using httpx -tech-detect -status-code.
   Return JSON: { "stacks": [ { "url": "...", "technologies": [ "..." ], "status": N } ] }`,
  {
    tools: RECON_TOOLS,
    model: "sonnet",
    budget: { max_tokens: 30_000 },
    timeout_ms: 180_000
  }
);

const js = (ctx) => agent(
  "js",
  `Extract endpoints from JavaScript on the top 5 subdomains.
   Use katana -js-crawl -d 2 and pipe through linkfinder.
   Return JSON: { "endpoints": [ { "url": "...", "source": "..." } ] }`,
  {
    tools: RECON_TOOLS,
    model: "sonnet",
    budget: { max_tokens: 40_000 },
    timeout_ms: 240_000
  }
);

// -------- Classifier and triage --------
const classify = (url) => agent(
  `classify-${hash(url)}`,
  `Classify: ${url}. Categories: static, api, admin, auth, upload, other.
   Return JSON: { "category": "..." }`,
  {
    model: "haiku",
    budget: { max_tokens: 500 },
    tools: []
  }
);

const triage = (ctx) => agent(
  "triage",
  `You are a senior pentester. Given the JSON below, rank the top 10 leads
   by exploitability. Consider: exposed admin panels, uncommon services,
   old technology versions, novel endpoints.
   Do not propose active exploitation — this is triage only.
   DATA: ${JSON.stringify(ctx.aggregated).slice(0, 60_000)}
   Return JSON: { "leads": [ { "rank": N, "target": "...", "reason": "...",
                                "next_step": "...", "risk": "low|med|high" } ] }`,
  {
    tools: ["Read"],
    model: "opus",
    budget: { max_tokens: 30_000 },
    output_schema: {
      type: "object", required: ["leads"],
      properties: { leads: { type: "array" } }
    }
  }
);

// -------- The pipeline --------
export default pipeline(
  scopeCheck,

  subs,

  parallel(ports, tech, js),

  phase("classify-urls", async (ctx) => {
    const urls = (ctx.js.endpoints || []).map(e => e.url).slice(0, 200);
    const jobs = urls.map(u => classify(u).run(ctx));
    const cats = await Promise.all(jobs);
    ctx.categorized = urls.map((u, i) => ({ url: u, category: cats[i].category }));
    return ctx;
  }),

  phase("aggregate", (ctx) => {
    ctx.aggregated = {
      target: ctx.target,
      subdomains: ctx.subdomains.subdomains,
      hosts:      ctx.ports.hosts,
      stacks:     ctx.tech.stacks,
      endpoints:  ctx.categorized,
      generated_at: new Date().toISOString()
    };
    return ctx;
  }),

  triage,

  // -------- Human gate --------
  phase("gate", async (ctx) => {
    const leads = ctx.triage.leads.filter(l => l.risk !== "low");
    if (leads.length === 0) {
      return { status: "no-active-testing-needed", leads: [] };
    }
    const approved = await ctx.humanReview({
      title: `Approve active testing on ${leads.length} leads?`,
      leads,
      timeout_ms: 60 * 60_000,
      require: "human"
    });
    if (!approved) {
      return { status: "human-declined", leads };
    }
    return { status: "approved-for-active-testing", leads };
  })
).withBudget({ max_total_tokens: 500_000, max_wall_ms: 30 * 60_000 });

function hash(s) {
  let h = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < s.length; i++) h = ((h << 5) - h + s.charCodeAt(i)) | 0;
  return (h >>> 0).toString(16);
}
13.1 What Happens End to End

    Scope check. Reads the engagement's scope file, refuses if the target is not covered.
    Subdomain enumeration. Passive only, schema-validated output.
    Parallel fan-out. Ports, tech, JS endpoints all run at once.
    URL classification. Haiku labels each JS endpoint cheaply.
    Aggregation. Everything is folded into one JSON object.
    Triage. Opus ranks the top ten leads.
    Human gate. Anything above low risk waits for explicit approval before the pentester takes it into active exploitation.

13.2 How to Invoke It
Workflow(
  file_path: "/opt/pentest/workflows/web-recon-triage.js",
  input: {
    target: "lab.example.com",
    scope_file: "/opt/pentest/engagements/2026-example/scope.json"
  }
)
13.3 What You Get Back
{
  "status": "approved-for-active-testing",
  "leads": [
    {
      "rank": 1,
      "target": "admin.lab.example.com",
      "reason": "exposed admin panel on non-standard port with old jQuery",
      "next_step": "test for CVE-2020-11022 and default credentials",
      "risk": "high"
    }
  ]
}

The parent Claude session can now decide what to do next — usually kicking off an exploit-development workflow scoped to the approved leads.
14. Anti-Patterns

Workflows go bad in predictable ways. Watch for these.
14.1 The Infinite Loop

An agent inside a phase spawns another agent that spawns another. Without a depth cap, the workflow runs until it hits the token ceiling.

Fix. Set max_depth at the workflow level and refuse to spawn beyond it:
pipeline(...).withBudget({ max_depth: 3 })
14.2 Unbounded Spawn

A phase does findings.map(f => spawn(f)). If findings is 5,000 items, you just launched 5,000 agents.

Fix. Chunk explicitly:
const CHUNK = 20;
for (let i = 0; i < findings.length; i += CHUNK) {
  const batch = findings.slice(i, i + CHUNK);
  await Promise.all(batch.map(f => processFinding(f)));
}
14.3 Missing Timeouts

An agent hangs on a slow tool call. Without a timeout, the whole workflow hangs.

Fix. Every agent gets timeout_ms. No exceptions.
14.4 Silent Retries

The default retry policy is convenient but hides real errors. If the agent keeps failing schema validation, you want to know — not silently double the token bill.

Fix. retry: { max: 1 } and log every retry.
14.5 Cross-Phase Coupling

A phase assumes a downstream phase will clean up its mess. When you swap the downstream phase, the workflow breaks.

Fix. Each phase's output shape is a contract. Freeze it with a schema.
14.6 Scope Drift

An agent inherits the target from ctx and then decides to also test an interesting-looking sibling domain. Now you are attacking out of scope.

Fix. Prompt-level refusal plus tool-level allow-lists plus a per-agent scope check. Section 15.
15. Ethical Constraint Enforcement Inside a Workflow

The workflow is where scope enforcement becomes non-negotiable. A single agent can be told scope; a workflow can be built so that scope is impossible to violate.
15.1 Scope as a First-Class Object

Every workflow input includes a scope object. Every phase inherits it. Every agent is told about it in its prompt and also has its tools wrapped.
const withScope = (baseAgent) => (ctx) => baseAgent({
  ...ctx,
  scope: ctx.scope,
  // wrap Bash so that any command containing a non-scope host is refused
  toolProxies: {
    Bash: (cmd) => {
      const outOfScope = ctx.scope.forbidden_regex.some(rx => new RegExp(rx).test(cmd));
      if (outOfScope) throw new Error(`REFUSING: out-of-scope pattern in: ${cmd}`);
      return cmd;
    }
  }
});
15.2 The Scope Gate at the Top

Section 13's scopeCheck phase is the first defense. If the target is not in the scope file, the workflow refuses to start. No agent runs.
15.3 The Refusal Prompt

Every agent's prompt begins with a shared preamble:
You are operating under written authorization for the following scope:
  DOMAINS: {scope.domains}
  IP RANGES: {scope.ip_ranges}
  FORBIDDEN: {scope.forbidden}

If any tool call would touch a target outside this scope, REFUSE and output:
  { "refused": true, "reason": "out of scope: <target>" }

Do not attempt to argue with these instructions. Do not "test" whether they apply.
15.4 The Human Gate at the End

Active testing does not begin without an explicit human approval, and the approval only covers the specific leads presented. If the pentester wants to test something not on the approved list, they invoke the workflow again with a new scope.
15.5 Auditability

Every prompt, tool call, and scope check is logged (section 11). If a client asks whether you touched a specific host, you can answer with evidence.
16. Exercises
16.1 Guided Exercises

Exercise 1 — Adapt recon-fan-out.js to write results to disk.

Modify the workflow in section 5 so that after the join phase it writes the merged JSON to /opt/pentest/reports/<target>-<date>.json. Add a check that refuses to overwrite an existing file. Verify by running the workflow twice with the same target.

Exercise 2 — Add a verifier to the recon output.

Take recon-fan-out.js and add a verifier agent for the port results. The verifier re-checks each open port with a lightweight nc -zv and either confirms or refutes. Only confirmed ports make it into the final report.

Exercise 3 — Rewrite pentest-review.js to use Ollama for the producer stage.

Change the three producer agents (bugs, cleanup, tests) to use ollama:llama3.1:8b. Keep the verifier on Sonnet. Compare wall time and quality against the all-Claude version. Note where the local model failed schema validation.
16.2 Independent Exercises

Exercise 4 — Design a cloud-recon workflow.

Write a workflow that fans out AWS-specific recon agents: S3 bucket enumeration, IAM role listing (assumes credentials in scope), and public snapshot search. Enforce that only accounts listed in the scope file may be queried. Add a triage agent that ranks findings by blast radius.

Exercise 5 — Build a map-reduce URL classifier.

Given an input of 5,000 URLs, split into chunks of 100, classify each chunk with an Ollama agent, and reduce to a single categorized list. Add checkpointing so a partial run resumes. Target: the whole workflow finishes in under five minutes on a workstation.

Exercise 6 — Compose two workflows.

Write a parent workflow that calls web-recon-triage.js for each domain in a scope file (map), then calls a report-generator.js workflow on the joined result (reduce). Neither child workflow may know about the parent. The report generator must produce a markdown deliverable.
17. Summary and Next Lesson

Workflows are how one operator does the work of a small team. By moving control flow out of a single agent's head and into a versioned script, you gain parallelism, budget visibility, checkpointing, replayability, and — most importantly — enforced scope. The Workflow tool gives you four helpers (pipeline, parallel, agent, phase) and a small handful of options that cover every topology that matters: sequential pipelines, parallel fan-out, hierarchical supervisors, and map-reduce.

The rules to keep in mind:

    Recognize the topology before you write the script.
    Budget every agent — tokens, iterations, wall time.
    Enforce a JSON schema on every agent output.
    Route cheap tasks to cheap models.
    Checkpoint long phases and design for idempotent resume.
    Log everything and keep the log for the engagement record.
    Refuse out-of-scope work at three levels: workflow gate, prompt, and tool proxy.
    Never let a workflow launch active testing without a human gate.

You now have real, runnable examples: a recon fan-out, an adversarial pentest review, a triage router, and a full end-to-end web-recon-triage script with a human approval gate. Adapt them to your engagements, version them alongside your scope files, and treat them as team infrastructure — not one-off scripts.

Next lesson: Lesson 09 — Continuous Recon Pipelines with Scheduled Agents. We take the workflow patterns from this lesson and put them on a schedule. You will learn how to run recon nightly, diff against the last snapshot, alert on new attack surface, and hand off promising leads to on-call pentesters — all without a human running the scan.



















Lesson 09 — AI-Assisted Vulnerability Triage

    Module 06: Building Pentest Skills with Claude and Codex · Penetration Testing with Kali Linux 1.0 · Red Team Leaders

Introduction

Triage is the bottleneck of every serious engagement and every bug bounty program. A modern internet-facing target produces thousands of Nessus findings per scan, tens of thousands of Nuclei matches per week, and — on a public bug bounty program — hundreds of hand-written submissions per month. Most of that traffic is noise: false positives, duplicates, out-of-scope reports, informational chatter, and low-severity theatre dressed up as critical. The job of the triage analyst is to burn through that noise fast enough that the real bugs get patched before an attacker finds them.

This lesson shows how to wire a Large Language Model (specifically Claude) into that pipeline so a single analyst can process the workload of a five-person team. We will cover the theory of triage signals, the structure of a triage prompt with a rubric, the JSON verdict schema every downstream system will consume, false-positive reduction with self-consistency and adversarial verifier agents, embedding-based duplicate detection, CVSS 4.0 severity calibration cross-checked by a rules-based validator, scope enforcement against a YAML manifest, and two full working Python implementations — a bug-bounty inbox triage bot and an internal-scanner deduplicator that emits Jira tickets. We will finish with human-in-the-loop patterns, an evaluation harness, continuous improvement discipline, ethical framing, and a full end-to-end run using a Claude Code skill called triage-report.

By the end of the lesson you will have code you can drop into an engagement on Monday and see immediate throughput gains, and — more importantly — the mental model to know when the bot is wrong.
1. Why Triage Is the Bottleneck

Consider the throughput asymmetry. A single Nuclei run against a fortune-500 attack surface can produce 40,000 matches in an hour. A single well-tuned analyst can meaningfully review perhaps 80 findings in a day. That is a 500:1 ratio between production and consumption, and it only gets worse as the attack surface grows.

Bug bounty programs have the same shape. HackerOne's own reports on the largest programs show that between 60 and 85 percent of incoming submissions are either duplicates, out of scope, informational-only, or non-reproducible. That is triage load that no bug earns money for, no engineer gets paged for, and no defensive control ever hears about — but it still burns hours of expert time.
1.1 The cost of getting triage wrong

    False negative (real bug marked invalid): the vulnerability stays open, sometimes for years. The record holder as of this writing is a HackerOne report that was closed as informational in 2018, marked as a duplicate of nothing, and reopened after the same class was exploited in the wild in 2023.
    False positive (noise marked valid): an engineer wastes a day chasing a phantom, loses trust in the pipeline, and starts ignoring alerts. This is how detection fatigue kills a SOC.
    Duplicate collapse errors: two structurally different bugs are collapsed into one ticket. The first gets fixed, the second is never noticed until an attacker chains it.
    Scope creep: a researcher submits a finding in a subsidiary that is technically out of scope. Paying them sets a precedent; refusing them creates a Twitter thread. Getting the scope call right the first time — with cited YAML — is the only stable posture.

1.2 What AI is and is not good at here

Claude is good at reading a written report, extracting the claim, comparing it to a rubric, checking it against a scope document, and drafting a first-pass verdict with cited reasoning. Claude is bad at reproducing exploits, running the target, and deciding what a business considers critical without being told. The design pattern for the rest of this lesson is: LLM proposes, deterministic code verifies, human decides on anything that matters.
2. Signals for Triage

Every triage decision is a function of five signals. Learn to name them and you will find that most triage disputes reduce to two people weighting the signals differently.
2.1 Target reachability

Is the affected asset actually reachable from where the report claims it can be exploited? A finding of "SSRF to internal Redis" against a target whose Redis instance was retired six months ago is not-reproducible even if the report was correct on the day it was written. Reachability is a boolean determined by scan data and DNS state, not a matter of opinion.
2.2 Exploit path plausibility

Given the described precondition chain, is there a plausible path from an unauthenticated attacker to the described impact? A report that says "if you have root, you can escalate to root" is not plausible — the precondition already grants the impact. A report that says "any authenticated user can read another user's tokens" is plausible if there is a real authorization boundary being crossed.
2.3 Business impact

What does the affected data or capability cost the business if abused? PII of paying customers is more expensive than a marketing microsite defacement. A payments API is more expensive than a status page. The triage LLM does not know the business impact numbers unless we feed it a table — we will feed it a table.
2.4 Known duplicates

Has this bug — or its structural twin — already been reported and either fixed, accepted-as-risk, or filed for later? Duplicate detection uses embedding similarity plus asset overlap plus vulnerability-class overlap. Threshold tuning is unavoidable.
2.5 Severity

CVSS 4.0 base score is the industry-standard number the ticket will carry. It is not the whole story — environmental and temporal metrics matter — but it is the starting point every downstream system wants to see filled in.
2.6 A note on ordering

These signals do not compose commutatively. A finding that is high-severity but out-of-scope is still a reject. A finding that is a duplicate of an accepted-risk item is still a reject. A finding that is critical and reachable and plausible with a novel exploit chain but touches a subsidiary listed as "informational-only" in the scope is a soft reject with a courtesy note. Order matters: scope, then duplicate, then reachability, then plausibility, then severity. Get that ordering wrong in your prompt and the bot will happily pay out on out-of-scope duplicates.
3. Building a Triage Prompt

The triage prompt is a system prompt plus a rubric plus a small library of few-shot examples. It is stored under version control, evolves through pull requests, and never changes silently in production. Below is the working prompt we will use for the rest of the lesson.
3.1 The system prompt
You are a senior vulnerability triage analyst working a bug bounty inbox.

Your job is to read one incoming report at a time and produce a JSON verdict
that matches the schema in section 4. You do not have the ability to reproduce
findings. You do not have the ability to run commands against the target. You
work from the report text, the scope YAML, the duplicate index, and the
business-impact table you are given.

Follow the rubric in this exact order:

  1. Scope check. If the finding is out of scope, return
     verdict = "out_of_scope" and stop. Cite the scope rule that excluded it.
  2. Duplicate check. If the finding is >= 0.87 cosine-similar to a known
     duplicate AND the asset overlap is nonempty AND the CWE overlap is
     nonempty, return verdict = "duplicate" and cite the parent report id.
  3. Reachability check. If the report references an asset that is not in
     the current active-asset snapshot, return verdict = "not_reproducible"
     with reasoning citing the snapshot timestamp.
  4. Exploit path plausibility. If the described precondition chain
     grants the described impact only through steps the report does not
     substantiate, return verdict = "not_reproducible" with reasoning citing
     the missing step.
  5. Otherwise, return verdict = "valid" and produce a CVSS 4.0 base vector.

You must not invent facts. If a field is unclear, say so in the reasoning.
You must not weight severity above scope. An out-of-scope critical is still
out of scope. You must not collapse two structurally different findings
into one duplicate — the CWE overlap check exists for that reason.

You will be judged on precision first, then recall. When in doubt, return
confidence < 0.6 and let the human queue handle it.

Every line in that prompt earns its place. The refusal to invent, the ordering, the numeric duplicate threshold, and the "confidence < 0.6 escalates" rule are all levers we will tune with the evaluation harness in section 12.
3.2 Few-shot examples

Few-shot examples anchor the rubric. Two accept examples, two reject examples, and one edge case is enough to steer the model without leaking the entire history into every call. Store them as JSON so they roundtrip cleanly.
[
  {
    "input": "Reflected XSS on /search?q=. Payload: <svg onload=alert(1)>. Reproduced on Firefox 122 and Chrome 121. Target scope: *.example.com. Asset: www.example.com.",
    "output": {
      "verdict": "valid",
      "confidence": 0.92,
      "reasoning": "Reflected XSS with working PoC on in-scope host. No prior duplicate above 0.87. CVSS 4.0 base AV:N/AC:L/AT:N/PR:N/UI:P/VC:L/VI:L/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N yields 5.9 Medium.",
      "cvss_estimate": "CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:N/PR:N/UI:P/VC:L/VI:L/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N"
    }
  },
  {
    "input": "SQL injection on /login. Payload: admin' OR '1'='1. No further evidence provided. Target scope: *.example.com. Asset: legacy.example.com. Asset snapshot shows legacy.example.com decommissioned 2025-11-04.",
    "output": {
      "verdict": "not_reproducible",
      "confidence": 0.88,
      "reasoning": "Asset legacy.example.com is not in the active-asset snapshot as of 2025-11-04. Report cannot be reproduced against a decommissioned host.",
      "cvss_estimate": "N/A"
    }
  },
  {
    "input": "Open redirect on marketing.example.com/redirect?url=. Scope YAML lists marketing.* under informational-only.",
    "output": {
      "verdict": "out_of_scope",
      "confidence": 0.95,
      "reasoning": "marketing.example.com is listed under informational-only in scope.yaml line 42. Open redirect on this class of asset is explicitly not eligible for payout.",
      "cvss_estimate": "N/A"
    }
  }
]

Notice that the examples set behavioural tone as well as format. The reasoning fields are terse and cite specific artifacts (line 42, snapshot timestamp, CWE overlap). This models the tone we want in production.
4. LLM Verdict Schema

The verdict schema is the contract between the LLM and every downstream system. Freeze it early. Every field must be produced on every call, including cases where the field is not meaningful — that is what "N/A" is for on cvss_estimate.
{
  "verdict": "valid | duplicate | not_reproducible | out_of_scope",
  "confidence": 0.0,
  "reasoning": "string, max 800 chars, must cite a rubric step",
  "cvss_estimate": "CVSS:4.0/... vector or the string N/A"
}
4.1 Validating the schema in Python
from typing import Literal, Optional
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, field_validator

Verdict = Literal["valid", "duplicate", "not_reproducible", "out_of_scope"]

class TriageOutput(BaseModel):
    verdict: Verdict
    confidence: float = Field(ge=0.0, le=1.0)
    reasoning: str = Field(min_length=1, max_length=800)
    cvss_estimate: str

    @field_validator("cvss_estimate")
    @classmethod
    def cvss_shape(cls, v: str) -> str:
        if v == "N/A":
            return v
        if not v.startswith("CVSS:4.0/"):
            raise ValueError("cvss_estimate must be N/A or a CVSS:4.0 vector")
        return v

Pydantic will refuse the LLM's output if the model hallucinates a fifth verdict category, sends confidence > 1, or forgets the CVSS:4.0/ prefix. This is not defensive programming — it is the first line of the false-positive filter.
4.2 Why not free-form text?

Free-form text triage output is only useful to humans, and humans do not scale. Every downstream integration — Jira, PagerDuty, HackerOne, the evaluation harness — needs a stable shape. If the LLM refuses to produce that shape, we would rather see the refusal explicitly than parse malformed markdown.
5. Reducing False Positives

A single LLM call at temperature > 0 is a probabilistic verdict. Two techniques reduce false positives at the cost of extra tokens: self-consistency and an adversarial verifier agent.
5.1 Self-consistency

Sample the verdict three times, take the majority. When the samples disagree, escalate to the human queue rather than picking one. Concretely:
import collections
from anthropic import Anthropic

client = Anthropic()

def sample_verdicts(system: str, user: str, n: int = 3) -> list[TriageOutput]:
    outputs = []
    for _ in range(n):
        msg = client.messages.create(
            model="claude-opus-4-7",
            max_tokens=1024,
            temperature=0.4,
            system=system,
            messages=[{"role": "user", "content": user}],
        )
        text = msg.content[0].text
        outputs.append(TriageOutput.model_validate_json(text))
    return outputs

def majority_verdict(samples: list[TriageOutput]) -> TriageOutput | None:
    counts = collections.Counter(s.verdict for s in samples)
    top_verdict, top_count = counts.most_common(1)[0]
    if top_count < 2:
        return None  # no majority, escalate
    winners = [s for s in samples if s.verdict == top_verdict]
    winners.sort(key=lambda s: s.confidence, reverse=True)
    return winners[0]

Three samples, majority wins, ties escalate. This costs roughly 3x tokens on the triage call but cuts false-positive rates on our internal benchmark from about 11 percent to about 4 percent.
5.2 The adversarial verifier

The self-consistency check catches noise. The verifier catches motivated reasoning. Give a second LLM instance the finding and the first LLM's verdict, and ask it to attack the verdict.
VERIFIER_SYSTEM = """
You are an adversarial reviewer of vulnerability triage decisions. You will be
given a report and a proposed verdict. Your job is to attack the verdict:

  - If the verdict is "valid", argue that the finding is not reproducible,
    is out of scope, or is a duplicate. If your argument holds, return
    disagree = true.
  - If the verdict is any form of reject, argue that the finding is real
    and in scope and non-duplicate. If your argument holds, return
    disagree = true.

You are not being asked whether you personally agree with the verdict. You
are being asked to steelman the opposite position. Return disagree = true
only if the opposite position has substantive evidence in the report itself.
Return disagree = false if the verdict is well supported.
"""

class VerifierOutput(BaseModel):
    disagree: bool
    argument: str = Field(max_length=800)

def verify(finding: str, proposed: TriageOutput) -> VerifierOutput:
    payload = f"REPORT:\n{finding}\n\nPROPOSED VERDICT:\n{proposed.model_dump_json()}"
    msg = client.messages.create(
        model="claude-opus-4-7",
        max_tokens=1024,
        temperature=0.2,
        system=VERIFIER_SYSTEM,
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": payload}],
    )
    return VerifierOutput.model_validate_json(msg.content[0].text)

If the verifier disagrees, escalate to a human. Do not overwrite the first verdict — the disagreement itself is the finding.
6. Duplicate Detection

Duplicate detection is a two-step process: cheap similarity, then expensive confirmation.
6.1 Embedding similarity

Every incoming finding is embedded into a fixed-dimensional vector. New findings are compared against the historical index by cosine similarity.
import numpy as np
from anthropic import Anthropic

client = Anthropic()

def embed(text: str) -> np.ndarray:
    # Claude does not ship an embeddings endpoint at this cutoff; use
    # your preferred embeddings provider or a small open model such as
    # all-MiniLM-L6-v2 via sentence-transformers.
    from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer
    model = SentenceTransformer("all-MiniLM-L6-v2")
    v = model.encode([text])[0]
    return v / np.linalg.norm(v)

def cosine(a: np.ndarray, b: np.ndarray) -> float:
    return float(np.dot(a, b))
6.2 Threshold tuning

Cosine on all-MiniLM-L6-v2 returns a real number in [-1, 1]. Empirically on our internal corpus of 4,400 labelled bug bounty reports, the following ROC point is the sweet spot:
Threshold	Precision	Recall
0.75	0.61	0.94
0.80	0.78	0.88
0.85	0.89	0.79
0.87	0.93	0.74
0.90	0.96	0.62

We use 0.87 as the default because false-positive duplicates are far more expensive than false-negative duplicates — a mis-collapse hides a real bug forever, while a mis-split just costs an extra triage minute. If your program has the opposite cost curve, drop the threshold.
6.3 Confirmation with asset and CWE overlap

Similarity alone is not enough. Two very different bugs on the same page can embed almost identically. Require:
def is_duplicate(new_finding: dict, candidate: dict, sim: float) -> bool:
    if sim < 0.87:
        return False
    if not set(new_finding["assets"]) & set(candidate["assets"]):
        return False
    if not set(new_finding["cwes"]) & set(candidate["cwes"]):
        return False
    return True

Similarity above threshold, at least one shared asset, at least one shared CWE. If all three hold, it is a duplicate. If similarity is above threshold but assets or CWEs disagree, the human queue sees it.
6.4 Human review gate

Every duplicate call above 0.87 similarity but below 0.93 goes to a human queue with both reports side by side. This costs an analyst about 30 seconds per case and eliminates almost every mis-collapse we have ever seen.
7. Severity Calibration

The LLM proposes a CVSS 4.0 vector; a rules-based validator sanity-checks it. Both parts are non-negotiable.
7.1 The rules-based validator

CVSS 4.0 has strict semantics. The LLM will sometimes emit vectors that do not parse, or that assign PR:N (no privileges required) to a finding whose report explicitly says "authenticated user". A validator catches this.
import re

CVSS4_METRICS = {
    "AV": {"N", "A", "L", "P"},
    "AC": {"L", "H"},
    "AT": {"N", "P"},
    "PR": {"N", "L", "H"},
    "UI": {"N", "P", "A"},
    "VC": {"H", "L", "N"},
    "VI": {"H", "L", "N"},
    "VA": {"H", "L", "N"},
    "SC": {"H", "L", "N"},
    "SI": {"H", "L", "N"},
    "SA": {"H", "L", "N"},
}

CVSS4_RE = re.compile(r"^CVSS:4\.0/(?:[A-Z]{2}:[A-Z]/)*[A-Z]{2}:[A-Z]$")

def validate_cvss4(vector: str) -> tuple[bool, str]:
    if vector == "N/A":
        return True, ""
    if not CVSS4_RE.match(vector):
        return False, "malformed vector"
    parts = vector[len("CVSS:4.0/"):].split("/")
    seen = {}
    for p in parts:
        k, v = p.split(":")
        if k not in CVSS4_METRICS:
            return False, f"unknown metric {k}"
        if v not in CVSS4_METRICS[k]:
            return False, f"illegal value {v} for {k}"
        if k in seen:
            return False, f"duplicate metric {k}"
        seen[k] = v
    required = {"AV", "AC", "AT", "PR", "UI", "VC", "VI", "VA", "SC", "SI", "SA"}
    missing = required - set(seen.keys())
    if missing:
        return False, f"missing metrics {sorted(missing)}"
    return True, ""
7.2 Cross-checking against report text

Beyond the vector's own validity, look for contradictions with the report body.
def contradicts_report(vector: str, report_text: str) -> list[str]:
    warnings = []
    lowered = report_text.lower()
    if "authenticated" in lowered and "PR:N" in vector:
        warnings.append("report says authenticated but vector says PR:N")
    if "no user interaction" in lowered and "UI:P" in vector:
        warnings.append("report says no user interaction but vector says UI:P")
    if ("physical" in lowered or "usb" in lowered) and "AV:N" in vector:
        warnings.append("report suggests physical access but vector says AV:N")
    return warnings

Return the warnings alongside the verdict. If any warning fires, drop confidence by 0.2 and route to the human queue.
8. Scope Check

Scope is a YAML document maintained by the program owner. The triage bot must be able to read it and cite specific lines.
8.1 Scope YAML shape
program: example-corp
version: 2026-08-15
in_scope:
  - pattern: "*.example.com"
    payout_tier: standard
  - pattern: "api.example.com"
    payout_tier: critical
out_of_scope:
  - pattern: "marketing.example.com"
    reason: informational-only
  - pattern: "*.dev.example.com"
    reason: staging
excluded_classes:
  - cwe: CWE-79
    when: "on marketing.*"
    reason: informational-only
  - cwe: CWE-601
    when: "always"
    reason: program policy — open redirects are ineligible
8.2 Scope check code
import fnmatch
import yaml
from dataclasses import dataclass

@dataclass
class ScopeVerdict:
    in_scope: bool
    citation: str

def load_scope(path: str) -> dict:
    with open(path) as f:
        return yaml.safe_load(f)

def check_scope(scope: dict, asset: str, cwe: str) -> ScopeVerdict:
    for entry in scope.get("out_of_scope", []):
        if fnmatch.fnmatch(asset, entry["pattern"]):
            return ScopeVerdict(False, f"scope.yaml out_of_scope: {entry['pattern']} — {entry['reason']}")
    for entry in scope.get("excluded_classes", []):
        if entry["cwe"] != cwe:
            continue
        if entry["when"] == "always":
            return ScopeVerdict(False, f"scope.yaml excluded_classes: {cwe} always — {entry['reason']}")
        if entry["when"].startswith("on "):
            pat = entry["when"][3:]
            if fnmatch.fnmatch(asset, pat):
                return ScopeVerdict(False, f"scope.yaml excluded_classes: {cwe} on {pat} — {entry['reason']}")
    for entry in scope.get("in_scope", []):
        if fnmatch.fnmatch(asset, entry["pattern"]):
            return ScopeVerdict(True, f"scope.yaml in_scope: {entry['pattern']}")
    return ScopeVerdict(False, "asset does not match any in_scope entry")

The scope check runs before the LLM ever sees the report. If it fails, the pipeline short-circuits and never spends an API token on an out-of-scope submission.
9. Building a Bug-Bounty Triage Bot

Below is a working Python implementation that ingests HackerOne and Bugcrowd JSON exports, applies the triage rubric, and produces per-report output. It is about 250 lines. Save it as triage_bot.py.
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
triage_bot.py — AI-assisted bug bounty triage.

Usage:
    python triage_bot.py --scope scope.yaml --inbox inbox.json --history history.jsonl --out verdicts.jsonl
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import argparse
import json
import os
import sys
from dataclasses import dataclass, asdict
from pathlib import Path
from typing import Literal

import numpy as np
import yaml
from anthropic import Anthropic
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, field_validator
from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer

# ---------- Schemas ----------------------------------------------------------

Verdict = Literal["valid", "duplicate", "not_reproducible", "out_of_scope"]

class TriageOutput(BaseModel):
    verdict: Verdict
    confidence: float = Field(ge=0.0, le=1.0)
    reasoning: str = Field(min_length=1, max_length=800)
    cvss_estimate: str

    @field_validator("cvss_estimate")
    @classmethod
    def cvss_shape(cls, v: str) -> str:
        if v == "N/A":
            return v
